- **[EpiSEM-Bench: Evaluating Safety and Evidence Recovery in LLM Agents under Missing Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31650)**  `arXiv:2610.31650`  
  _Qijin Zhang, Hanzhen Wang, Yuehan Zhao, Haoyu Dai, Yao Shu, Zhongxiang Dai, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-using language model agents rely on environmental facts to decide whether an action is permitted. When a tool response omits a safety-relevant fact, an agent may carry out a prohibited action even though the user request is benign and every returned statement is true. We introduce \bench{}, an executable benchmark for evaluating unsafe execution, safety boundaries, and evidence recovery in LLM agents under missing evidence. It contains 300 matched unsafe/safe task pairs across six domains. By systematically suppressing critical facts, the benchmark measures unsafe execution under critical evidence omission and determines the minimum observed failure cardinality, including failures with full evidence and cases with no observed failure after exhaustive testing. An independent audit tool tests whether agents seek missing facts and then withhold prohibited actions while completing permissible tasks. The benchmark reveals substantial variation across the ten models in omission robustness, evidence acquisition, and appropriate action. Resistance to missing evidence does not necessarily translate into useful recovery. Recovery access reduces unsafe execution for all ten models, but agents differ in whether they actively obtain the missing facts and use them to act appropriately in both worlds.
  </details>

- **[Simulated Control Evals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31593)**  `arXiv:2610.31593`  
  _Jeffrey Olmo, David Zhang, Tyler Tracy_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Control evaluations measure whether safeguards can prevent an AI model from causing undesired outcomes even when it is intentionally trying to do so. These evaluations typically use purpose-built control settings and extrapolate their results to a model's deployment. However, the effectiveness of safeguards in deployment can depend on features of environments and tasks that control settings do not represent. To broaden where control evaluations can be run, we introduce simulated control evaluations (SCEs), which use a language model to simulate tool responses in place of an environment. We evaluate SCEs on LinuxArena, where execution provides ground-truth outcomes. We compare SCE prediction errors with those from a separate control setting, BashArena, and with variation in control metrics within LinuxArena. For independently sampled evaluation and deployment task sets, SCEs predict safety with a root mean squared error of 3.6 percentage points across five monitors at a 5% audit budget, compared with 10.1 for BashArena. SCEs closely reproduce distributions of attack strategies, attacker reasoning, and monitor reasoning, with over tenfold lower KL divergence from execution than BashArena. SCEs explain 78-85% of the between-environment variance in attack success, monitor scores, and safety. This accuracy is sample efficient: SCEs predict safety with less error than BashArena using fewer than 5% as many task-pair draws as the deployment task set. Strategies discovered through simulation also improve monitor performance on held-out LinuxArena attacks, outperforming BashArena and approximately matching improvement from executed strategies. These results suggest that deployment-specific SCEs can be more predictive than control evaluations executed in separate control settings.
  </details>

- **[What Makes a Good Unmasking Policy? A Controlled Study of Learned Unmasking in Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31576)**  `arXiv:2610.31576`  
  _Jinya Sakurai, Xun Xu, Jaehong Yoon_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Masked diffusion language models generate tokens in parallel, but their efficiency depends on which predictions are committed. Learned unmasking policies improve the speed--accuracy trade-off, yet differ in supervision, inputs, and capacity, obscuring which choices matter. We view supervised policies as distilling a decoding procedure through a teacher and examine independently what the teacher labels as committable, on which states the policy imitates it, what the policy observes, and how much the number of function evaluations (NFE) must decrease to offset policy overhead. We find that labeling predictions matching the final output is overly permissive at low NFE, and hidden states are weak alone but can complement confidence. About a 10% NFE reduction suffices to offset policy overhead for policies up to 7.5M parameters across tested settings. We therefore train a policy on trajectory-contraction targets, which accept only commitments staying on a reference trajectory, using confidence and hidden states. On LLaDA-8B-Instruct, the resulting 856K-parameter policy achieves the best accuracy--NFE frontier and highest throughput on GSM8K, is competitive at moderate targets on MATH-500 and HumanEval, and largely transfers to Dream-7B-Instruct.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Causal Masks: Auditing and Localizing Hidden Future-Token Leakage in Adaptive Sequence Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31522)**  `arXiv:2610.31522`  
  _Huiting Liu, Jinsong Wu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hidden future-token leakage can create false architectural gains: an autoregressive model may appear substantially better under teacher-forced evaluation while its free-running generation deteriorates. We investigate this failure in an adaptive residual-routing decoder whose sequence-level routing summary exposes prefix positions to future tokens despite a correct causal attention mask. An anomalous 24-layer checkpoint reduces validation loss from the baseline's 3.5958 to 1.4259, yet it fails a prefix-invariance audit and has substantially higher reference-model perplexity during generation. Layer-wise diagnostics and implementation inspection trace the violation to a full-sequence summary broadcast through the dynamic routing-query path. Two independently trained 12-layer causal repairs eliminate detectable leakage and return validation loss and reference perplexity to the control scale, removing the large apparent advantage. As diagnostic tools, we use the established prefix-invariance contract, maximum prefix-output discrepancy (LeakScore), complementary behavioral metrics, and layer-wise profiles. Supporting experiments cover six synthetic implementation pairs spanning five module families, clean pretrained controls, controlled fault injection, and cache consistency across numerical precisions. These results illustrate how hidden future-token access can produce misleading claims of architectural progress and why lower teacher-forced loss should be interpreted together with whole-model causal checks and free-running evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Jev-DLC: Decision-Level Continual Learning from Typed Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31521)**  `arXiv:2610.31521`  
  _Yangyang Feng, Jianle Gan, Zhuoyan Feng, Chengwei Qin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Individual preferences often become visible only after deployment, when a user accepts one decision or revokes it in favor of another. Jev-style typed interfaces make this feedback explicit by identifying the selected action and, under revocation, the decision it replaces. We study how these consequences persist across requests as Decision-Level Continual Learning from Typed Feedback (Jev-DLC). Jev-DLC treats each correction as both evidence about a recurring action identity and a directed relation between the preferred and rejected choices. It preserves these signals in complementary bounded channels above a frozen scorer and combines their scores to shape the next decision. The method retains no raw interaction history, adds no scorer call, and performs no runtime language-model backward pass. We also introduce TCPA-Bench, a chronological suite that credits a correction only through its effect on later decisions. On TCPA-Repeat, Jev-DLC reaches 44.83% user-macro accuracy overall, 2.43 points above Recent-4 history replay. After at least two earlier feedback events, it reaches 56.07%, a 7.54-point margin over the strongest reported comparison at this depth. These results show how explicit typed corrections can become compact causal state for continual personalization.
  </details>

- **[From Prompt Rules to Golden Data Model: Production-Scale Generation of Personalized, Compliant Pharmaceutical Claims](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31519)**  `arXiv:2610.31519`  
  _Suyash Mishra, Qiang Li, Subhabrata Adak, Upender Phogat, Srikanth Patil, Satyanarayan Pati_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to generate domain-specific commercial & medical content (e.g., claims, statements, and promotional phrases) in regulated industries such as pharmaceuticals. However, ensuring its regulatory compliance, factual accuracy, and audience-specific personalization remains a major challenge. We report a production case study asking whether moving compliance and personalization signal from prompt-level rules into retrieval time structured context improves pharmaceutical claims generation, using Composer, a deployed internal tool built on a Golden Data Model (GDM) of 1,867 pharmaceutical claims spanning four products. The GDM integrates claim metadata, references, audience labels, material type, usage history, Match Text Variant (MTV) links, and a six dimension quality score (6C). Across six product-audience segments, the context pipeline improves intended indication (+29%), intended content type (+62%) and intended target audience (+67%), 6C quality score (+86%), and claim reuse (+34%). Production log analysis over the deployment period shows a 29.5% reduction in weekly API cost. We report this as descriptive deployment evidence. This work is a systems and data architecture contribution: it shows that in a regulated, high-stakes domain, restructuring claim data for retrieval-time filtering is a more scalable lever than expanding prompt-level rules, and documents the engineering path data cleaning, graph linking, and quality scoring.
  </details>

- **[DuoCook: An Overcooked-Inspired Benchmark for Visual Planning and Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31511)**  `arXiv:2610.31511`  
  _Ziyi Jiao, Heng Zhang, Drishti Regmi, Xiaokang Ye, Lianhui Qin, Peter Yichen Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coordinating two players in a cooking task requires a vision-language agent to track changing scene states, sequence recipe steps, and allocate shared resources under a time budget. We introduce an Overcooked-inspired benchmark for evaluating these capabilities in Unreal Engine environments constructed using coding agents. A single vision-language model controls both players from top-down visual observations, selecting high-level actions while the engine executes navigation. The benchmark contains 20 maps in five categories. These categories support evaluation of centralized coordination under equipment contention, changing terrain, and handoff dependencies. A prop legend and pot ingredient-count indicators provide explicit visual cues, while grid-based destinations support spatial grounding. The evaluation compares four VLMs on all 20 maps, measuring task score, order completion, delivery precision, action acceptance rate, and model API expenditure. A memory ablation on the same 20 maps examines the effect of a shared, self-authored notebook while retaining six-response action history in both conditions. The strongest evaluated model, \texttt{gpt-6-astra}, achieves 26.2\% mean order completion. Notebook memory has mixed score effects across models, with positive mean differences for Claude and Qwen and negative differences for GPT and Gemini.
  </details>

- **[DIAL: Steerable Language Models with Distilled Instruction-Adherence Levels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31438)**  `arXiv:2610.31438`  
  _Thanh Q. Tran, John Russell Himawan, Arun Verma, Kiwan Wong, Wei Xiao, Daniela Rus, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a user says "Keep it short", how short should the LLM's response be? More generally, users and applications require different levels of instruction adherence, but prompting does not reliably provide fine-grained control over them. We therefore study "steerable language models" that provide fine-grained numerical control over the degree to which models follow general instructions specified in text. However, existing approaches do not jointly offer fine-grained control, efficiency, and scalability across diverse instructions. To address this gap, we introduce DIAL (Distilled Instruction-Adherence Levels) to achieve all three properties. For fine-grained control, we perform classifier-free guidance (CFG) with a frozen LLM to provide training signals for on-policy distillation of instruction adherence at different levels. For efficiency, we design a lightweight steering adapter that uses a classifier over the LLM's hidden states to compute a closed-form steering edit. The classifier adaptively sets the steering magnitude at each token based on the target adherence level, while accounting for the geometry of the LLM's hidden states to preserve response coherence. For scalability, the classifier is text-conditioned, allowing a shared adapter to support new instructions without per-instruction fitting, and training requires only instruction and request text. Evaluation across multiple LLM families shows that DIAL achieves a competitive balance between concept expression and response fluency, with concept scores increasing monotonically across adherence levels. We introduce SteerIF, an instruction-only dataset of 98, 625 concept, persona, verifiable, and descriptive instructions. When trained on SteerIF, DIAL provides fine-grained control over personas and, when combined with prompting, improves verifiable instruction following.
  </details>

- **[RIDAR: Safeguarding Research Integrity in Autonomous Research Agents via Automated Detection and Runtime Intervention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31372)**  `arXiv:2610.31372`  
  _Jiening Siow, Amy Xin, Junjie Wang, Ziyu Tang, Zijun Yao, Yunjia Qi, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous research agents are increasingly used to automate large portions of scientific research, yet they are prone to research integrity violations (RIVs): behaviors that compromise the validity, reproducibility, or faithfulness of the research process. We present RIDAR, a framework for automated RIV detection and runtime intervention. First, we introduce RIVT, a comprehensive taxonomy of 14 RIV types spanning the full research lifecycle. Building on RIVT, we present RIDAR-post-hoc, a framework to detect RIVs within agent trajectories. RIDAR-post-hoc achieves 85.2% F1 performance on RIV-Gold, a benchmark for RIV detection with 60 expert-annotated trajectories. Applying RIDAR-post-hoc to RIV-Wild, a dataset of 300 agent trajectories across 50 distinct research tasks, we detect 3,964 RIV instances in 99.7% of trajectories, revealing the prevalence of RIVs in current agents. Finally, we present RIDAR-runtime, a framework for dynamically detecting and correcting RIVs during agent runtime. RIDAR-runtime effectively reduces agent RIVs remaining at task completion by 36.8%. We will release our code and data to facilitate future research.
  </details>

- **[ValuSpec: Plug-and-Play Candidate Valuation before Target Verification for Tree-Based Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31338)**  `arXiv:2610.31338`  
  _Liang He, SiYuan Ma, Qishi Zhan, Yongqi Fan, Mingyu Cao, Qizhen Lan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tree-based speculative decoding accelerates large language model inference by verifying multiple candidate branches in parallel. However, in practical serving scenarios, similar requests repeatedly induce overlapping candidate branches. While retrieval-based methods mitigate this by reusing historical drafts, the resulting candidate trees often contain many low-quality branches as the candidate tree grows, which reduces verification efficiency and wastes target-side computation. To address this, we propose ValuSpec, a plug-and-play candidate valuation mechanism that filters low-quality branches from a supplied candidate tree before expensive target-side verification. ValuSpec introduces transfer-based supervision to align filtering with the target model's behavior. A filtering threshold is estimated from the target model's greedy trajectories on a calibration split, without any parameter training. The target model then verifies the filtered tree under the output-identical greedy rule, so accepted tokens are guaranteed to match standard autoregressive decoding exactly. Experimental results on HumanEval, GSM8K, Dolly, and SpecBench show that, in our main evaluation, ValuSpec achieves up to 2.38x end-to-end speedup in retrieval-based tree settings. Furthermore, integrating ValuSpec into the EAGLE-3 dynamic tree framework yields up to 2.78x end-to-end speedup without modifying its tree construction strategy, demonstrating that ValuSpec serves as a generally applicable and plug-and-play candidate valuation module.
  </details>

- **[Subgoal-aware Memory Graph for GUI Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31307)**  `arXiv:2610.31307`  
  _Dezhao Luo, Daniel Beechey, Derek Yuen, Weilin Luo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GUI agents aim to automate complex user tasks by interacting with mobile interfaces, but ef- fective decision-making in long-horizon set- tings requires reusing prior experience rather than relying solely on the environment ob- servations. Existing memory-based methods typically store experience either at the single- action level or as entire trajectories. Single- step actions are often too fine-grained to carry meaningful semantics, since the success of an individual action does not necessarily imply progress towards the overall goal. In contrast, whole trajectories are often too coarse-grained and difficult to reuse across different tasks. We propose a subgoal-aware memory graph that distils past trajectories into structured, reusable evidence at the level of subgoals. We define a subgoal as consecutive actions shar- ing a single intent, the granularity at which progress toward the task becomes meaning- ful. To reduce redundancy and support cross- task reuse, we organise subgoal experiences as nodes in a graph, where related memories can be merged, extended, and retrieved based on the environment observation. On SPA-Bench, our method improves success rate by 11.11% over the baseline.
  </details>

- **[RIPPLE: Recovery Internalization through Process-Level Policy Learning and Effect-Aware Credit for Long-Horizon Agentic Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31276)**  `arXiv:2610.31276`  
  _Jingxuan Xu, Zihang Xu, Yuechen Luo, Ziqi Miao, Wenqiang Zhu, Weihao Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon coding agents can observe a failure and generate a plausible critique yet continue along the same incorrect trajectory. We call this the Critique-to-Action Handoff Gap. We introduce RIPPLE, a recovery-oriented training framework that internalizes process-level guidance into a single self-recovering policy. A prefix-only teacher provides evidence-grounded recovery supervision without access to future actions or terminal outcomes. After lightweight distillation, reinforcement learning assigns stage-specific credit to reflection triggering, diagnosis, and corrective action. Local validity signals gate trajectory advantages so that intermediate recovery decisions do not indiscriminately inherit terminal outcomes. Across four repository-level software-engineering benchmarks, RIPPLE improves the base policy by an arithmetic mean of 4.8 score points, with no auxiliary critic at deployment. Controlled ablations isolate the benefits of local, stage-aligned credit, and matched-prefix interventions show that critique semantics affect subsequent correction. Recovery gains concentrate where the prefix contains sufficient diagnostic evidence and the repository state remains reversible within the remaining budget.
  </details>

- **[Reducing Multi-Horizon Photovoltaic Forecast Error through Tree-Anchored Expert Combination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31248)**  `arXiv:2610.31248`  
  _Sun Ze, Zhou Liguo, Xu Yuqing, Yu Lei, Jiang Mingming, Alois Knoll_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A strong direct tree model may remain the best standalone photovoltaic forecaster, yet a weighted combination can reduce its residual errors. We examine whether complementary temporal predictors can lower the tree's mean absolute error (MAE) over 15--240 minutes without weaker ensemble members degrading performance. The candidate bank contains LightGBM and seven neural implementations with different temporal representations. A combiner fitted to MAE has nonnegative weights that sum to one. Unhelpful candidates can receive zero weight, while the tree anchors the combination and provides a development-stage fallback. The main design shares weights across horizons; horizon-specific weights and alternative losses test whether further complexity is needed. On 56,078 windows from two co-located public PVDAQ systems, the historical shared-MAE policy reduces scaled MAE by 3.21\% relative to the tree.The latest bounded neural correction does not reduce MAE further than this static combination. The results support selective combination of complementary predictions in this bank rather than equal averaging or indiscriminate addition of modules. Both analyses reuse a previously inspected evaluation period. They do not establish independent replication, weather-specific causation or universal gains from eight experts.
  </details>

- **[Reward-Weighted Preference Optimization for Selective Combinatorial Optimization Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31239)**  `arXiv:2610.31239`  
  _Haohui Zhang, Xinyu Hu, Wouter van Heeswijk, Neil Yorke-Smith, Martijn Mes_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning has emerged as an effective approach for combinatorial optimization, but most existing methods focus on problems such as the Travelling Salesperson Problem (TSP) in which all elements must be served. Selective combinatorial optimization problems (SCOPs), including the Orienteering Problem (OP), Prize-Collecting TSP (PCTSP), Stochastic PCTSP and Knapsack Problem (KP), additionally require selecting which elements to include. This selection creates sampled solutions with substantially different objective values, yet standard preference optimization reduces such comparisons to binary rankings and discards the magnitude of these differences.We introduce Reward-Weighted Preference Optimization (RWPO), a pairwise training objective that incorporates reward-gap information into preference learning. For candidate trajectories sampled from the same instance, RWPO weights each preference loss according to the normalized difference in their rewards, assigning greater training emphasis to comparisons that represent larger improvements in solution quality. We further combine RWPO with scale-specific fine-tuning and reward- and likelihood-weighted tilted sampling to adapt trained policies to larger and shifted instance distributions without relying on local-search post-processing.Experiments on the OP, PCTSP, Stochastic PCTSP, and KP show that RWPO consistently improves over unweighted preference optimization and policy-gradient baselines across a wide range of instance sizes. On the OP, the resulting solver exceeds the mean reward of the Compass metaheuristic at scales from 100 to 2,000 nodes. The results demonstrate that preserving reward-gap information can substantially improve preference-based learning for selective combinatorial optimization and support generalization to large-scale instances.
  </details>

- **[Trion-1: Unifying Language, Protein Sequence, and Structure with Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31215)**  `arXiv:2610.31215`  
  _Tianyu Lu, Kaicheng Yang, Jiangtao Feng, Keyue Qiu, Yawen Ouyang, Yixin Wu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We envision a general-purpose protein foundation model as a unified, interactive model of sequence, structure, and function, capable of understanding, generation, and cross-modal translation while retaining the instruction-following and reasoning capabilities of modern LLMs. Toward this vision, we introduce Trion-1, the first native, unified, multimodal diffusion language model (dLLM) for proteins. Trion-1 is built on two key design choices: (1) a unified discrete token space that jointly represents sequence, structure, and natural language; (2) a dLLM backbone that replaces strict left-to-right factorization with bidirectional or semi-bidirectional token interactions, enabling global residue communication and long-range contact modeling that are naturally suited to structure modeling. This formulation unifies six core capabilities: folding, inverse folding, sequence understanding, structure understanding, sequence design, and structure design, while retaining the native language capabilities of general-purpose LLMs. On established benchmarks, Trion-1 achieves broadly competitive performance. In folding, Trion-1 achieves a TM-score above 0.8 with a single sample under strict homology control, matching or exceeding the existing tri-modal model using 200 samples. For sequence understanding, Trion-1 performs on par with top-tier LLMs such as Claude Opus 4.7 (62.37%). These results support well-aligned sequence-structure-language representations and strong cross-modal generative capabilities.
  </details>

- **[Different Tasks, Different Timesteps: Temporal Task-Aware Quantization for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31198)**  `arXiv:2610.31198`  
  _Ofek Avivi, Eliron Rahimi, Avi Mendelson, Chaim Baskin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion Language Models (DLMs) introduce unique quantization challenges due to their iterative denoising process. In this work, we reveal that quantization sensitivity is both taskand time-dependent, with different downstream tasks exhibiting distinct precision requirements across denoising steps. We further show that these patterns can be predicted without groundtruth labels or task-specific evaluation metrics. Building on these findings, we introduce T 2Quant, a task and time-aware mixedprecision quantization framework that uses a label-free sensitivity signal to optimally allocate activation precision across denoising steps under a fixed bit-width budget. Extensive experiments across three DLMs and six benchmarks demonstrate that T 2Quant consistently improves the average accuracy of existing quantization methods, achieving gains of up to 6.7 percentage points under the same budget.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Fisher Vectors for Second-order Multi-scale Deep Feature Aggregation in Scalable Scene Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31146)**  `arXiv:2610.31146`  
  _Alexy Bhowmick, Sarat Saharia, Shyamanta M. Hazarika_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aggregating CNN responses into a compact discriminative vector, given their lack of geometric invariance, is a challenge. To address this challenge, we propose a codebook-free method to encode second-order information of multi-scale CNN features within the Fisher Kernel framework. We use a scalable retrieval pipeline to show the efficacy of the proposed vector. The increased discriminative power is confirmed by experimental evaluation of our method on two difficult benchmarks (INRIA Holidays, UKBench). The significant improvement in retrieval accuracy over state-of-the-art approaches comes with only a slight dimension increase.
  </details>

- **[Post-Audit Mirage: Identifiability Limits of Offline Agent Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31130)**  `arXiv:2610.31130`  
  _Aman Behera, Payal Payal_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Offline tests can fail to reveal whether an agent update will remain safe after deployment. We operationalize this post-audit mirage using matched safe and harmful lifecycles that expose identical verifier-visible evidence while an isolated scorer records opposite deployment truth. Our theorem applies standard observational-equivalence and indistinguishability ideas to this executable audit boundary and states the resulting error-abstention tradeoff. The Post-Audit Mirage benchmark makes that boundary executable across authorization, scheduling, and shared-capacity environments. IdentifiedRangeMonitor uses fresh group-labelled harm outcomes to return deploy, hold, cannot determine, or unsupported. Experiments show that monitoring can recover the missing distinction in favorable settings but may remain undecided for rare groups, small effects, or missing observations. Verifier-agent evaluation must therefore test both calculation validity and whether the available evidence supports the deployment decision.
  </details>

- **[SetCommit: Coordinated Token Commitments for Few-Step Diffusion Language Model Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31120)**  `arXiv:2610.31120`  
  _Yuannuo Feng, Yubing Ye, Wenshuai Yao, Yizhe Chen, Ngai Wong, Wenyong Zhou, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models generate multiple token positions in parallel, but few-step decoding makes parallel commitment difficult: tokens that appear reliable individually may be jointly inconsistent, while premature commitments can prevent later context-dependent refinement. In this paper, we introduce SetCommit, a training-free method for coordinated parallel token commitment. Instead of selecting tokens independently by confidence, SetCommit selects a commitment set that balances token reliability against local joint commitment risk, preserving conditioning opportunities for subsequent denoising steps. It further performs budget-neutral local exchanges, replacing vulnerable earlier commitments when newly revealed context supports more reliable alternatives. SetCommit requires no retraining or architectural modification and is compatible with existing diffusion decoding strategies. Experiments with LLaDA-8B and Dream-7B on GSM8K, MATH-500, MBPP, and HumanEval show accuracy/pass@1 gains of up to 7.4 percentage points over native decoding at 32 model evaluations. At common quality targets, geometric-mean incremental speedups range from $1.44$ to $1.74\times$. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Constraint Reasoning and Large Language Models Join Forces: Providing Contrastive Explanations for Real-World Optimisation-Based Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31119)**  `arXiv:2610.31119`  
  _Roger X. Lera-Leri, Filippo Bistaffa, Athina Georgara, Juan A. Rodríguez-Aguilar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We focus on optimisation-based Decision Support Systems in real-world applications. In this context, we aim to provide contrastive explanations that answer users' questions about recommendations based on the solution of an optimisation problem, specifically a Mixed-Integer Program (MIP). Addressing this endeavour is crucial to ensure human oversight of the decisions implemented, as required by international guidelines on trustworthy and responsible automated decision-making systems. We propose a novel methodology that combines constraint reasoning techniques and Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate contrastive explanations for optimisation problems encoded as MIPs. Although LLMs have shown impressive capabilities in Natural Language processing, they can produce incorrect answers due to reasoning limitations. To overcome these limitations, we leverage constraint reasoning techniques to distil the information necessary to produce an explanation, which is then passed as input to an LLM as the so-called graph of reasons. Such a graph captures the semantics and the relationships among constraints of the optimisation problem. Results on real-world scheduling domains show that incorporating constraint reasoning improves explanations generated by the LLM. In particular, using the graph of reasons enables the LLM to produce natural-language explanations that include more relevant reasons while reducing errors compared to explanations generated without this structure.
  </details>

- **[Can AI Co-Design Structures? An AI Agent for Early-Stage Structural Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31113)**  `arXiv:2610.31113`  
  _Ricardo Maia Avelino, Mennatallah El-Assady, Tom Van Mele, Philippe Block_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in large language models and agentic workflows offer a new paradigm to early-stage structural design translating design intent into structural models. Although text-to-3D approaches have demonstrated that natural language can be used to produce visual, mesh-based geometric models, structural design requires domain-specific geometric arrangements and semantically rich data types. This paper introduces a proof-of-concept workflow for prompt-to-structure generation using an AI agent connected to a COMPAS-based structural modelling API through a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server. Through controlled modelling operations, the agent creates and modifies a graph-based structural data model composed of explicit building elements. The resulting artifacts can be inspected, modified, serialised, and further connected to downstream AEC workflows. The approach is demonstrated through selected prompt-to-structure examples and by progressive and editable design sequences that resemble typical iterative structural design workflows. The results are preliminary, but demonstrate the possibility of combining natural language, agentic workflows, and domain-specific structural data structures as a first step toward AI-assisted structural co-design.
  </details>

- **[Who Is the Influencer? Consensus Dynamics in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31105)**  `arXiv:2610.31105`  
  _Gouki Minegishi, Jingyuan Feng, Hiroki Furuta, Takeshi Kojima, Yusuke Iwasawa, Yutaka Matsuo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent LLM systems are increasingly used in practice, but their behavior is complex and hard to understand. We design a simple task to ask which model has the most influence on the final consensus when several models are mixed in one system. Experimentally, across seven model families, we find that the opinions of the other models are absorbed into the opinion of the stronger model, the one with more parameters. This happens even though the task requires no pretraining knowledge and has no correct answer. To understand this phenomenon, we interpret the agents as weighted particles moving on a simplex. Larger models carry a larger weight and therefore occupy a deeper well in the energy landscape. A large weight means strong in-context learning: a model with a large weight amplifies the majority of its context. Larger models are generally better at in-context learning, and this is what makes the larger model the influencer. We hope that this work helps to interpret multi-agent systems, whose behavior is becoming too complex for humans to understand.
  </details>

- **[DeepOpen: Decision-Oriented Intent Classification on BANKING77 and CLINC150](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31104)**  `arXiv:2610.31104`  
  _Shuang Song Zhongqiao Yuan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present DeepOpen, a decision-Oriented intent-classification framework with dataset- specific systems for BANKING77 and CLINC150. On BANKING77, a single English classi- fier continued with sharpness-aware minimization (SAM) achieves 94.1234% test accuracy (2,899/3,080). This is the best observed result among three fixed SAM seeds, selected for display after test evaluation; their mean is 94.0368%. On the 150 in-scope CLINC150 classes, probability fusion of one Laya specialist and three DeBERTa-v3-large classifiers achieves 98.0222% (4,411/4,500). We describe the two configurations, reproduce the reference com- parisons displayed in the accompanying evaluation explorer, and separate headline scores from evidence about improvement. Under a uniform BANKING77 evaluation path, the SAM mean exceeds the historical checkpoint by 0.0433 percentage points, with a shared-row 95% interval of [-0.2273, +0.3247]. Thus, the continuation does not establish a reliable accuracy gain. The report documents strong observed task-specific performance, selection effects, and unresolved historical execution-path differences, rather than a new backbone or a unified algorithmic advantage.
  </details>

- **[Solving Flood-It Puzzles: A Comparison of Tailored A* Search with Declarative Solver Approaches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31100)**  `arXiv:2610.31100`  
  _Philipp von Manteuffel, Benjamin Blankertz_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present an optimized approach for finding minimal move sequences to solve Flood-It puzzles using A* search and compare it with approaches using SAT, Integer Programming, and Constraint Programming solvers. The performance of A* search is considerably improved by using an appropriate heuristic and sophisticated equivalences of game states to limit the search space. Each aspect is systematically evaluated, providing insights that generalize to other problems. By comparing the best variant of each approach, the A* search is the fastest in finding a minimal solution in about 75 % of the Flood-It instances tested. This result is in line with our expectation since A* specifically looks for a minimum solution. In contrast, the SAT-based approach is designed to solve the decision problem of whether a solution with a given length exists. An iterative search is required to determine a minimum solution length using a SAT-based approach. However, with increasing complexity, quantified by the minimum solution length, the SAT-based approach outperforms A*. We also compared the different approaches to the decision problem, where A* again performed best for the largest number of puzzles. Here, again, SAT supersedes A* for higher complexities, albeit with a lower crossing point.
  </details>

- **[Co-Recursive Semantic Self-Extension: A Mathematical Foundation for Open-Ended Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31097)**  `arXiv:2610.31097`  
  _Simon Hegelich, Bardh Prenkaj, Habiba Sarhan, Morteza Shahrezaye, Kolja Hegelich, Eric Cambria, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Artificial intelligence has advanced through scale, data, and computation, yet remains bounded by its representational framework. We ask what makes intelligence open-ended. Every inferential system selects meaningful distinctions, limiting what it can learn. Within a fixed purpose, performance reaches a ceiling set by its semantic domain. Teleological succession, in which purposes arise from earlier ones, faces a similar limit: Boolean-closed purpose-transformations exhaust their reachable semantic territory. We shift from purposes to inferential systems. Drawing on Kant and Gödel, we model cognitive states as partial evaluations over a formal language. Unresolved propositions may be undecided or undecidable. Reasoning can resolve the former but not the latter, leaving a frontier for extension. Non-trivial reductions of undecidable propositions expose undecidable intermediate premises whose reflective reification supports multiple decisive extensions. Finite trace-guided generation remains fallible, requiring meta-level selection and polycontextural exploration. Using Kolmogorov complexity, we show that no architecture with a fixed state description, transition mechanism, and initial condition can generate histories incorporating irreducible algorithmic information. Because physical systems are fixed in this sense, we examine collectives. Coupling finitely many finite agents does not remove the bound. Extension requires persistent external memory preserving semantic content and historical coordinates. Collective generative capacity depends on balancing overlap for transfer with difference for generation. We propose semantic self-extension as a design principle: intelligence should not merely resolve ambiguity within existing meanings, but transform unresolved structures into new spaces of meaning, generating new distinctions, questions, representations, and purposes.
  </details>

- **[Dual Exam: Controlled Evaluation of Multimodal Agents and World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31066)**  `arXiv:2610.31066`  
  _Zhefan Rao, Meng Chu, Xuan Zhang, Jingfan Chen, Guohao Cai, Runyi Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World Models (WMs) can support Multimodal Agents (MAs) through simulated experience, while Agent trajectories can inform World Model refinement. Evaluating this interaction requires separate evidence about action selection and state evolution. We introduce Dual Exam, which tests Agents under fixed environment response mechanisms and World Models under fixed action interventions across physical, digital, social, and scientific domains. The Agent track compares 10 configurations on 1,088 items in 15 reporting categories. The World Model track contains 400 cases across eight scenarios; 15 configurations have full coverage and one covers six scenarios. GPT 6 Astra leads the case-weighted Agent Index at 31.3, and GPT-Image-2 leads the full-coverage WM Overall at 21.7. These scores use different tasks and metrics and are not directly comparable. A 150-case pilot of one coupled pairing records zero task successes and motivates component-level diagnosis. Results reveal uneven task performance and failures to preserve task-relevant state. Their value for predicting coupled success or sustained reciprocal learning remains to be established.
  </details>

- **[Diagnosing the Explicit-to-Applied Theory of Mind Gap in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31060)**  `arXiv:2610.31060`  
  _Prajvi Saxena, Sabine Janzen, Wolfgang Maass_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models are deployed in high-stakes roles such as therapy, education, and personal assistance, their social cognition must be assessed reliably. Current Theory of Mind (ToM) benchmarks report aggregate scores that conflate two distinct competencies: explicit ToM (recognizing what someone believes) and applied ToM (using that belief to predict behavior). Building on this distinction, we investigate the gap and its recoverability using four structurally diverse benchmarks. Aggregate reporting obscures uneven competence: on SimpleToM, Llama-3.1-8B scores 51.5 percent overall while recognizing mental states at 99.4 percent and predicting behavior at only 28.9 percent. To assess recovery, we compare structured prompting, matched in-context learning, and our proposed Belief-State Test-Time Training (BSTTT), which adapts lightweight parameters on answered support questions through action reconstruction. Across Qwen2.5-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, and GPT-oss-20B, recovery depends on the model, task, and intervention. On SimpleToM, reminders combined with BSTTT improve Llama's behavior prediction by 40.8 percentage points; reminders account for most of the overall improvement, with BSTTT adding 1.3 to 5.5 points beyond reminders across models. Contextual examples often outperform weight updates, while BSTTT remains stronger in specific model-benchmark settings. Temporal belief tracking remains difficult despite partial recovery. These findings show that aggregate ToM scores can overstate applied competence while hiding performance recoverable through targeted assistance. We recommend decomposed reporting of explicit and applied accuracy alongside intervention comparisons that establish which failures are recoverable and under what conditions.
  </details>

- **[Directing to Diversify: Multi-Directional Search for Automatic Heuristic Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31048)**  `arXiv:2610.31048`  
  _Jianghan Zhu, Jianan Zhou, Fei Liu, Ni Zhang, Yaoxin Wu, Zhuoyi Lin, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong potential to automate heuristic design for NP-hard combinatorial optimization (CO) tasks without human intervention. Recent methods predominantly rely on evolutionary computation (EC) frameworks, where heuristic diversification is mainly induced through EC-like operators or downstream selection, while the direction of heuristic transformation remains largely unharnessed. In this paper, we propose a multi-directional Perturbation-Rewrite Search (PRS) framework for automatic heuristic design that factorizes heuristic transformation through directional search primitives at the generation level. Specifically, PRS operates on heuristic ideas through three complementary directions: expansion toward richer and more expressive designs, compression toward simpler and less specialized designs, and steering for fine-grained refinement, together enabling broader and more sustained exploration of diverse regions in the heuristic design space. PRS further organizes candidate exploration within a breadth-first search-based process with adaptive operator control, which ensures thorough candidate utilization and sustained exploration, and maintains strong search dynamics while preventing premature convergence. Extensive empirical results across diverse CO tasks show that PRS consistently discovers high-quality heuristics, demonstrating the effectiveness and generality of explicitly directional exploration design.
  </details>

- **[Conflict-Aware Logit Adapters for Utility-Preserving Anti-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31046)**  `arXiv:2610.31046`  
  _Quoc Phong Dao, Binh Anh Nguyen, Linh Ngo Van, Thanh-Toan Do, Mehrtash Harandi, Trung Le_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While reasoning traces improve model capability, they also provide supervision signals for downstream distillation. Initial efforts to mitigate this issue rely on decoding-time interventions, but they incur high inference overhead and can harm the teacher model's natural fluency. We propose Conflict-Aware Logit Adapters (CALA), a lightweight training-time defense. CALA attaches a small residual adapter to a frozen teacher model and trains only the adapter. A proxy student identifies positions where imitation is most sensitive, and the adapter perturbs the output distribution at these positions while remaining within high-probability regions. A conflict-aware optimization strategy balances anti-distillation and utility objectives, while regularization preserves the teacher's performance and fluency. At inference time, CALA incurs modest overhead and requires no auxiliary model. Experiments on reasoning benchmarks show that CALA substantially reduces successful student imitation while maintaining near-original teacher performance and generation quality. These results establish CALA as a practical and efficient alternative to decoding-time approaches.
  </details>

- **[Algorithmic drift in generative adaptive learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31041)**  `arXiv:2610.31041`  
  _Firuz Kamalov, Hana Sulieman, Ahmed El Sayed, Sivaraj R., Nitesh Sughnani_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative artificial intelligence promises to democratize personalized tutoring, yet the longitudinal stability of adaptive systems remains under-theorized. Current ethical discourse focuses on static bias. This paper argues that a distinct threat lies in algorithmic drift: a feedback loop in which minor, demographically asymmetric observation errors compound over time into systemic inequities. We make three contributions. First, we formalize algorithmic drift as a coupled learner--tutor dynamical system. Second, we corroborate this theory with an agent-based Monte Carlo simulation in which either a transparent online tracker or an LSTM deep-knowledge-tracing network drives an IRT-based curriculum policy. Third, we introduce the temporal fairness stabilizer (TFS), a targeted intervention that periodically re-probes learner capability, and benchmark it against standard exploration baselines with explicit group-fairness metrics and replication-level inference. Within the modeled environment, TFS closes the achievement gap where generic exploration does not, and we characterize the conditions under which it succeeds. We separate what is established analytically, what is demonstrated in simulation, and what remains to be validated with real learners. Our results reframe algorithmic fairness in education as a temporal property of a closed loop rather than a deployment-time checkbox.
  </details>

- **[CANNBot-CAKE: Collaborative Agentic Kernel Evolution for Ascend NPU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31034)**  `arXiv:2610.31034`  
  _Deng Pan, Xue-Jian Gao, Junyi Fan, Bin Du, Jiasheng Li, Xinxian Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Efficient custom operators are essential for modern deep learning workloads, yet developing high-performance kernels for Ascend NPUs remains slow, expert-intensive, and difficult to automate. Although AI agents now approach expert performance on GPU kernel benchmarks, current models remain less capable of generating AscendC kernels for NPUs, potentially reflecting the relative scarcity of relevant training data. We thus present CAKE, a CANN-native system for deployable custom operators that combines adaptive multi-DSL routing, separate Generation and Evolution modes, and reviewed knowledge reuse through CAKE Book and a Self-Evolving Agent. On 53 CANN-Bench v1.1 operators on Ascend 950PR, CAKE's aggregated results achieve $100\%$ case pass rate on the standard set (1,060/1,060) and $98.28\%$ on the hidden set (4,167/4,240), with an overall mean score of 78.63 out of 100 and an average performance speedup of $1.334\times$ over the baseline. Evolution then pushes correct operators further: EngramGateFusion cuts aggregate device time by $46.6\%$ over 20 rounds and roughly halves the mean roofline gap. These results offer a practical path toward closing Ascend's AI-for-kernel capability gap.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Artificial Wisdom (AW): A Trajectory-Based Framework for Context-Sensitive Decision Regulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31012)**  `arXiv:2610.31012`  
  _Md. Jalil Piran_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Capability benchmarks measure what large language models can do, but not whether their recommendations remain appropriate as decision conditions change. This paper proposes a trajectory-based measurement framework and prospective validation protocol for artificial wisdom, defined behaviorally as context-sensitive regulation of judgment. Versioned reference specifications determine admissible responses and transition materiality before evaluation. Seven stage-level criteria assess judgment under current conditions, while two transition components assess material-update adequacy and commitment-preserving stability. Stability requires prior adequacy; switching within registered commitment classes is permitted, and persistent error earns no stability credit. A nine-component profile is primary, with a secondary bottleneck score and separate critical-failure reporting. Three simplified simulations examine precision, bottleneck estimation, and sensitivity to model-family count. A single-criterion simulation met its precision criteria at 24 scenarios per domain. Near a bottleneck tie, the estimated minimum had a downward bias of 0.55 points at that size. A simplified incremental-prediction simulation detected a Brier improvement of 0.027 with probabilities of .508 and .742 using eight and twelve families, respectively; it does not establish power for the operational protocol. An exploratory predecessor-rubric study comprising 71 evaluation records illustrates aggregation sensitivity but does not validate the proposed instrument. The twelve-family target remains provisional pending feasibility assessment and calibration of the complete analysis. Whether the profile predicts independent decision outcomes beyond capability remains an open empirical question.
  </details>

- **[Mitigating Saliency Collapse: Robust Saliency-Aware Long-Text Image-Text Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31005)**  `arXiv:2610.31005`  
  _Qiuyu Kong, Zanxi Ruan, Marco Cristani, Yiming Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-text image-text alignment is essential for fine-grained scene understanding, yet existing methods mainly focus on extending context length or improving local alignment. We identify an overlooked failure mode, saliency collapse, where models over-rely on text that features visual prominence while underutilizing non-salient context. Consequently, retrieval performance degrades sharply when salient content is reduced, despite that the non-salient context remains informative. To address this, we propose RoSA, a saliency-aware finetuning framework that decomposes image-text pairs into complementary salient and contextual views, enabling joint learning of prominent and contextual semantics. We propose a modality-independent decomposition strategy to combine object-level visual saliency estimation with LLM-based text decomposition to bridge vision and language. We employ auxiliary objectives to explicitly align these views, ensuring robust representation with both salient and contextual features. RoSA greatly improves long-text retrieval and demonstrates strong robustness to saliency collapse across benchmarks. Moreover, our auxiliary objectives are plug-and-play, bringing consistent gains to existing tuning methods. Code and checkpoints are available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Zero-Shot Forecasting of In-Situ Soil Moisture and Temperature with a Time-Series Foundation Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30971)**  `arXiv:2610.30971`  
  _Mohammad Talaei, Mohammad Goudarzi, Maille Todd, Vanessa N.L. Wong, Iadine Chadès_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Soil moisture and temperature govern plant-water availability and microbial activity, making them key indicators for evaluating land-restoration efforts on degraded landscapes. However, the physical and data-driven models normally used to forecast them both need information that a newly instrumented restoration site does not yet have: hydraulic parameters for the former and months of per-site training data for the latter. We evaluate TimesFM 1, a 500M-parameter time-series foundation model, zero-shot on 15-minute telemetry from 27 sensor stations across three land-management regimes (Control, Treatment, Scald) at a semi-arid restoration site, forecasting 5 cm and 30 cm soil moisture and enclosure temperature at seven lead times from 1 h to 168 h. Using gap-aware rolling-origin evaluation across sensors, we find that TimesFM beats the best rule-based statistical forecasting baselines (persistence, seasonal-naive, or 7-day climatology) by 21-76% at sub-daily lead times but loses by 6-79% beyond a day, a pattern explained by a diurnal-alignment mechanism that holds for both soil-moisture depths and temperature. A CatBoost baseline trained walk-forward on the same origins with full history and weather covariates never wins overall, and we show that its weakness is partly a cold-start effect specific to short deployments. We additionally quantify the inference cost of zero-shot forecasting on commodity CPU hardware. Our results highlight a practical trade-off between zero-shot forecasting capability, predictive performance, and computational efficiency.
  </details>

- **[Measuring the Reasoning-Action Coupling in Driving Vision-Language-Action Models through Counterfactuals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30953)**  `arXiv:2610.30953`  
  _Gabriel Manalu, Saad Wazir, Thao Do, Seongah Kim, Hyeontaek Hwang, Tolcha Yalew Kidane, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Driving vision-language-action (VLA) models bring an appealing form of explainability to a safety-critical domain, since a system that can state the reasons for its maneuvers offers the transparency that deployment and public trust in autonomous driving demand. These models read the world into language before they act on it, describing the scene and reasoning over it in text that ranges from chain-of-thought to multi-stage question answering on the way to a trajectory. That framing quietly assumes two separable properties, namely that the trajectory follows the generated reasoning and that the generated reasoning tracks the causes that actually produce the trajectory. To thoroughly investigate these properties, we apply causal, counterfactual interventions to four driving VLAs across different benchmarks. First, we perturb the models' own reasoning and show that altering its meaning produces small trajectory changes relative to blanking the same models' camera inputs, a pattern that persists across benchmarks and model architectures. We then construct scene-level counterfactuals through photorealistic removal of key objects named in the models' reasoning and analyze whether the generated reasoning and trajectories track these changes. Overall, our findings indicate that the reasoning these models present to account for their resulting driving behavior is weakly coupled to that behavior itself, pointing to limits on the explainability these systems are intended to provide.
  </details>

- **[The Geometry of Meaning in Neural Manifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30938)**  `arXiv:2610.30938`  
  _Valentino Maiorca_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Just as different languages express the same concept through different words, neural networks express similar functions through incompatible coordinate systems. Different architectures, random initializations, and optimization trajectories produce incompatible parameterizations in extrinsic coordinates, obscuring functional relationships between independently trained models. When models independently solve similar tasks on related data, do their learned representations share exploitable structure despite incompatible coordinates? This thesis shows that semantic correspondence is a fundamental primitive revealing shared geometric structure beneath incompatible parameterizations. By identifying which elements refer to the same semantic content, correspondence breaks the symmetry of arbitrary implementation choices, anchoring incompatible systems to a shared semantic frame and revealing the intrinsic geometry that enables zero-shot transformation between independently trained models.Building on this principle, fourteen contributions explore correspondence-based alignment across zero-shot model stitching, cross-modal translation, policy transfer, activation steering, and neural decoding. They span domains (vision, language, reinforcement learning, neuroscience), architectures (CNNs, Transformers, graph networks), and scales (from small supervised models to billion-parameter generative systems). Each exploits different geometric properties (angular, metric, spectral, sparse, or distributional), yet all instantiate the same principle. It extends beyond artificial systems: fMRI data show that different human brains processing identical stimuli exhibit alignable geometric structure.By revealing intrinsic geometric structure beneath extrinsic parameterization, semantic correspondence transforms model isolation into interoperability.
  </details>

- **[DConstrain: Compiling Context-Parameterized Transitions for Diffusion Language Model Constrained Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30920)**  `arXiv:2610.30920`  
  _Shidong Cao, Jing Ma, Wenhao Chai, Yuchen Tian, Lun Du, Da Zheng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models (DLMs) decode multiple tokens in parallel, so individually valid tokens can be incompatible together under a schema. Exact joint inference over a grammar graph enforces compatibility, but it requires token-labeled successors across many hypothetical parser configurations. Encoding stacks and field histories in compiled states can cause exponential growth, which forces prior exact decoders to fix property orders and exclude valid outputs. We present DConstrain, which compiles schemas into context-parameterized transition programs. Cold compilation specializes token consumption to local grammar states while retaining context-dependent stack operations, environment guards, and caller-dependent deferred parsing symbolically. Tracking field histories as runtime values supports arbitrary property order without enumerating field subsets offline. At runtime, warm compilation binds reached configurations to these programs to construct exact successors, overlapping with the model forward pass because graph construction requires no model scores. Furthermore, token-effect grouping merges tokens with identical compiled effects before runtime contexts are bound, so groups are reused across stacks and environments even when concrete successors differ. Objective-specific score aggregation and token restoration provably preserve both the Viterbi optimum and the token-labeled path distribution of ungrouped inference. Across three benchmarks and two DLM families, DConstrain compiles 100% of BFCL schemas under arbitrary property orders, whereas an optimized finite-state baseline compiles only 71.30-85.81%. Under arbitrary property order, it reduces BFCL end-to-end decoding latency by 6.65-46.26 times with 100% schema validity and raises DiffusionGemma's BFCL accuracy from 37.27% under fixed order to 81.56%.
  </details>

- **[Harnessing Language Models as Optimization Modeling Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30917)**  `arXiv:2610.30917`  
  _Yunxiang Luo, Xin Xu, Tianhao Chen, Zhixi Xiong, Peiqi Jiang, Fan Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can translate natural-language optimization problems into solver programs, but reliably producing correct formulations and implementations remains challenging. Existing approaches improve optimization modeling through agentic workflows or task-specific post-training. This raises a practical question: can a lightweight harness using a single model role achieve competitive performance without additional training? We introduce OPTICYCLE and OPTISTATE, two lightweight inference harnesses for optimization modeling, and evaluate them on nine benchmarks comprising 2,634 problems using 11 Qwen variants spanning 0.6B-27B parameters. Our results show that execution-guided revision consistently improves macro accuracy over direct generation for all evaluated models, with gains of up to 49.46 percentage points. Despite their simplicity, both harnesses outperform six adapted agentic workflows in same-model comparisons and four evaluated post-trained optimization models at comparable model sizes, without additional training. Explicit formulation state provides further but model-dependent improvements, suggesting that additional structure is not universally beneficial.
  </details>

- **[TwinStore: A Synthetic Consumer Platform Using Vision--Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30909)**  `arXiv:2610.30909`  
  _Minhyeok Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present TwinStore, a source-available synthetic consumer platform for physical retail built on vision--language models. TwinStore turns a store into a digital twin---from 82 ready-made formats, a floor-plan image or phone photos of its shelves---and fills it with synthetic shoppers who plan a trip, walk the aisles and decide what to buy by looking at rendered shelves with packs, price tags and promotion signs. Every trip ends in a receipt, so a layout, price, pack or promotion can be tried on the same shoppers before it reaches the store. Around this core, TwinStore provides 3D playback, paired comparisons and layout optimization, shopper interviews, a trade-area model, counterfactual edits of real shelf photos, a marketing studio with eleven tools, and an assistant that turns a plain sentence into a drafted study or an answer grounded in a run's own numbers. In a held-out validation on eleven public datasets, simulated receipts approach the households' own natural variation (category-mix divergence 0.017--0.018 against a real drift of 0.012), price elasticities fall inside the range measured in the field, and three independent decision models agree. TwinStore runs on cloud or on-device models and is distributed with installers for Windows, macOS and Linux.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Phenotypic Similarity: Relation Refinement and Selective Population Graph Learning for Multimodal Brain Disorder Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30902)**  `arXiv:2610.30902`  
  _Yinyu Lan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Population graph learning supports brain disorder diagnosis by sharing information across subjects. Its effectiveness, however, depends on whether the relationships guiding this exchange are relevant to diagnosis. Similarity in demographic or acquisition attributes does not necessarily indicate diagnostic agreement, and heterogeneous subjects may require different representation updates within the same population graph. We propose PopDRP, a framework that combines diagnosis-guided relation refinement with hierarchical selective state-space learning. Using only training labels, Contrastive Affinity Refinement calibrates attribute-based affinities according to diagnostic agreement. Neural Graph Rectification then refines these relationships using fused functional-connectivity and non-imaging representations, guided by graph reconstruction and dual-view alignment objectives. On the resulting graph, a Selective Pyramid State-Space backbone integrates input-dependent state updates, graph diffusion, and hierarchical pooling and unpooling to learn population representations at multiple resolutions. Together, these components connect the refinement of inter-subject relationships with selective representation learning over the population graph. Experiments on ABIDE and ADHD-200 under stratified ten-fold cross-validation show improved accuracy and AUC over the compared baselines. Ablation studies further support the contributions of relation refinement and the selective hierarchical backbone to classification performance.
  </details>

- **[Parameter Learning in Active Inference via Variational Message Passing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30899)**  `arXiv:2610.30899`  
  _Benedikt Waldeck_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Active inference casts perception, learning and planning as variational inference, where the Expected Free Energy (EFE) defines the planning objective. Recent work showed that EFE minimization can be written as Variational Free Energy (VFE) minimization on a generative model augmented with epistemic priors, and that the resulting entropy-corrected objective is minimized by sum-product message passing (SP-MP) on modified factor functions. These formulations treat model uncertainty as inference over a finite set of known models. We extend them to planning during learning, where beliefs over the observation and transition parameters $\theta_A$, $\theta_B$ are Dirichlet priors. Closed-form Dirichlet updates are usually obtained by a structured mean-field factorization between parameters and states, but under exactly that factorization the information-gain corrections vanish. We resolve this by giving every factor its own parameter prior, frozen during a rollout. Conjugacy then makes each factor belief a product of a marginal over the categorical variables and a one-step Bayes posterior of the parameters. This reintroduces the datum-dependent parameter posteriors that mean-field factorization destroys. We show that evaluating the epistemic priors under the resulting Bethe joint recovers the information gains. Stationarity of this single closed-form objective yields SP-MP whose factor functions are augmented by the information-gain weight. Experiments on two grid POMDPs, in which only a planner that learns the model finds the safe route, show that the method plans to efficiently learn $\theta_A$ and $\theta_B$. It reaches the goal on every episode, whereas SP-MP planners with the same inference update plateau or take the risky route.
  </details>

- **[Semi-Linear Gaussian Bayesian Networks: Enhancing Flexibility in Node Dependencies via penalized B-Splines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30838)**  `arXiv:2610.30838`  
  _Aitor Oviedo-Madrid, Mabel Morales-Otero, Jose Gonzalez-Gomariz, Ruben Armañanzas_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bayesian networks for continuous variables often rely on restrictive assumptions: linear Gaussian models lack flexibility to capture nonlinear dependencies, while non-parametric approaches provide greater flexibility at a high computational cost. This creates a gap for modeling scenarios where variables are approximately Gaussian but exhibit nonlinear relationships. We introduce Semi-Linear Gaussian Bayesian Networks (SLGBN), a novel class of continuous Bayesian networks designed to bridge this gap, preserving Gaussian conditional distributions while introducing nonlinear conditional mean functions. The proposed framework provides a unified probabilistic framework combining linear Gaussian and nonlinear Gaussian conditional models within the same Bayesian network Nonlinear conditional mean functions are estimated using generalized additive models (GAMs) with penalized B-splines. We present parameter estimation procedures for both types of nodes, as well as a greedy hill-climbing algorithm for learning the network structure under the SLGBN framework. To assess its performance, we compare SLGBN with other continuous Bayesian network models, including semiparametric Bayesian networks (SPBN) and Linear Gaussian Bayesian networks (LGBN). Experimental results evaluate both goodness of fit and the ability to recover the underlying network structure. The results show that, in the presence of nonlinear Gaussian relationships, SLGBN achieves better fit and improved structure recovery compared to alternative models, particularly when combined with the Extended Bayesian Information Criterion (EBIC*) score. Furthermore, by retaining Gaussian conditional distributions, SLGBN offers computational advantages over fully semiparametric approaches.
  </details>

- **[TESSA: A Governance-Oriented Custom GPT for SME Guidance under the EU AI Act: Design, Implementation, and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30822)**  `arXiv:2610.30822`  
  _Chalisa Veesommai Sillberga, Kai-Kristian Kemella, Pekka Sillberga, Katri Harjuveteläinenb, Mika Saaria, Pekka Abrahamssona_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The increasing adoption of generative AI presents new opportunities while raising AI governance and regulatory challenges, particularly for small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) navigating the European Union Artificial Intelligence Act (EU AI Act). To support AI governance education, this study developed TESSA, a governance-oriented educational artifact within the OpenAI Custom GPT environment. Following a design science research approach, TESSA was evaluated against baseline ChatGPT using 100 benchmark scenarios through governance alignment assessment, human expert review, and consistency testing. Preliminary LLM-assisted assessment showed governance alignment rates of 90% for TESSA and 83% for baseline ChatGPT, while independent validation by an AI regulation law practitioner yielded 71% and 70%, respectively. Human expert review indicated that both systems provided generally strong governance guidance, while TESSA maintained high consistency across repeated executions. The findings suggest that governance-oriented grounding can support structured, practical, and stable AI-assisted compliance guidance for SMEs. The study contributes (i) a governance-oriented Custom GPT artifact for AI governance education, (ii) a human-validated regulatory update mechanism, and (iii) a multidimensional evaluation framework for governance-oriented AI artifact.
  </details>

- **[Agentic Knowledge-Model Co-Evolution via Consistency Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30790)**  `arXiv:2610.30790`  
  _Sirui Chen, Zhining Liu, Chao Zhang, Jingrui He, Zheng Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents can evolve by learning from accumulated experience. Existing approaches extract reusable external knowledge, such as skills and memories, from experience to guide subsequent task solving, with some further internalizing this knowledge through model training. However, different ways of analyzing and transforming the same experience can yield knowledge with substantially different utility, even when the resulting guidance appears reasonable. Inspired by consistency regularization, we identify consistent utility gains across different ways of analyzing the same experience, rather than relying on any single way of analyzing it. We introduce SPLICE, a framework for consistency-regularized knowledge-model co-evolution. It constructs evolution views by perturbing how the same experience is analyzed, producing different knowledge candidates. Knowledge updates are then guided by common gain, which accounts for utility gains and their consistency across views. During model evolution, self-distillation internalizes the evolved knowledge alongside reinforcement learning, using knowledge associated with consistent utility degradation as a contrastive reference. The two processes interleave, with model updates driving further knowledge evolution. Experiments across diverse domains show that SPLICE outperforms knowledge evolution and model training baselines by up to 7.1 and 4.6 points in average accuracy, while its evolved knowledge reduces output tokens per correct solution by up to 44% and transfers across model sizes and families.
  </details>

- **[An Introduction to World Models for Generalist Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30789)**  `arXiv:2610.30789`  
  _Zhihui Zhu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models have recently emerged as a promising direction toward developing generalist agents capable of understanding, predicting, and interacting with diverse environments. However, despite growing interest, the concept of a world model remains loosely defined, encompassing approaches ranging from predictive dynamics models and learned simulators to models that jointly generate future observations and actions. This introductory paper aims to clarify the concept of world models and provide a unified perspective on their role in developing generalist agents. Starting from the partially observable Markov decision process (POMDP) framework, we connect world modeling to classical control, dynamical systems, signal processing, and reinforcement learning. We introduce different approaches to world modeling, emphasizing the knowledge they capture, their learning objectives, and how they support decision-making. We further explore the relationship between world modeling and policy learning, highlighting how these capabilities can be unified through conditional prediction without requiring separate models. Rather than offering an exhaustive survey of existing methods, this paper focuses on fundamental concepts, modeling principles, and connections, with the goal of providing an accessible and unified introduction to world models for generalist agents.
  </details>

- **[ProQuant: Preserving Expert Quality over Quantity in Ultra-Low-Bit Quantization of Mixture-of-Experts Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30779)**  `arXiv:2610.30779`  
  _Jiaqi Chen, Xianglong Yan, Chengzhu Bao, Xie Chen, Yulun Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) large language models reduce computation through sparse activation but still incur substantial storage costs. Mixed-precision post-training quantization (PTQ) reduces these costs by quantizing different model components at different bit widths, but existing methods often struggle to preserve generation quality under tight bit budgets. We identify two key challenges: (1) nonlinear quantization errors at extremely low precision are underestimated when cross-layer propagation is ignored; (2) direct router fine-tuning may cause routing drift, and calibration gains may not generalize to downstream tasks. To address these issues, we propose ProQuant, a weight-only PTQ framework for MoE models. Prune-or-Prioritize Mixed-Precision Allocation (PoP) jointly optimizes expert pruning and bit-width assignment. It allows higher-precision experts to substitute for severely degraded ones while improving bit-budget utilization. Routing-Consistent RMSNorm Finetune (RoCo) adapts expert input representations through mathematically equivalent transformations. It compensates for quantization errors to reduce routing drift. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ProQuant outperforms existing methods across diverse generative tasks. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Scaling Laws for Generative Reward Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30765)**  `arXiv:2610.30765`  
  _Angel Raychev, Nathan Lile, Rafael Rafailov_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study generative reward models (GenRMs) as online preference providers for policy optimization in creative writing. Across Qwen3 reward-model sizes from 0.6B to 14B, Thinking judges trained with GRPO have higher static Gold-label accuracy than Answer-Only judges trained with SFT. This ranking often reverses when the judges train policies through online DPO: Answer-Only rewarders yield higher peak and top-five-average policy Elo in seven of eight matched settings under a fixed, learned 32B Gold evaluator. Intermediate Thinking-judge checkpoints can also outperform later, more statically accurate checkpoints as rewarders. Sweeps over rewarder size, policy size, training duration and DPO regularization show substantial benefits from reward-model capacity, including judges larger than the policy. We analyze trajectories with evaluator-specific Elo arenas, conditional match-resampling intervals and descriptive scaling fits. These results demonstrate an evaluator-rewarder gap in the studied pipelines: fixed-distribution judging accuracy does not determine downstream optimization quality. The comparison changes judge-training objective as well as response format, and the main ranking is conditional on its learned reference rather than a universal human-quality result. A limited follow-up using a different learned judge is inconclusive overall.
  </details>

- **[AgentDP: Towards Automated Model Deployment for Heterogeneous Embedded AI Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30760)**  `arXiv:2610.30760`  
  _Leming Shen, Qiang Yang, Xinyu Huang, Zijing Ma, Yuanqing Zheng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper presents AgentDP, which aims to deploy pre-trained AI models on heterogeneous embedded devices by fully automating model optimization and adaptation (O&A) with Large Language Model (LLM) agents. AgentDP not only significantly reduces human effort via automation but also enhances model inference efficacy by integrating LLMs with device-specific domain knowledge.
