To deliver a robust automation framework, AgentDP develops novel solutions to address three key technical challenges: 1) To help users elicit clear requirements, we propose a user requirement standardization module; 2) To mitigate the execution environment mismatch between AI models and heterogeneous embedded devices, we design an operator-aware model partition paradigm that enables different parts of the model to be efficiently executed on distinct inference engines. 3) To avoid re-searching optimization hyperparameters for new tasks, we propose a configuration reuse module that heuristically retrieves previous hyperparameter configurations for efficient reuse. Extensive experiments demonstrate that by fully automating the entire O&A process, AgentDP reduces entire model adaptation time by up to 80% compared with manual O&A while achieving higher task accuracy (more than 13% on average) than the baselines. Additionally, a comprehensive user study also exhibits the robust generalizability of AgentDP in adapting to different user requirements for heterogeneous embedded devices.
  </details>

- **[Is Complex Agentic Memory Always Needed? Naive RAG Is Stronger Than We Thought](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30755)**  `arXiv:2610.30755`  
  _Zhengye Bao, Shengtao Zhang, Haokai Zhang, Shiqi Lou, Muning Wen, Minnan Luo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM memory is widely used in long-term conversation understanding. Agentic memory systems often use mutation, structural organization, learned retrieval strategies, or other complex operations. Is this complexity always necessary? We study Raw-RAG, a naive RAG baseline using dense retrieval and reranking over raw history to supply evidence to a reader LLM. Across LoCoMo, LongMemEval, MEME, and MemoryAgentBench, Raw-RAG ranks first among the compared memory systems in six of seven benchmark-model settings, outperforming the strongest compared agentic memory system by 0.13-5.53 points. We investigate why raw-history retrieval remains competitive. Evidence analysis shows that its higher coverage of necessary evidence can offset lower utilization. Useful evidence includes both answer values and the relations and dependency paths needed to derive them. Even complete access to transformed memory stores leaves an accuracy gap relative to raw evidence, highlighting the importance of preserving raw information. These findings challenge the necessity of complex agentic memory for the evaluated conversation tasks and establish naive RAG as an essential baseline.
  </details>

- **[Faithful Latent Harm Monitors Work No Better Than the Models Own Behaviour at a Matched False Alarm Rate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30727)**  `arXiv:2610.30727`  
  _Abhishek Mishra, Sagnik Chatterjee, Suyash Maniyar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models have been reported to encode the harmfulness of a request separately from their decision to refuse it, and latent safety monitors such as Latent Guard build on this report by classifying prompts as harmful from the internal activations of the model, with the aim of catching harmful intent even when the model complies. Whether these representations give a signal of harm that survives distribution shift and is independent of model behaviour is unclear. We test this on out-of-distribution prompts made by rewriting existing harmful and benign requests with surface rewording, persuasive framing and jailbreak-style wrappers. Across 15 instruction-tuned LLMs and 38 activation-based readers, we compare harm detection with that of TF-IDF and sentence-embedding classifiers, with independently trained safety classifiers, and with the refusal of each model. Activations are read at the last token of the user instruction, where the model has read the whole request, and at the last chat-template token before the reply, where the planned response can be represented. Because models disagree about which prompts to refuse, we can separate a signal of the harmfulness of the prompt from a signal of the refusal decision of the model. At the end of the instruction, the representation is largely independent of the refusal behaviour of each model but degrades under distribution shift, so that a TF-IDF classifier ranks harm better than the original Latent Guard in every model we test, and linear and MLP probes do about as well as sentence-embedding classifiers trained on the same examples. The representation just before generation detects more, catching 44 percent of harmful prompts at a matched false-alarm rate against 32 percent for the refusal of the model, but that gain follows the refusal decision itself, and part of it comes from refusals that string-matching evaluation misses.
  </details>

- **[A Topology-Guided GeoAI Agentic Framework for Urban Flood Screening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30660)**  `arXiv:2610.30660`  
  _Yutian Tao, Haoan Feng, Xin Xu, Leila De Floriani_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open-world geographic artificial intelligence (GeoAI) agents can transform public geospatial data into accessible decision support for real-world challenges, but must balance autonomous reasoning with reliable spatial analysis and human oversight. We present a topology-guided GeoAI agentic framework for urban flood screening that takes a place name, drawn region, or uploaded boundary and returns ranked candidate basins with an interactive map and report. A deterministic controller retrieves public elevation, rainfall, soil, land-cover, exposure, and infrastructure layers, extracts terrain depressions with discrete Morse theory, and ranks basins with a relative flood-priority score. The language model acts only at recorded decision points: selecting a persistence threshold from a precomputed candidate table, drafting recommendations from computed evidence, and reviewing each recommendation against the same evidence. Three human-in-the-loop checkpoints let users revise the threshold, factor weights, or recommendation. In a College Park, Maryland case study, the selected threshold reduced 278 raw terrain minima to 70 basins, with 77% of these basins' lowest points also identified as sinks by a separately computed eight-direction (D8) flow-routing model. Across four further study areas, agreement ranged from 61% to 83%. By combining open data, explicit spatial reasoning, traceable evidence, and human oversight, the framework enables transparent first-pass flood screening while keeping consequential recommendations inspectable and user-controlled.
  </details>

- **[Anticipating Social Consequences with Distributional World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30657)**  `arXiv:2610.30657`  
  _Viet-Thanh Pham, Thuy-Trang Vu, Zhuang Li, Lizhen Qu, Gholamreza Haffari_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Social world models predict how interactions develop after an action, helping agents negotiate, cooperate and mediate. In these situations, one action can lead to several plausible futures, hence predicting only one future can overlook consequential alternatives, while exploring them through repeated simulations increases inference cost. We propose DSWM, a Distributional Social World Model that predicts alternative responses, their probabilities and immediate and final outcome values in one output per candidate action. We train a 4B model on repeated simulations using supervised fine-tuning and GRPO. At inference time, the actor uses these possible consequences to choose an action. Across three seen and two unseen environments, with interaction backbones absent from training collection, DSWM achieves the best average performance on 13 of 15 metrics. Ablations show that retaining multiple futures and their predicted probabilities improves outcomes in all five environments.
  </details>

- **[Speculative Acceptance Does Not Decay with Position: A Full-Length Diagnostic on Long Chain-of-Thought with Independent Drafts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30644)**  `arXiv:2610.30644`  
  _Zhiming Meng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The claim that speculative-decoding acceptance rate decays as a chain-of-thought grows longer has become a de facto industry narrative, motivating draft-model co-design and position-aware training. Nearly all direct evidence comes from trained drafters whose offline training length is far shorter than inference, so a purely positional law has never been isolated. We present the first systematic, full-length measurement of acceptance rate as a function of generation position on MATH-500 (all 500 problems), under greedy and sampling decoding, with two independent drafts against DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B: a same-family draft (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-1.5B) and a cross-family draft (Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct), yielding about 1.78M verification rounds. Using within-problem paired tests, we find that after isolating a greedy-decoding repetition pathology, acceptance rate does not decay with position in any of the four configurations within the supported ranges (greedy 0-8K, sampling 0-12K); the second half of each trajectory is slightly but significantly higher than the first. A controlled temperature sweep shows that the apparent sampling-mode degradation of high-entropy reflection tokens is a visibility artifact of argmax agreement, not a drop in expected acceptance probability. Rejections concentrate with high precision on reflection trigger tokens such as "wait", "however" and "check", an entropy-selective failure that a lightweight three-arm LoRA trajectory-alignment intervention (uniform, reflection-marked, entropy-weighted) fails to improve in mean accepted length. We thus supply the positional baseline that independent draft-target pairs were missing, and localize the residual failure to decision-point entropy rather than position.
  </details>

- **[Large language models show systematic failures in incorporating evolving medical knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30631)**  `arXiv:2610.30631`  
  _Min Zeng, Zaifu Zhan, Kai Yu, Rui Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical knowledge evolves as clinical guidelines revise recommendations, yet whether large language models (LLMs) faithfully incorporate such changes remains unclear. We introduce ChronoEvo, comprising 1,709 recommendation-level revisions across 12 forms of medical knowledge change, to distinguish recognition of revised guidance from incorporation into clinical recommendations. In the primary model, 90.8% of responses recognized revisions and 80.2% were clinically correct, but only 58.5% fully incorporated guidance, yielding a 32.3-percentage-point recognition-incorporation gap. This dissociation persisted across 14 models and was corroborated by two clinicians' adjudicated assessment of 300 responses, yielding a 40.3-percentage-point gap in the validation sample. Changes in recommendation strength and patient eligibility were associated with retention of outdated guidance. A revision-salience intervention reduced outdated-content retention and improved clinical correctness but did not significantly improve full incorporation. These findings identify faithful incorporation of evolving medical knowledge as a distinct challenge for clinical LLM reliability, even when updated guideline evidence is available.
  </details>

- **[Model Organisms of Collusion: Corrective Transfer in Untrusted Monitors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30582)**  `arXiv:2610.30582`  
  _Juan-Pablo Rivera, Ari Spiesberger, Juan J Vazquez, Arun Jose, Axel Ahlqvist, Adeline Kassler, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI control protocols aim to get useful work from models that may be misaligned by monitoring their behaviour. One approach uses a second copy of the same model as the monitor. This avoids relying on a weaker reviewer the model could outsmart, but risks collusion: the model, now monitoring itself, may cover up its own misbehaviour. One remedy is to fine-tune the monitor on whatever examples of misbehaviour the control team has caught. We ask whether this training also removes collusion that the control team has not yet found. We fine-tune Llama-3.3-70B and Qwen2.5-72B to install five policy-monitor behaviour pairs, correct the monitor on 300 honest reviews of one caught collusion, and measure collusion on the other four in unseen prompt formats. Averaged over the three seeds of each family, correction reduces held-out collusion from 91% to 14-20% under three of the four methods we compare (individual seeds 10-32%), while a control fine-tuned on the same number of unrelated examples leaves 56-93%. Family-average accuracy on control items changes by at most five points and capability is preserved, apart from a drop in mathematical accuracy on one Llama seed. Under the tested training configuration, methods with similar caught-task accuracy differ in held-out transfer, from 58% to 83% average reduction, so the caught behaviour alone does not reveal how far a correction reaches. Catching one collusion lets the control team reduce, but not remove, collusion it has not caught, by retraining the monitor alone.
  </details>

- **[Diagnosing Personalization Failures in Mobile GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30573)**  `arXiv:2610.30573`  
  _Zhixin Lin, Dongliang Xu, Jungang Li, Shidong Pan, Jun Feng, Yanbin Sun, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unlike conventional GUI agents, personalized GUI agents require user preferences to guide sequential execution over changing GUI states. This raises two challenges: determining HOW each preference should alter execution strategy and WHEN it should take effect along the interaction trajectory. Existing benchmarks primarily evaluate overall task success, offering limited insight into these questions. We introduce PrefDiag-Bench with two components: (1) a categorization of preferences into five types, characterizing HOW they affect execution; and (2) a stage-wise diagnostic framework with 1,664 probes, tracing WHEN preferences should take effect and where failures occur. We evaluate eleven GUI agent systems, including end-to-end agents and agentic workflows, under two forms of user context: explicit user profiles and behavioral memory logs. Although existing agents achieve relatively high completion rates on non-personalized tasks, performance is substantially lower on preference-personalized tasks. Even the best-performing model, Seed-2.0-Pro, achieves only 47.17% on Process preferences under explicit user profiles. Agents struggle particularly with Ranking and Process preferences. Further analysis reveals two key capability bottlenecks: selecting relevant evidence from user history and recognizing when preferences should take effect during execution. Controlled interventions that explicitly provide relevant evidence improve end-to-end task success by up to 7.06%. PrefDiag-Bench offers a more systematic evaluation of GUI agents' personalization capabilities beyond overall task success. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PLE: Physical-Engineering's Last Exam](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30537)**  `arXiv:2610.30537`  
  _Rujun Han, Yufan Zhuang, Arijit Ray, Han Yu, Zoey CuiZhu, George Lee, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier language models now score above 90% on popular knowledge benchmarks and are increasingly deployed as agents that carry out multi-hour professional work. Yet no existing benchmark tests whether they can perform the real-world, economically valuable work of a professional physical engineer, such as modelling physics and legal code requirements across a multi-format project corpus, optimising material and installation costs, and issuing 2D/3D engineering design packages that a licensed reviewer would sign off on. Existing engineering benchmarks focus on textbook question answering, isolated tool calls or self-contained toy design problems. As a result, most of these benchmarks are already saturated by leading agents and solving them is far away from delivering real engineering design. We introduce PLE, a benchmark of 85 long-horizon, agentic tasks in professional physical engineering consisting of electrical, civil, structural and mechanical engineering, where solving a task means completing a realistic, billable engineering deliverable. PLE combines two complementary curation paths. Expert-Authored tasks are created end to end by practising engineers from a professional data provider's expert pool. Co-Authored tasks start from problem statements written by practising engineers; an adversarial Creator-Solver agent pipeline constructs each task's realistic project environment, executable grader and reference deliverables before sending them to expert engineers for validation and difficulty intensification. We evaluate a variety of frontier LLMs under seven publicly available agent harnesses. Even the strongest configuration reaches a mean score of only 50.6%, while spending 27M tokens and 63 minutes per task on average, demonstrating the substantial gap between current agents and autonomous physical engineering design.
  </details>

- **[From Single-Actor Environments to Multi-Actor Event Worlds: Can LLMs Be Event World Models?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30488)**  `arXiv:2610.30488`  
  _Juntong Ni, Zewen Liu, Bohan Wang, Lige Zhang, Wei Jin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models help agents assess the consequences of their actions before acting. Many real systems, however, involve multiple actors who make their own decisions, can evolve even when the agent takes no action, and are only partially observed through event logs. We formalize these systems as event worlds and ask whether large language models can predict future observations from the log and the agent's proposed actions. We build complementary benchmarks from human Diplomacy games and real Polymarket trades. Our hierarchical framework compares prediction with and without other actors' actions, then tests multi-step rollouts, predictions for actions never taken, and usefulness to agents. Fine-tuning and more data improve prediction when all actors' recorded actions are given. With only the agent's action, models struggle when other actors strongly affect the outcome, and larger models and longer histories help little. This gap also limits multi-step prediction and agent decisions, with models overestimating plan success. Yet imagined experience and initialization from world models can still improve policy learning. Anticipating other actors' actions remains a central challenge for event world modeling.
  </details>

- **[Same Beliefs, Different Policies: Learning Reusable Representations for Early Stopping in LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30450)**  `arXiv:2610.30450`  
  _Jingwei Ni, Dmitry Abulkhanov, Vladislav Smirnov, Markus Leippold, Elliott Ash, Timothy Baldwin, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning LLMs often continue reasoning after reaching a correct answer, wasting computation and sometimes replacing it with an incorrect one. Deciding when to stop is a metareasoning problem: the model must weigh what further reasoning could achieve against its cost, given what it can already answer. Direct utility training learns a stopping representation at a chosen token price; supporting new prices can require additional controller training. We introduce BELT (BELief-based Termination), which separates outcome prediction from stopping control. It learns beliefs about current and future answers from a frozen LLM's hidden states, then fits a linear stopping policy on the frozen features. The prediction targets are independent of token price, so one probe supports different accuracy-cost trade-offs through CPU policy refitting or threshold adjustment. Averaged over five out-of-distribution (OOD) math benchmarks, BELT reduces generated tokens by 41% on Qwen3-1.7B and 51% on OLMo-3-7B-Think without loss of accuracy. The same math-trained BELT transfers to coding and agentic tasks without training on code or agentic data. In all three domains, its accuracy-cost trade-offs are competitive with or better than those of end-to-end controllers. On OLMo3, its probe has 21.2x fewer parameters than one checkpoint of the strongest baseline, OS-Pruner, and needs 55x fewer training GPU-hours than OS-Pruner's five price-specific checkpoints.
  </details>

- **[A Three-Pillar Governance Framework for Safe and Trustworthy AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30445)**  `arXiv:2610.30445`  
  _Edward C. Cheng, Jeshua Cheng, Alice Siu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents capable of autonomous decision-making introduce significant challenges related to safety, alignment, accountability, and human oversight. Existing Human-in-the-Loop (HITL) and responsible AI approaches provide important foundations, but many remain fragmented across system design, governance, and operational deployment. This paper presents a Three-Pillar (3P) model and framework for developing safe and trustworthy AI agents through transparency, accountability, and trustworthiness. The framework defines an evolutionary path in which increasing agent autonomy is introduced through progressive validation, structured human oversight, and auditable operational controls. We further describe how the model supports agent lifecycle monitoring, decision traceability, escalation management, and human-agent collaboration within enterprise agentic environments. By integrating oversight principles with operational mechanisms, the proposed framework provides a practical foundation for deploying AI agents that remain aligned with human intent while enabling controlled progression toward higher autonomy.
  </details>

- **[SpecMAGIC: Specification-Guided Multi-Task Generalization in Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30443)**  `arXiv:2610.30443`  
  _Zijian Guo, İlker Işık, H. M. Sabbir Ahmad, Wenchao Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-task generalization is an important yet underexplored problem in multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL). Many real-world applications require multi-agent systems to generalize across complex, temporally extended tasks with diverse inter-agent coordination requirements. We introduce a specification language that combines temporal-logic task specifications with operators for expressing such requirements. We propose SpecMAGIC, a hierarchical framework that leverages the automaton structure of task specifications for subtask decomposition. A centralized high-level planner identifies and allocates compatible subtask sequences across agents, while decentralized low-level policies execute individual subtasks. This enables zero-shot generalization to new tasks by composing reusable subtask policies according to new specifications, without retraining. We establish soundness and probabilistic completeness of the high-level planner. Experiments on multi-agent navigation tasks show that SpecMAGIC substantially outperforms existing approaches on unseen task specifications and maintains strong performance as the number of agents increases.
  </details>

- **[RoleFlow: Self-Evolving Multi-Role Agentic Collaboration via Structural Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30429)**  `arXiv:2610.30429`  
  _Yi Ge, Pan Wang, Xuehao Cui, Zian Zeng, Xu Hu, Heng Huang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) based multi-agent systems often rely on predefined collaboration structures, limiting their adaptability to evolving task requirements. Although recent approaches enable dynamic orchestration, trajectory-level rewards they apply assign one outcome to each decision, ofen struggling to identify the contributions of individual structural changes. We propose RoleFlow, a self-evolving multi-role agentic system that refines collaboration structures through feedback-driven atomic edits and on-demand role synthesis. To make structural adaptation learnable, we introduce Single-Edit Counterfactual Policy Optimization (SECPO), which evaluates each edit against its no-edit counterpart at the same chain state and assigns targeted credit to edit-specific tokens. Across seven benchmarks spanning four domains, RoleFlow achieves the optimal average performance, surpassing the strongest competitor by 4.7 points. It leads on all three multi-hop QA benchmarks by 3.2-7.5 F1 points and also shows supremacy on mathematical reasoning, web interaction, and code generation. Further analyses demonstrate cross-benchmark transferability, training-data efficiency, and cross-executor generalization.
  </details>

- **[Soft Ontology Priors over Latent Concept Posteriors for Weakly Supervised Neuro-Symbolic Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30391)**  `arXiv:2610.30391`  
  _Tengwei Song, Robert Hoehndorf_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neuro-symbolic (NeSy) predictors that supervise only the task label suffer from reasoning shortcuts: distinct concept mappings achieve the same maximum likelihood, leaving the recovered concepts unidentifiable and unsafe for downstream reuse. We attack this failure by injecting a soft ontology prior over the latent concepts into the training objective and compiling it together with the task constraint into a smooth, decomposable probabilistic circuit. Under independent Bernoulli perception, the normalisers of the resulting structured concept posterior reduce to weighted model counts that are linear in the compiled circuit size, because the neural leaf weights fold into the circuit before evaluation. The soft compilation strategy places expert axioms in the circuit's weights rather than its support (task-structural axioms guaranteed by the data-generating process stay hard), so noisy expert axioms only redistribute prior mass and cannot empty the support. We characterise how the prior reweights label-restricted shortcut mass through differences in ontology violation energy, show that soft priors incur bounded log-density perturbations under bounded axiom noise, and prove that the hard-support counterpart can be made degenerate by a single corrupted axiom. We validate these predictions on an audited six-concept view of the real-world BDD-OIA driving benchmark and on controlled NeSy tasks.
  </details>

- **[Route-and-Generate: KL-Regularized Router Improvement for Reasoning Routing in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30351)**  `arXiv:2610.30351`  
  _Tien-Phat Nguyen, Truong Nguyen, Thanh-Thien Le, Hoang Phan, Thien Huu Nguyen, Linh Ngo Van, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models often apply complex reasoning indiscriminately, leading to overthinking on simple tasks and underthinking on harder ones. We present Route-and-Generate (Rouge), a hierarchical training framework that enables LLMs to adaptively decide when and how much reasoning is required for each input. Our approach decouples reasoning effort allocation from answer generation by introducing a routing policy that allocates a fixed sampling budget across multiple reasoning formats, while the base model is optimized with GRPO using per-sample rewards. To promote efficient reasoning, we employ a rank-based reward that favors shorter correct solutions, and update the routing policy via a closed-form KL-regularized policy improvement step distilled into a neural router. This design encourages concise answers on easy problems while reserving longer reasoning for harder ones, achieving improved accuracy-efficiency trade-offs across multiple reasoning benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Breaking the high-degree barrier in Lehmer's problem with a human-AI discovery loop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30277)**  `arXiv:2610.30277`  
  _Yuri Chervonyi, Sergei Gukov, Thang Luong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While artificial intelligence has made rapid progress in mathematical reasoning, many open problems depend on structure that is revealed only through large-scale computational exploration and analytical synthesis. One such example is Lehmer's problem from 1933. It asks whether the Mahler measure of an integer polynomial, which measures how far its roots stray from the unit circle, can be made arbitrarily small. Previously, constructing irreducible polynomials with integer coefficients and small Mahler measure has been carried out systematically only for even degrees $d \le 180$. At higher degrees, Boyd's analytical one-parameter family frequently factors, skipping nearly 50% of target degrees and leaving no general construction for arbitrary $d$. Here, we address the high-degree regime through a human-AI discovery loop, in which we extend the conversational prompting with a fast numerical pipeline, an exact symbolic verifier, and large-scale distributed search. The AI agent prototypes numerical and symbolic solvers locally, launches parallel searches across distributed clusters and works with the human researcher to extract and verify mathematical hypotheses. Through this interactive loop, we derive a five-parameter construction, which we call the Generalized Boyd algorithm. It eliminates degree-skipping and constructs irreducible polynomials with Mahler measure strictly below Boyd's limit point $\alpha_1 \approx 1.255434$ for every target degree. Evaluated across all even degrees up to 2000, our approach improves upon six previously published values and holds 99.6% of all best known records. With this work we release the verified dataset of polynomials with minimal Mahler measure across all studied even degrees.
  </details>

- **[Feedback That Backfires in Games: Do LLM Players Repeat the Move They Just Watched Fail?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30205)**  `arXiv:2610.30205`  
  _Esmail Gumaan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When an LLM agent's move fails, the harness writes the move and the failure message into the transcript and asks again. In tool calling, this record makes small models more likely to repeat the failed action. We test whether the same holds in games (Sokoban, Connect Four, Othello), where a move can fail by being illegal or by being legal but losing, both verified exactly. On fixed probe items built by perturbing a correct move, the record backfires: pooled over 8 instruction-tuned models (135M-1.7B) it makes the failed move more likely (corrective gain G = -1.38 nats per token), through the move's surface form, for illegal moves but not for losing ones, and not for every model. An exploratory analysis shows what sets the sign: after the record, the failed move's log-probability barely depends on how likely it was before (slope 0.18). Moves the model would not have played become more likely, and greedy decoding starts to produce them; moves it was about to play become less likely, but greedy decoding still repeats 38% of them. A description of the failure that omits the move string keeps the model's preference instead. Along the OLMo 2 1B post-training ladder the inversion is present in the base model and largely removed at the DPO stage. RAGEN's default models invert in RAGEN's own Sokoban format before any RL. A "do not repeat" instruction does nothing, a clean retry is the worst harness, and a decoder ban stops exact repeats but not illegal play. All measurements ran in float32 on a free Colab GPU, which first reproduced the original paper's number.
  </details>

- **[When Stable and Preferred Semantics Agree: A Path-Based Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30176)**  `arXiv:2610.30176`  
  _Yan Zhang, Kehan Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We investigate structural conditions under which preferred and stable semantics agree in abstract argumentation frameworks. Our approach is based on suitably chosen sets of paths in the attack graph and binary relations induced by them. We establish a general path-based criterion for semantic agreement and instantiate it with several classes of paths, obtaining sufficient conditions expressed in terms of well-foundedness of the corresponding relations.The resulting conditions strictly generalize two principal structural criteria for preferred and stable agreement in the literature: Dung's limited controversiality condition and the SCC-symmetry condition. In particular, Dung's result is recovered as a special case by taking the relevant path set to be the set of even paths. We further introduce two infinite sequences of path sets, each yielding a family of agreement conditions. For the first sequence, a Collapse Theorem reduces the infinitely many associated well-foundedness conditions to three essentially distinct ones, whereas the second sequence gives rise to genuinely distinct conditions with no analogous collapse. Our results apply to both finite and infinite argumentation frameworks, admit simplified formulations in the finite case, and also yield sufficient conditions for the existence of stable extensions.
  </details>

- **[Neural Networks with Differentiable Belief-Function Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30173)**  `arXiv:2610.30173`  
  _Antonin Sulc_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Yager's 1987 rule of combination $\boxplus$ was proposed on epistemic grounds: under high conflict, mass should be redistributed to ignorance rather than renormalised onto the class simplex. We observe that backpropagation needs the same rule: Dempster's normaliser $(1{-}\kappa)^{-1}$, where $\kappa$ is the conflict mass, puts a $(1{-}\kappa)^{-2}$ term in the gradient that diverges exactly when evidence sources disagree, while Yager's $\boxplus$ is bilinear and has no such term. Building on this, we construct a deep evidential pipeline in which every mass in the chain from heads to output is a valid Dempster--Shafer mass function, combined by Yager's rule. The output mass on the ignorance set, $m(\Theta)$, serves as the network's ``I do not know'' signal. The network is fit to hard labels and never sees an annotator distribution, yet $m(\Theta)$ orders human disagreement in two modalities, visual and linguistic. Dempster's rises too and ranks contested inputs about as well, but its normaliser divides the conflict out: Yager's vacuity is $9\times$ larger on the most contested CIFAR-10H images, so only Yager's is readable as an amount of ignorance without recalibration. For out-of-distribution detection it is competitive with strong single-pass baselines, while the clamped Dempster variant that scores higher becomes gradient-unstable once the clamp is removed.
  </details>

- **[Accelerating Wildfire Hazard Mapping Across Canada with an AI Surrogate Modelling Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30170)**  `arXiv:2610.30170`  
  _Hager Radi Abdelwahed, Toluwaleke Olutayo, Chirag Goel, Jana Osea, Aldo Zaimi, Tom Swystun, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Canada's increasing wildfire activity requires frequent national hazard mapping to guide proactive mitigation, including fuel management, land-use planning, infrastructure protection, and evacuation routing. However, process-based wildfire simulation frameworks (e.g., BurnP3 and BurnP3+) rely on computationally intensive Monte Carlo simulations to model burn probability, fire intensity, and rate of spread. These workflows can take days to weeks to process a single regional modelling unit. To overcome these computational bottlenecks, we present three contributions developed through collaboration between AI researchers and wildfire modelling experts: (a) a standardized framework for training, fine-tuning, and evaluating AI surrogate models against process-based wildfire simulators; (b) a multi-task deep learning surrogate trained on national-scale data to emulate burn probability, mean fire intensity, and mean rate of spread at a fraction of the computational cost (seconds); and (c) domain-specific metrics and counterfactual evaluation protocols to probe out-of-distribution generalization. Integrating geospatial data (fuel, terrain, ignition likelihood) and regional weather, our model approximates BurnP3+ simulation outputs, achieving strong performance across agreement, rank-correlation, error, and spatial-overlap metrics. It preserves downstream hazard classifications and produces physically plausible responses to expert-informed counterfactual interventions. On a representative region, the surrogate achieves 806-fold end-to-end CPU acceleration and uses 13% of BurnP3+'s host memory, facilitating rapid scenario analysis.
  </details>

- **[The Cost of Saying Hello: An Empirical Study of Handshake Overhead in AI Agent Communication Protocols](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30151)**  `arXiv:2610.30151`  
  _Pranav Varshney_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Every interaction between AI agents begins with a handshake: the initiating agent must locate its counterpart, learn what it can do, and establish a session before the first useful message can be sent. Surveys of agent communication protocols name handshake overhead as an efficiency criterion, yet none report measurements. This paper presents the first empirical, phase-decomposed benchmark of the handshake in three major agent protocols: MCP (in both its 2025 initialize and 2026 discover eras), A2A, and ACP. Using each protocol's official SDK, we instrument every request for round trips, per-phase latency, wire bytes, and token cost across cold-start, warm-repeat, capability-mismatch, and degraded-metadata scenarios (N=50 per cell, on loopback and under an emulated 50 ms RTT). Round-trip count dominates handshake cost. A legacy MCP handshake takes three round trips and 230 ms at 50 ms RTT; A2A needs one round trip and 61.0 ms. Round trips are not the whole bill: when an LLM reads the counterpart's metadata, handshake cost scales with the counterpart's capability inventory at 24-107 tokens per capability, so a 200-capability counterpart spends 11% of a 200k-token context window before the agent reads its own task. MCP's own 2026 revision cuts its handshake from three round trips to two and, with era pinning, to zero on warm reconnects, protocol evolution that corroborates the cost we measure. We release the full harness, raw data, and analysis atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[TraceForge: Distilling with Your Own Thinking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29680)**  `arXiv:2610.29680`  
  _Yan Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Using an LLM to generate training data for another is among the most general ways to train one, and it remains common practice. Some frontier teams have closed that route for their own models: what comes back is an answer, or a short summary, without the detailed reasoning trace behind it. That is a little ironic: they were themselves built on distilling human knowledge, and distilling an open-source model is still entirely routine; the restriction applies to their own outputs alone. Recent work recovers the hidden reasoning by replaying encrypted blocks across models. It somehow works, but it breaks the providers' terms of service, so it is not something a product can be built on long-term.We built the alternative. We take the context and the teacher's answer as input, and generate the reasoning trace ourselves, in the style of the student's own model family. Training is two-stage, supervised and then adversarial: a generator learns to regenerate the family's own reasoning traces from their context and answer, and a critic learns how real a reasoning trace looks. Applied to a teacher's answer, the generator writes one in the family's style. An independent judge rates how likely traces are to be genuine on a scale from 0 to 1: ours average 0.68, real traces 0.66, and traces selected by the critic from four candidates 0.82. These are authenticity scores, not correctness rates.We test the idea on five benchmarks with everything else held fixed. On general tasks the five training corpora are about the same; on coding, the generated reasoning beats Fable 5's own thinking content. With the same 5,000 questions and the same teacher answers, HumanEval+ improves by 3.7 points and MBPP+ by 7.1 at the final checkpoint, and the MBPP+ lead holds at every checkpoint. We suspect that reasoning written in the student's family style is easier for it to learn from.
  </details>

- **[AgentMonopoly: Behavioral Patterns and Persona Control in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29330)**  `arXiv:2610.29330`  
  _Changshun Wu, Mengyu Li, Mathis Marsault, Xiaowei Huang, Chih-Hong Cheng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  How do LLM agents' strategies differ, and how much can instructions to adopt a persona change their behavior over a game? We study these questions with AgentMonopoly, an environment where agents communicate, buy and trade properties, and make decisions that affect later turns. We compare models from four families, each at two sizes, in games with identical models, different sizes of the same family, or different families. We also assign one agent a persona, a description of how it should behave, intended to change a tendency observed in games without that persona. The tested models differ in how often they trade, waive rent, and manage cash and properties. Larger models do not consistently win more games. Persona instructions change the words agents use and some of their actions, but the changes do not always match the role across all decisions. These results show why persona following should be assessed through actions and outcomes as well as language. As our findings are conditional on the tested checkpoints, prompts, and five seeds per configuration, the open-source nature of AgentMonopoly provides the experimental infrastructure for further extending these comparisons. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[From Comparison to Selection: Enhancing Cross-Scale Generalization of Pointer Networks Trained with Reinforcement Learning for for Capacitated Vehicle Routing Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27936)**  `arXiv:2610.27936`  
  _Sofiene Lassoued, Devara Tertia, Stefan Lier, Andreas Schwung_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vehicle routing in delivery and logistics remains challenging as the number of clients varies. Classical approaches based on mixed-integer programming and heuristics can scale poorly or require problem-specific design, motivating learning-based alternatives. Reinforcement learning is particularly promising, provided a single policy can generalise across problem sizes without retraining. Although attention-based Pointer Networks are inherently size-agnostic, their solution quality degrades under both upscaling and downscaling. Using the Capacitated Vehicle Routing Problem (CVRP) as a controlled proxy, we investigate this degradation and identify three contributing factors: shifts in node density, attention dispersion, and dilution of the mean-pooled graph context. We systematically compare architecture-wide, encoder-side, and decoder-side modifications to address these limitations and propose a learned, permutation-invariant graph-context aggregation mechanism, implemented as a weighted average and as Set2Set. Results reveal distinct requirements for transfer in each direction: entropy-based attention scaling reduces the CVRP20-to-CVRP100 performance gap by 1.41 percentage points, while the proposed aggregation methods achieve the largest improvement on the more challenging CVRP100-to-CVRP20 transfer, approximately 0.9 percentage points. Exploiting this complementarity, we introduce an approach selector that chooses the best model for each training-target size pair. The selector reduces cost relative to the reference model across all nine seen train-test pairs and eight of nine pairs involving unseen sizes (CVRP35, CVRP75, and CVRP200), achieving improvements of up to 3.2%.
  </details>

- **[RSI Control: Can AI Models Perform Covert Sabotage in the Self-Improvement Process?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27735)**  `arXiv:2610.27735`  
  _Kaiwen Zhou, Udari Madhushani Sehwag_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive self-improvement, in which frontier AI models work on training the next generation, is quickly becoming a reality. However, the safety of this process has received far less attention than it should. A misaligned model training its successor can shape what that successor knows, how it behaves, and what it wants, and the harm carries forward into the next generation. We study this risk through the lens of AI control. We introduce RSI Control Arena, a testbed in which a frontier model, acting as an autonomous R\&D agent, is given a benign main task from a stage of a successor model's training pipeline together with a covert side task to install a misaligned property in the successor. The testbed contains 16 side tasks in three classes, forming 22 task pairs with main tasks that span the pipeline from data curation to reinforcement learning. We evaluate six frontier models as agents against LLM monitors that read the transcript of the run and the agent's reasoning. We observe that every model completes a substantial share of the side tasks without degrading the main-task performance, including side tasks that make the successor itself a better saboteur. Under minimal instructions to stay covert, models already produce successful attacks that monitors fail to detect, and providing a short list of evasion tactics further reduces the detection rate. Monitors with access to agent's reasoning detects most attacks, but not all. Our results indicate that recursive self-improvement raises significant safety concerns, and that strong safety effort should be in place before models are trusted with greater responsibility of training their successors.
  </details>

- **[REFLEX: RNA Ensemble Generation via Flexibility-Calibrated Stochastic Bridge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26261)**  `arXiv:2610.26261`  
  _Wanli Ma, Tianmeng Hu, Ke Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  RNA molecules often function through multiple experimentally observed conformational states. A central challenge is to capture ensemble diversity without sacrificing structural fidelity: samples may collapse toward averaged representative structures or drift into unsupported conformational regions. We introduce REFLEX (RNA Ensemble generation calibrated by FLEXibility), a sequence-conditioned generator for RNA backbone-frame ensembles based on a heteroscedastic stochastic bridge on SE(3). During training, experimental B-factors calibrate residue-wise bridge widths, exposing each observed conformer through local perturbation neighborhoods with residue-specific scales while keeping constrained regions sharper. A learned flexibility condition guides the bridge-field network to recover target conformer-specific frames from these calibrated noisy states. At inference, REFLEX uses only the input sequence, requiring no MSA-subsampling heuristics. On a multi-conformer RNA benchmark, REFLEX achieves the best precision-recall trade-off for ensemble coverage across evaluation thresholds, while recovery-stress ablations indicate gains in target-frame recovery under stronger perturbations.
  </details>

- **[When Should AI Chime In? Benchmarking Timely Contribution of Proactive Agents in Multi-Party Chat](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25896)**  `arXiv:2610.25896`  
  _Ruotong Wang, Brian S. Yu, Xinyi Zhou, Joseph Chee Chang, Pao Siangliulue, Jonathan Bragg, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Proactive agents can support group collaboration by surfacing useful information without waiting to be asked, but even relevant contributions can be disruptive if poorly timed. In this work, we isolate the timing question: given a fixed candidate contribution such as a clarification or relevant fact, when should an AI agent chime in? We introduce PITCH (Proactive Intervention Timing in CHat), to our knowledge the first benchmark with large-scale human judgments of proactive agent timing collected from participants in the same unscripted multi-party conversations they took part in. To collect high-quality judgments, we design a novel interface in which 2-4 people chat synchronously, author candidate AI contribution messages, place their own and others' contributions into the chat history, and then deliberate as a group about the best placement for each message. PITCH contains 384 candidate contributions and 1,091 judgments, each marking an ideal turn, an acceptable window, or that a contribution should not appear, from 89 participants engaged in 35 conversations. Evaluating 15 models across 28 configurations, we find that while models can place contributions well when given the entire conversation history, they systematically chime in too early when asked to decide live as the conversation unfolds: best-turn accuracy falls from 57.8% to 19.0%, with nearly half of placements occurring before every participant's acceptable window. In comparison, lay humans deciding live are more accurate (23.7% vs 19.0%), and fewer of their placements are premature (39.7% vs 46.4%). Model reasoning traces show that 93% of intervention justifications cite relevance, while almost none consider waiting. By providing a human-grounded benchmark, our work reveals gaps in agents' ability to integrate into group settings and points towards proactive agents that can reason explicitly about appropriate timing.
  </details>

- **[TorchTPU - A Hybrid Eager/Compiled Architecture for Native PyTorch Execution on TPU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24161)**  `arXiv:2610.24161`  
  _Claudio Basile, Michael Voznesensky, Gunhyun Park, Ben Wilson, Parker Holloway, Zhanyong Wan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  TorchTPU is an open-source collaboration between Google and Meta that introduces a native and principled PyTorch backend for Google TPUs. It enables seamless out-of-the-box model execution and TPU migration while delivering industry-competitive inference and training performance through eager execution optimizations,this http URLintegration, and native hardware acceleration.
  </details>

- **[R$^2$Orch: Recursive Self-Improvement for Agent Orchestration via Self-Play within a Skill-Internalization Loop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22740)**  `arXiv:2610.22740`  
  _Mingda Zhang, Yun Huang, Qiang Huang, Yanjin Li, Rui Qian, Qichao Zhao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In recent years, agentic orchestration has been widely applied to solve complex human tasks. However, existing orchestration learning still faces key challenges, including hand-designed curricula, advantage collapse when a task's rollouts all reach the same outcome, and unreliable evaluation signals that judge each skill by one noisy score. To address these challenges, we propose R$^2$Orch, a new paradigm of Recursive Self-Play Orchestration: an inner loop of self-play that improves the orchestrator on self-chosen tasks, and an outer loop that improves how the inner loop improves. To support this paradigm, we introduce Recursive Learnability, a training objective that rewards a Proposer for choosing tasks whose rollouts keep reaching different outcomes as skills are withdrawn. Built on this objective, we further propose Skill Withdrawal, in which the outer loop withdraws each skill once a controlled evaluation within self-play shows that the orchestrator performs as well without it. Moreover, the two loops form one cycle: failures are distilled into skills, skills into parameters, and withdrawals lead the Proposer to new failures, so the orchestrator moves toward zero-skill deployment. Experimental results on twelve datasets show that R$^2$Orch consistently outperforms baselines across question answering, math, code, and interactive tasks. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Improving Environment-Agnostic Agents: The Role of Scaffolding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22289)**  `arXiv:2610.22289`  
  _Sergey Rodionov_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agents built on stronger language models may increasingly construct useful procedures for themselves, reducing the advantage of developer-supplied scaffolding. We investigate why this advantage persists and whether an environment-agnostic agent can be improved without environment-specific engineering. We evaluate a Codex-based agent in two nearly identical prompt variants on ARC-AGI-3, AGI Maze, and Terminal-Bench. Each variant is deployed with unchanged code, prompts, and general-purpose tools across environments, receiving environment-specific information as input. Changing only an instruction to encourage reporting difficulties and returning control for automatic continuation improves mean scores on both ARC-AGI-3 and AGI Maze, while Terminal-Bench shows small, mixed changes. Yet the general agent remains below an ARC-AGI-3-specific reference, including with GPT-5.6-sol. The reference prescribes organization and control rather than supplying new game rules or solutions. To investigate this gap, we transfer reset decisions to a scripted controller that follows a convention already given to the agent. This small change recovers part of the specialized agent's advantage. A complementary AGI Maze experiment shows that additional tutorial experience can reduce performance. Together, these findings highlight the distinction between having information and tools available and using them effectively. Supplied procedures still matter for the models tested here. The performance gains also come with higher token-based cost. The experiments do not separate better use of information and procedures from additional or differently allocated inference. We provide the EnvTraverse framework and an evaluated baseline agent for testing whether further environment-independent changes improve performance in some environments without degrading it in others.
  </details>

- **[Realtime-Bench: Can Frontier Models Solve Real-time Interactive Problems?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22155)**  `arXiv:2610.22155`  
  _Jingyu Tang, Xu Yang, Zhehao Zhang, Zheng Wang, Qi Cheng, Haozhe Zhao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-time interaction requires agents to perceive evolving environments and act under temporal constraints, yet existing evaluations of real-time streaming models focus on conversational quality and streaming perception, leaving effective action under strict temporal constraints untested. We introduce Realtime-Bench, a suite of carefully curated dynamic tasks where delayed actions directly affect success. Under Wall Clock, where environments advance according to actual inference latency, the best success rate reaches only 44% and the best Pass^3 is only 36%, demonstrating that real-time interaction remains challenging. To separate serving latency from models' real-time streaming capability, we compare Wall Clock with Paused Clock, which removes inference delays as an idealized reference, and introduce Token Clock, which advances simulated time according to generated token counts, testing temporal awareness independently of physical serving speed. We find that temporal costs limit success in evolving environments and that higher reasoning effort has mixed effects under time pressure, reflecting a trade-off between decision quality and timely execution. Based on these results, we discuss future directions for improving real-time multimodal models, including adaptive reasoning with temporal awareness and continuous perception and action.
  </details>

- **[CLIVO: Differentiable Cluster-Based Instruction Mixing with Learned Validation Objectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16876)**  `arXiv:2610.16876`  
  _Yuhan Wang, Rui Hu, Batian Di, Tan Jiang, Dongsheng Ma, Leichao Dong, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Data selection turns large multimodal instruction pools into compact training subsets tailored to a vision-language model's target capabilities. Effective selection requires both useful examples and an appropriate balance of supervision. Sample-level rankings can overrepresent similar content without optimizing this balance, while group-level methods are constrained by coarse allocation units, predefined validation sets, and costly or inaccurate mixture search. We propose CLIVO, a target-aware framework that connects fine-grained semantic allocation, automatically learned validation objectives, and differentiable mixture search. Specifically, semantic clusters define independently adjustable training shares, and merged cluster experts provide reusable proxies for evaluating mixtures. We select validation examples by correlating their proxy losses with real probe performance, then learn nonnegative weights to form a target-specific objective. With experts and validation weights fixed, a single backward pass supplies gradients for all shares, avoiding an additional mixture-response regression model. Selecting 100K instructions from an 8M pool, CLIVO attains 97.2% of the full-data four-target average score using only 1.25% of the data. At this budget, it outperforms the strongest evaluated selection baseline by 1.16 points with random within-cluster sampling; combining learned quotas with sample ranking increases this advantage to 1.55 points. CLIVO further supports interpretable, flexible, and reusable data curation through semantic visualization, modular selectors, and shared experts.
  </details>

- **[FreeEvolve: Learning to Evolve Beyond Fixed Loops](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09197)**  `arXiv:2610.09197`  
  _Lecheng Kong, Like Hui, Nikos Kanakaris, Prithwish Jana, Sahika Genc, Narayanan Sadagopan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent evolvers automate the design of the prompts, skills and workflows around language model agents, yet the optimization process they follow is still designed by hand: a fixed search loop decides how candidates are evaluated, which are kept and when the search stops. We propose FREEEVOLVE, which automates this process as well. An environment specifies the goal, target agent, evaluator, data and resource limits; within these limits, the evolver itself decides what to test, how much evidence to collect, which candidates to pursue and when to stop. These decisions follow an editable evolution skill, which we improve through meta-evolution by scoring each candidate skill on the fresh target agent it produces. The optimization process thus becomes a capability learned from experience rather than a loop engineered in advance. On tau3-bench, ARC-AGI-2, ARC-AGI-3 and Terminal-Bench 2.1, FREEEVOLVE controls the evolution campaign by itself, yet improves the primary held-out metric by 13.6 points on average and matches or exceeds hand-designed evolvers. The learned process keeps improving with experience: meta-evolved skills add 6.9 points over the seed skill on fresh target agents, demonstrating transferability across environments.
  </details>

- **[MemMux: Runtime Verification and Honest Resource Attribution for Fleets of Parallel Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07257)**  `arXiv:2610.07257`  
  _Sumanyu Muku_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Developers increasingly run a fleet of coding agents side by side on one workstation. Terminal multiplexers like tmux and a new generation of agent managers were built to arrange windows, not to govern memory. When ten agents each spawn language servers, test runners, and browsers, no standard tool can say how much memory belongs to which agent, confirm that a terminated agent's descendants are gone, notice a child that has escaped its agent, or keep the machine off the swap cliff when an out-of-memory (OOM) kill would silently discard uncommitted work. We treat these as runtime-verification problems: a hosting substrate should emit signals an operator can check while agents run. We present MemMux, an unprivileged local runtime (no kernel control groups) turning resource governance into checkable signals: per-agent attribution, complete reclamation, escaped-process visibility, bounded footprint under overcommit, and monitoring overhead. A claims-disciplined benchmark measures each against tmux and a raw-process baseline on identical workloads. Under a binding memory budget on a Linux host, MemMux holds the fleet under budget (7500 MiB) with zero swap by admitting a subset and reclaiming under pressure, while the ungoverned tools run every agent, pin the machine at its RAM ceiling (2x over budget), and spill about 2 GiB into swap. MemMux reclaims 100% of a terminated agent's process subtree where the raw baseline strands half of it (tmux matches us here; the raw baseline does not), and it is the only launcher we compare against that surfaces escaped children (10 of 10 detected). The cost: the always-on 1 Hz monitoring loop runs near 0.6% CPU at one agent but 2.7% at ten, above our 2% target. Running the harness on real Claude Code sessions shows 100% attribution and low overhead carry over to live agent trees. We release the engine, benchmark, and reproducer.
  </details>

- **[JusticeAxis: Benchmarking Legal Judgment between Rigid Rule Application and Ungrounded Discretion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00353)**  `arXiv:2610.00353`  
  _Zhengkai Tu, Mingda Zhang, Zijia Wang, Xiaoying Tang, Jimmy Huang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A sound judgment applies the law to established facts and weighs the circumstances in which they arose. However, existing methods swing between rigid statute matching and ungrounded discretion, benchmarks score a label or a rubric, and the experience that would supply the balance stays unverified. We formalize legal judgment as a reference-anchored task, whose object is a single decision that stays tied to the statute and to the circumstances at once. We introduce JusticeAxis, 256 real-world criminal cases from 18 countries with audio, image, and text evidence, and three lawyer-written judgments for every case: the recorded one and one for each failure. We further propose JusticeAgent, a harness whose element agents establish the facts and whose judge agent applies the law under skills carrying experience of the circumstances. Skills are distilled from execution trajectories and admitted only under Bayesian credible bounds. Experiments show that failure turns direction with scale: open-weight backbones drift to unsupported grounds, frontier models to the statutory default. We further verify that JusticeAgent, as a simple yet effective plugin, carries a frozen open-weight backbone to commercial level. Project resources are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EvoSteer: Online Self-Evolving Graph Orchestration via Reference-Anchored Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38661)**  `arXiv:2609.38661`  
  _Mingda Zhang, Hanwen Zhang, Qiang Huang, Zijia Wang, Pengfei Guo, Yuchen Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In recent years, LLM-based multi-agent systems have been widely applied to orchestrate tool-using agents into executable communication graphs. However, existing self-evolving orchestration still faces key challenges, including post-hoc evolution that revises the team only after the trajectory ends, credit diffusion that gives every action the same terminal advantage under confounded baselines, and skill admission that is uncalibrated and never retired. To address these challenges, we propose EvoSteer, a new paradigm of Online Self-Evolving Graph Orchestration -- the orchestrator builds a running team and repairs its plausible but failing steps from execution features and a learned value estimate. To support this paradigm, we introduce Anchored Trajectory Balance (AnchorTB), a regression-style flow-matching loss that assigns each orchestration action a coefficient by balancing subtrajectories against a frozen reference. Built on the learned flow, we further propose Validated Skill Admission, in which a candidate skill is tried before promotion and promoted only if paired evidence passes a sequential test under a shared nominal testing budget. Moreover, AnchorTB combines measured task-level reference reward statistics with prefix-dependent corrections. Experimental results on twelve datasets show that EvoSteer significantly outperforms baselines across question answering, mathematical reasoning, code generation, and interactive decision making. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[R$^2$ Flow: Recursive Self-Improvement via Recursive Skill Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33867)**  `arXiv:2609.33867`  
  _Mingda Zhang, Qiang Huang, Yanjin Li, Zijia Wang, Qika Lin, Xiaoying Tang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based agents can improve themselves across tasks by reusing and revising the skills they orchestrate into executable procedures. Flow-based training fits this loop: it samples procedures in proportion to reward, and the flow through each skill credits it for the next library revision. Three obstacles stand in the way of making this self-improvement reliable: flow training suffers strategy collapse over tree-structured histories; nonnegative flow-based credit rewards frequent use as if it were benefit; and library edits rest on the task reward the policy optimizes. We introduce R$^2$ Flow, a recursive self-improvement framework that alternates policy learning, independent verification, and versioned skill-library updates on a shared-state orchestration graph. The graph merges histories that differ only in the order of independent steps, allowing flow training to pool evidence across equivalent executions. A flow-share readout of the trained flow, invariant to the backward policy, and a separate signed utility rank which skills to change, verifier evidence decides whether an edit is warranted, and a residual-variance plateau sets when to update. Committed edits reshape the graph the next policy learns on, realizing recursive skill evolution. Across question answering, mathematical reasoning, interactive decision making, and code generation, R$^2$ Flow improves task accuracy and library-edit precision over heuristic orchestration, reinforcement learning, and skill-evolution baselines, and transfers across executors. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ChatT2: An Adaptive Framework for Developing a Large Language Model-Based Agent for Natural Product Domain Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25620)**  `arXiv:2609.25620`  
  _Yihan Wang, Qiandi Gao, Yihui Zhuang, Liangjun Ge, Heqian Zhang, Jiaquan Huang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific investigations into microbial natural products (NPs) present significant challenges for novices, largely due to the complexity of microbial systems, biochemical diversity, technical skill requirements, and the demands of bioinformatics and data analysis processes. To address these issues, we introduce ChatT2, a large language model (LLM)-based agent that is specifically tailored to the unique characteristics of bacterial type II polyketides. These polyketides form a structurally distinct and therapeutically important NP family. ChatT2 was developed within an autonomous multiagent framework composed of a mentor, an executor, and an evaluator, each with defined responsibilities. The mentor acts as an intermediary between ChatT2 and the user, utilizing chain-of-thought prompting to refine the intent of the user. Under the guidance of the mentor, the executor synthesizes multimodal information via retrieval-augmented generation techniques and seamlessly integrates bioinformatics and cheminformatics tools. The evaluator ultimately assesses the output of the executor to ensure the richness and accuracy of the retrieved information. Our research highlights how ChatT2, designed with this multiagent framework, addresses the challenges faced by general LLMs in terms of understanding limited, specialized corpora and complex biological information and provides both experts and novices with a valuable tool for exploring various NPs of interest. The ChatT2 webserver can be accessed atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Kernel-Managed Shared Memory for System-Wide Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10144)**  `arXiv:2609.10144`  
  _Ryan Lum, Yongfeng Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI systems become more useful when they can adapt to the people using them, but in multi-agent systems, useful context learned by one agent often remains unavailable to others. We present kernel-managed shared memory, a system-level abstraction in which specialized agents write structured, tagged memories while the agent-system kernel, not individual agents, governs retrieval, privacy enforcement, and prompt injection. We implement and evaluate this design on AIOS and compare it against three alternatives across three assistant models (GPT-4o, Llama-3.1:8B, Qwen-2.5:7B) and 1,800 total trials. Against an unmanaged external memory backend (Mem0) using identical underlying storage, kernel-managed retrieval and injection improve personalization scores by 2.4-4.0 points on a 5-point scale (e.g., 1.05 to 4.69 profile usage on GPT-4o), with every comparison significant at p < 10^-18. Against standard retrieval-augmented injection, gains are similarly large and consistent across all three models. Against full, unfiltered context concatenation, a soft ceiling on available context rather than on response quality, kernel-managed injection statistically matches performance on two of three models and shows a small, model-specific deficit on the third, while using substantially shorter prompts: end-to-end latency is 15-61% lower across all three models, with corresponding reductions in per-call token usage and inference cost. These results indicate that centralizing memory management in the agent-system kernel, rather than leaving retrieval and privacy enforcement to individual agents, delivers most of the personalization benefit of unconstrained context at a fraction of its cost.
  </details>

- **[LEBGen: An LLM-Enhanced Bayesian Network Framework for Few-Shot Travel Survey Data Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08288)**  `arXiv:2609.08288`  
  _Zijian Shen, Bin Zhou, Jiguang Wang, Ya Zhao, Jintao Ke_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Travel survey data are essential for transportation planning and travel behavior analysis, yet collecting large-scale representative samples is costly and time-consuming. A practical alternative is to generate synthetic survey records from a few-shot sample. However, such samples provide incomplete coverage of heterogeneous traveler groups and insufficient evidence for recovering the complex dependencies between demographic characteristics and travel behavior. Existing approaches have complementary limitations. Probabilistic generative models such as Bayesian networks (BNs) offer explicit distributional control, but structures learned from few-shot samples may omit meaningful dependencies or retain spurious ones. Large language models (LLMs) can help address these difficulties in BN structure learning by providing behavioral knowledge that complements the limited statistical evidence. We therefore propose LEBGen, an LLM-enhanced BN framework that uses this knowledge to refine network structure for few-shot travel survey data generation. Specifically, the LLM first identifies traveler personas from demographic attribute and travel behavior statistics, then recovers dependencies missed by the persona-augmented BN structure and prune spurious ones. The refined BN is parameterized exclusively from the observed data to generate synthetic records. Under a 2% few-shot setting on the 2022 Hong Kong Travel Characteristics Survey, LEBGen reduces the mean marginal Jensen-Shannon divergence from 0.0671 to 0.0091 and the mean absolute Cramer's V error by 14.3% over the best-performing baseline, substantially improving both distributional and dependency fidelity.
  </details>

- **[Interface-Induced Trajectory Censoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03966)**  `arXiv:2609.03966`  
  _Wenbo Wang, Di Sang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-use evaluations typically measure calls through a serving stack, which can report zero calls even when model outputs contain call-shaped payloads. We study interface-induced trajectory censoring: a mismatch between model emissions and the interface that converts them into actions. On selected BFCL v4 subsets, changing only the serving adapter while holding model weights and other evaluation settings fixed moves scores under the official scorer from 0.00 to 0.96 on simple_python and from 0.00 to 0.19 on multi_turn_base. A template-parser factorial experiment shows strong complementarity: neither component replacement alone restores parsing from the baseline, whereas their joint replacement recovers 196/200 first-request parses. Across five Qwen2.5-Coder checkpoints, classifier-positive emissions increase with model size while the mismatched interface reports no calls; a separate matched-interface Qwen3 ladder exhibits few silent emissions through 8B. Experiments on 115 tau-bench retail tasks extend the mechanism to a stateful, non-code environment. In a preregistered, single-seed, 150-step 7B LoRA experiment, changing only the parser registered in verl increases tool executions from 10 to 16,844. However, neither a shared programmatic-feedback evaluation nor a post-hoc common native function-calling evaluation detects a held-out success gain from repair; rescue counts do not increase. Interface repair thus restores tool-mediated experience without a detected performance gain in this setting. These findings show that tool-use measurements depend on the model-interface configuration and motivate layer-wise observability and conformance checks before attributing evaluation or training outcomes to the model alone.
  </details>

- **[TRCA: Transition-wise Rubric Credit Assignment for Long-horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16156)**  `arXiv:2608.16156`  
  _Huan Zhang, Mingju Chen, Dongxu Zhou, Can Lv, Heng Chang, Sen Cui, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon large language model (LLM) agents are typically optimized with sparse terminal outcomes, making fine-grained credit assignment across multi-step interactions difficult. Existing approaches either rely on process evaluators, which incur annotation and inference costs, or derive step-level credit from successful trajectories. However, successful trajectories are extremely scarce during early-stage reinforcement learning, substantially weakening anchor-based methods. We propose Transition-wise Rubric Credit Assignment (TRCA), which derives step-level supervision directly from action-induced transitions without learned evaluators or successful anchors. TRCA evaluates each transition using Evidence, Execution, and Invalidity rubrics to capture task-relevant information acquisition, valid task execution, and invalid or regressive behavior. From these judgments, Foundational Rubric Reward measures local transition quality, while Breakthrough Rubric Reward tracks newly covered Evidence and Execution conditions to reward incremental task progress. Combined with terminal outcomes, these signals produce fine-grained step-level advantages for policy optimization. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and seven search-augmented question-answering benchmarks show consistent improvements over the evaluated baselines. With Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, TRCA improves the WebShop score by 6.0%-12.6%; with Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, it improves the average SearchQA score by 1.9%-18.3%. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of transition-wise rubric credit assignment for long-horizon tasks with sparse successful anchors.
  </details>

- **[D2R2: Discrete Diffusion with Regulation Reinforcement for Single-Cell Perturbation Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15288)**  `arXiv:2608.15288`  
  _Ninghan Fan, Qi Liu, Xunuo Zhu, Yukai Sun, Luyuan Chen, Xuheng Zhou, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predicting single-cell transcriptomic responses to genetic perturbations is central to functional genomics and virtual-cell modeling. Existing approaches, however, typically predict an entire expression profile as a whole, leaving the order in which individual gene responses are generated unmodeled. To address this problem, we introduce \textbf{$D^{2}R^{2}$} (\textbf{D}iscrete \textbf{D}iffusion with \textbf{R}egulation \textbf{R}einforcement), which reformulates perturbation prediction as regulation-guided gene-wise progressive generation. A Masked Discrete Diffusion Model represents expression as ordinal tokens and reconstructs a fully masked profile step by step, allowing generated gene responses to condition those that remain masked. A Regulatory Policy Module initializes the generation policy from a gene regulatory network inferred from control cells and adapts it to the perturbation and current partially generated state. Then, group-relative policy optimization refines only the ordering policy using final perturbation-effect agreement as reward. Across Norman19 and VCC-H1, $D^{2}R^{2}$ achieves the best performance on all five metrics on Norman19 and remains competitive on H1. Controlled ablations holding the generator and generation budget fixed show that biological-prior ordering improves over random ordering and is more reliable than uncertainty-based heuristics, whereas reversing the biological-prior ordering degrades every metric. Biological analyses further show that the refined policy prioritizes regulatory genes early while promoting perturbation-specific transcription factors and responsive genes. These results establish gene generation order as an effective, controllable, and biologically interpretable dimension of single-cell perturbation prediction.
  </details>

- **[From Faulty Memories to Corrected Actions: Dependency-Guided Rollback Repair for Memory-Augmented Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10502)**  `arXiv:2608.10502`  
  _Yiqi Wang, Caili Yu, Jiaqi Zhang, Yiqun Duan, Mingkai Zheng, Zhangkai Wu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Persistent memory lets language model agents reuse information across sessions, but it also makes errors durable: a poisoned, stale, or misattributed record can alter reasoning, tool use, answers, and subsequent memory writes. Existing defenses mainly detect or delete suspicious memories, or revise the current response. Deleting the source leaves already propagated claims, actions, and derived memories active, whereas resetting the store or replaying the full trace destroys benign state and repeats unnecessary computation. We therefore formulate post-failure memory recovery: given a failed execution and diagnosed faulty memories, regenerate the answer and clean up affected persistent state while retaining unaffected work. Our dependency-guided rollback repair builds a typed memory-to-action graph from runtime provenance, traces explicit downstream dependencies, preserves candidates with independent trusted support, deactivates unsupported memory state, and selectively replays only answer-relevant affected computation. We evaluate this approach on a 150-case controlled benchmark spanning three tool-use domains and four memory failure types, and on a 50-case trajectory-derived stress test adapted from LongMemEval-V2. On the controlled benchmark, it achieves 85.3% immediate recovery versus 77.3% for LLM-judge repair, the strongest baseline, while preserving all benign memories and using 41.8% fewer LLM calls. Selective replay, however, leaves higher recurrence than LLM-judge repair (26.6% vs. 12.1%), exposing a trade-off between targeted repair and broader recomputation. On the adapted subset, it reaches 68.0% recovery versus 54.0% for the next best method, with 5.9% recurrence. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>
