Building on this insight, our approach enhances image diffusion models by improving cross-view consistency. The pipeline comprises three key stages: first, we generate geometrically consistent sparse views using Cascaded Outpainting ControlNets; second, we propagate denser intermediate views via a component dubbed AGInpaint; and third, we globally eliminate visual inconsistencies (e.g., varying exposure) using the GCAlign module. Concurrently with generation, a 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) scene is reconstructed by initializing Gaussian balls on the mesh surface. Our method outperforms existing approaches in both geometric alignment and generation quality. Once synthesized, the scene can be rendered in diverse styles through relighting and style transfer techniques. project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[ArtSeek: Deep artwork understanding via multimodal in-context reasoning and late interaction retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.21917)**  `arXiv:2507.21917`  
  _Nicola Fanelli, Gennaro Vessio, Giovanna Castellano_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Analyzing digitized artworks requires both visual interpretation and deep artistic, contextual, and historical knowledge. We introduce ArtSeek, a multimodal framework for art analysis that combines multimodal large language models with retrieval-augmented generation. Unlike prior work, our pipeline relies only on image input, so it applies to artworks without links to Wikidata or Wikipedia, common in most digitized collections. ArtSeek integrates three components: a late-interaction multimodal retrieval module, a contrastive multitask network predicting artist, genre, style, media, and tags, and an agentic reasoning strategy enabled through in-context examples. Central to this approach is WikiFragments, a dataset of image-text fragments extracted from the entire English Wikipedia to support knowledge-grounded multimodal reasoning. Our multimodal query encoding raises retrieval NDCG@5 from 33.9 to 44.9 over encoding the full image-text query, and fixed-budget token pooling keeps late-interaction search practical over millions of fragments at under 7% of the cost of full multi-vector retrieval. ArtSeek sets a new state of the art in artwork attribute classification, with +8.4% F1 in style classification over GraphCLIP, and gains +7.1 BLEU@1 on ArtPedia captioning. On five knowledge-grounded question answering benchmarks graded by an LLM judge from a different model family, retrieval helps where the answer is documented in our index or lies inside the classifier's label space (+0.19 mean correctness on two of them) and is neutral where it is not; what separates the two regimes is how much of a benchmark our knowledge base covers. The gain transfers to two non-Qwen backbones (+0.22 to +0.28). A blind human study and a qualitative analysis of successes and failures corroborate this picture and expose where retrieval still misleads. Data and code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Automatic Fine-grained Segmentation-assisted Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16623)**  `arXiv:2507.16623`  
  _Frederic Jonske, Constantin Seibold, Osman Alperen Koras, Fin Bahnsen, Marie Bauer, Amin Dada, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable end-to-end clinical report generation has been a longstanding goal of medical ML research. The end goal for this process is to alleviate radiologists' workloads and provide second opinions to clinicians or patients. Thus, a necessary prerequisite for report generation models is a strong general performance and some type of innate grounding capability, to convince clinicians or patients of the veracity of the generated reports. In this paper, we present ASaRG (\textbf{A}utomatic \textbf{S}egmentation-\textbf{a}ssisted \textbf{R}eport \textbf{G}eneration), an extension of the popular LLaVA architecture that aims to tackle both of these problems. ASaRG proposes to fuse intermediate features and fine-grained segmentation maps created by specialist radiological models into LLaVA's multi-modal projection layer via simple concatenation. With a small number of added parameters, our approach achieves a +0.89\% performance gain ($p=0.012$) in CE F1 score compared to the LLaVA baseline when using only intermediate features, and +2.77\% performance gain ($p<0.001$) when adding a combination of intermediate features and fine-grained segmentation maps. Compared with COMG and ORID, two other report generation methods that utilize segmentations, the performance gain amounts to 6.98\% and 6.28\% in F1 score, respectively. ASaRG is not mutually exclusive with other changes made to the LLaVA architecture, potentially allowing our method to be combined with other advances in the field. Finally, the use of an arbitrary number of segmentations as part of the input demonstrably allows tracing elements of the report to the corresponding segmentation maps and verifying the groundedness of assessments. Our code will be made publicly available at a later date.
  </details>

- **[Few-shot Classification as Multi-instance Verification: Effective Backbone-agnostic Transfer across Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.00401)**  `arXiv:2507.00401`  
  _Xin Xu, Eibe Frank, Geoffrey Holmes_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We investigate cross-domain few-shot learning under the constraint that fine-tuning of backbones (i.e., feature extractors) is impossible or infeasible---a scenario that is increasingly common in practical use cases---and show how representing few-shot classification as a series of multiple instance verification (MIV) tasks enables competitive classification performance using the static embeddings produced by frozen, "black-box" backbones. More specifically, based on this novel representation, we introduce a new approach to few-shot domain adaptation named the "MIV-head": a classification head that is backbone-agnostic and computationally efficient. We show that the core components of the MIV-head, when trained on few-shot data from a target domain, collectively yield strong performance on test data from that domain. Importantly, they do so without fine-tuning the backbone, requiring only the "meta-testing" phase. In experiments under various settings on an extension of the Meta-Dataset benchmark for cross-domain few-shot image classification, using representative off-the-shelf convolutional neural network and vision transformer backbones pretrained on ImageNet1K, we demonstrate that the MIV-head achieves highly competitive accuracy when compared to state-of-the-art "adapter" (or "partial fine-tuning") methods applied to the same backbones, while incurring substantially lower adaptation cost. Moreover, compared to our approach, well-known "classification head" approaches achieve a substantially lower level of accuracy. We share our code at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[AutoExpert: Automating 3D LiDAR Annotation from Expert-Crafted Guidelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02914)**  `arXiv:2506.02914`  
  _Yechi Ma, Wei Hua, Shu Kong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The contemporary paradigm of scaling data annotation, crucial for developing machine learning solutions, is to hire ordinary human annotators and instruct them with expert-crafted guidelines to label data. This paradigm is laborious, tedious, and costly, motivating us to study an open problem, auto-annotation with expert-crafted guidelines (dubbed AutoExpert). We develop benchmarks by redesigning the evaluation protocol and re-annotating data with nuScenes and PandaSet, two 3D detection datasets for autonomous driving research that provide expert-crafted annotation guidelines. Their guidelines define 18 and 25 object classes, respectively, using nuanced language descriptions and a few visual examples. Following the guidelines that require using 3D cuboids to label LiDAR data, AutoExpert requires algorithms to learn on few-shot labeled images and texts to perform the task of 3D detection on LiDAR data. Apparently, the challenges of AutoExpert lie in the data-modality and task discrepancy. Nevertheless, public foundation models (FMs) serve as promising tools to tackle these challenges. To address AutoExpert, we adopt a conceptually simple pipeline consisting of three components: (1) 2D object detection and segmentation in RGB images, (2) lifting 2D detections into 3D using known sensor poses, and (3) 3D cuboids generation for the 2D detections. Within this pipeline, we enhance and evaluate a variety of methods such as open-vocabulary detectors, few-shot detectors, and self-supervised learned detectors. We also develop novel techniques, leading to refined components that boost 3D detection mAP from 12.1 to 25.4 on the AutoExpert-nuScenes benchmark.
  </details>

- **[DetoxAI: a Python Toolkit for Debiasing Deep Learning Models in Computer Vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05492)**  `arXiv:2505.05492`  
  _Ignacy Stępka, Lukasz Sztukiewicz, Michał Wiliński, Jerzy Stefanowski_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While machine learning fairness has made significant progress in recent years, most existing solutions focus on tabular data and are poorly suited for vision-based classification tasks, which rely heavily on deep learning. To bridge this gap, we introduce DetoxAI, an open-source Python library for improving fairness in deep learning vision classifiers through post-hoc debiasing. DetoxAI implements state-of-the-art debiasing algorithms, fairness metrics, and visualization tools. It supports debiasing via interventions in internal representations and includes attribution-based visualization tools and quantitative algorithmic fairness metrics to show how bias is mitigated. This paper presents the motivation, design, and use cases of DetoxAI, demonstrating its tangible value to engineers and researchers.
  </details>

- **[METTA: Memory-Enhanced Test-Time Adaptation for Meta-Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31656)**  `arXiv:2610.31656`  
  _Ali Najar, Hosein Hasani, Amir Kooshan Fattah, Yasamin Rajabi, Mahdieh Soleymani Baghshah_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Meta-reinforcement learning aims to train agents that adapt quickly to unseen tasks from limited interaction, but this requires representing task evidence over long interaction histories. Classical recurrent agents compress experience into hidden states, while transformer-based agents retrieve from past trajectories at quadratic memory and computation cost. In this work, we propose Memory-Enhanced Test-Time Adaptation (METTA) for meta-reinforcement learning. It decomposes the fast adaptation phase into two temporal scales: a causal neural memory performs fine-grained within-episode adaptation with linear sequence complexity, while compact episode-level summaries support long-range cross-episode task inference without attending over the full interaction history. Moreover, equipping agents with neural memory provides sufficient expressive capacity and test-time training capability, enabling effective adaptation to unseen held-out tasks and task families at meta-test time. On unseen held-out Meta-World ML10/45 test environments, METTA achieves a success rate over $50\%$ while maintaining a low training budget and short wall-clock time. On long-context sparse-reward PointMaze tasks, it outperforms transformer-based baselines by exhibiting more structured exploration behavior. These results show that METTA provides an efficient path toward more adaptive and scalable long-context meta-RL.
  </details>

- **[A Fix for Softmax and Cross-Entropy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31642)**  `arXiv:2610.31642`  
  _Jonathan T. Barron, Dan Gnanapragasam, Michael Tschannen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Standard implementations of softmax, log-softmax, and cross-entropy have two numerical bugs. The first bug, Softmax Collapse, rounds the target logit's gradient to zero if that one logit is much larger than the others, resulting in gradients that no longer sum to zero. The second bug, Softmax Cancellation, occurs when student logits are close to teacher logits, and produces non-zero gradients even when the student and teacher logits are equal. We present simple implementations of log-softmax, softmax, and cross-entropy that fix both bugs. Our fix eliminates the previously-observed "Slingshot" phenomenon, speeds up grokking with weight decay, prevents accuracy collapse without weight decay, stops student networks from drifting away from the teacher during distillation, and shows that after memorization, logit growth is bounded by an equilibrium between weight decay and Adam's $\epsilon$ hyperparameter.
  </details>

- **[Causal Chain of Thought: Mitigating Attention Leakage via Context Isolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31639)**  `arXiv:2610.31639`  
  _Gabriele Dominici, Teague McMillan, Fazl Barez, Marc Langheinrich, Martin Gjoreski_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting elicits step-by-step rationales that appear to explain model predictions, but there is no guarantee these steps causally drive the final answer. In standard autoregressive generation, dense attention allows each reasoning step to attend to the full context, enabling the model to derive answers through direct lookup while generating plausible-sounding but post-hoc justifications. We introduce CausalCoT, an inference framework that enforces causal transparency by decomposing problems into a directed acyclic graph of atomic sub-questions, evaluating each node in an isolated context containing only its direct causal parents. This Markovian structure removes attention pathways to non-parent content, making downstream predictions sensitive to local reasoning rather than global context leakage. Across a diverse suite of reasoning benchmarks, CausalCoT preserves competitive predictive performance while improving explanation faithfulness, increasing causal sensitivity of intermediate reasoning steps by an average of 12 and up to 21 percentage points over the strongest baseline. The structured dependency graph additionally enables test-time interventions in which local corrections propagate predictably through downstream reasoning.
  </details>

- **[BeNice! Closing Looping's Inference-Memory Gap via KV-Sharing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31633)**  `arXiv:2610.31633`  
  _Hayden Prairie, Jacob M. Ryan, Taylor Berg-Kirkpatrick, Daniel Y. Fu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped architectures scale quality while keeping parameter count fixed by repeatedly passing activations through the same block of layers. During inference, each recurrence generates independent KV-caches, causing memory to grow linearly. To address this challenge, prior work has proposed KV-sharing (i.e., sharing KV-caches across depth), finding that, in some cases, the KV-cache of the final recurrence can be shared with the earlier recurrences without quality loss, a property we denote as KV-shareability. However, the mechanisms underlying KV-shareability remain poorly understood, with even identically trained looped architectures not consistently exhibiting it. In this paper, we study how KV-shareability can be enabled in looped models. We propose a two-stage method: in the first stage, we use stochastic depth during pretraining, which induces contractive residual dynamics that drive representations toward a common limit across recurrences. In the second stage, we propose BeNice!, a post-training method that broadcasts the final recurrence's KV-cache into the first recurrence, training it to produce compatible residuals while preserving the original model's quality. As a result, BeNice! enables KV-shareability while matching standard finetuning's unshared validation perplexity. Using BeNice!, we show that KV-sharing is complementary to alternative KV-compression methods, composing with memory-efficient sequence mixers (e.g., multi-latent attention, Kimi delta attention) and low-precision KV-cache quantization (e.g., fp8, mxfp8, nvfp4). Finally, in simulated low-memory, long-context inference, KV-sharing improves cache hit rates by up to 31.4%, increases throughput by up to 23.6%, and reduces p90 time-to-first-token by up to 83.6%.
  </details>

- **[Questioning Instrumental Convergence in RLVR: Checkpoint Evidence from OLMo-3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31624)**  `arXiv:2610.31624`  
  _Barış Alparslan, Sandy Tanwisuth, Caleb Petterson_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Instrumental convergence is often invoked as a general reason to expect capable agents to acquire or express power-seeking subgoals: self-preservation, resource acquisition, deception, or resistance to oversight which poses an important threat to the society. For LLMs, reinforcement learning is the first stage of post-training that directly optimizes behavior against an objective; therefore, it is the stage where convergent instrumental behavior, on IC grounds, likely to appear. Does reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) make a model more likely to express instrumental-convergence behavior, or does this depend on the training regime, evaluation frame, and stage of post-training? We study this question as a checkpoint-conditioned behavioral trajectory rather than as a single before/after safety score. Using InstrumentalEval, we evaluate OLMo-3-7B-Think across SFT, DPO, 13 intermediate RLVR checkpoints, and the final RLVR model. The trajectory is nonmonotonic: instrumental rate rises from 25.0% at SFT to 46.1% near RLVR step 200, then falls to 11.8% at the final checkpoint. Paired item-level tests and step-wise regression support the final SFT-to-RLVR reduction. Prompt framing does not explain this pattern: a harmful-prompt indicator combined with other framing effects explains only 15% of within-checkpoint score variance on average, and Iterative Null-space Projection (INLP) removes a detectable framing direction without changing the SFT-to-final-RLVR gap. Together with preliminary 32B endpoint results (35.53% SFT to 22.37% RLVR final), these findings indicate that RLVR does not monotonically increase instrumental behavior in this setting: the rate rises early, then falls below the SFT baseline. The result is specific to this model family and benchmark, and judge agreement is moderate, so absolute rates should be read cautiously.
  </details>

- **[CRANE: Post-Hoc Counterfactual Retrieval for Multimodal Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31594)**  `arXiv:2610.31594`  
  _Franco Rugolon, Ioanna Miliou, Panagiotis Papapetrou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Counterfactual explanation methods have been introduced for unimodal settings, with dedicated frameworks for tabular, timeseries, text, and image data, yet no post-hoc method has been designed for classifiers that learn from multimodal inputs. Existing explainability methods for multimodal classifiers apply modality-specific attribution independently to each input, which fails to yield coherent explanations and does not transfer to arbitrary pre-trained multimodal architectures. To address these shortcomings, we present CRANE (Counterfactual Retrieval via Agnostic Nearest-neighbour Explanations), the first post-hoc, model-agnostic framework for nearest-neighbour multimodal counterfactual generation that retrieves real training examples from the target class as candidate explanations, ensuring plausibility by construction. Across four heterogeneous datasets and three fusion strategies, we show that Multimodal Consensus Retrieval (MC-R), which intersects per-modality neighbour sets to identify instances jointly close to the query in every modality, emerges as the strongest multimodal strategy, achieving the best or joint-best outcome on three of four datasets while grounding every explanation in the joint training distribution. Unimodal strategies remain competitive when a single modality dominates the decision boundary.
  </details>

- **[Action Learning for Scientific Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31585)**  `arXiv:2610.31585`  
  _Sarah Liaw, Yuanqi Du, Yilun Du_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding and controlling how systems evolve is central to science and engineering, where many problems require reasoning about complete trajectories. Existing approaches typically learn local dynamics or generate trajectories, but objectives constructed from individual transitions can miss dependencies across time, while generative models do not necessarily provide a tractable objective for evaluating and optimizing arbitrary paths. We study $\textit{action learning}$, the problem of recovering a stochastic action from trajectory observations, yielding a common objective for reasoning about dynamical systems. We introduce $\textbf{LEAP}$ ($\textbf{L}$earned $\textbf{E}$nergy on $\textbf{A}$ction $\textbf{P}$aths), which anchors the unknown action to a tractable reference process and learns the remaining correction by comparing observed trajectories with reference trajectories. This construction yields a differentiable objective on path space that can capture both local dynamical structure and nonadditive dependencies across time. Once learned, the same action can be flexibly reused for path ranking and inference, mechanism steering, constrained optimization and sampling. Across stochastic physical systems, many-body orbital dynamics and molecular simulations, LEAP recovers underlying dynamics, captures dependencies across time and enables transfer across inference objectives and physical conditions. We show that $\textit{action learning}$ is a promising framework for turning trajectory observations into reusable objectives for scientific inference and control.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Sequence Compression with Test-time Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31574)**  `arXiv:2610.31574`  
  _Sihan Yang, Jie Huang, Hanxuan Li, Yifan Guo, Weigao Sun, Yiran Zhong, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sequence compression is a fundamental operation and a critical information bottleneck in efficient language and multimodal models, and yet prevailing approaches based on pooling or learned projections remain underperforming. We revisit sequence compression through the lens of test-time regression: input tokens define a regression problem whose fitted coefficients form the compressed representation. Importantly, the regression problem is learned end-to-end via backpropagation, while its coefficients are computed efficiently in closed form during the forward pass. This leads to TTR-Compressor, a general alternative to conventional pooling and projection-based compression. We evaluate TTR-Compressor as the compressor for block-wise sparse attention and the visual token merger for multimodal large language models. On block-wise sparse attention, TTR-Compressor improves RULER performance over attention-based compressors by a large margin. For visual token merging, it consistently outperforms the standard MLP merger and matches its performance with 55.6% fewer visual tokens. These results establish test-time regression as an effective primitive for sequence compression in efficient language and multimodal models.
  </details>

- **[On the Token Collapse Phenomenon in Textual Graph Soft Prompting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31572)**  `arXiv:2610.31572`  
  _Andrea Giuseppe Di Francesco, Rishabh Jain, Franco Maria Nardini, Nicola Tonellotto, Pietro Liò, Fabrizio Silvestri_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent methods for injecting graph structure into LLM prompts include graph soft prompting, in which a graph neural network (GNN) learns graph-specific tokens that capture relational information among entities in textual prompts. G-Retriever combines graph soft prompts with a verbalized description of the same graph to strengthen the textual prompt signal. We conduct an in-depth analysis of G-Retriever and uncover a pathological behavior we term token collapse. Across three benchmarks, three graph encoders, two LLM families, and both frozen and LoRA-adapted models, G-Retriever's graph tokens become almost indistinguishable across inputs. We provide evidence that the same failure is inherited by subsequent methods, even with multi-token pooling. Under the standard setup, swapping a token with the dataset mean, a token from another example, or one derived from a corrupted graph produces no resolved answer-level effect in accuracy. Moreover, a learned constant prompt that never accesses the graph matches or surpasses G-Retriever on all three benchmarks. These findings indicate that, rather than conveying graph content as intended, the collapsed token functions primarily as a generic soft prompt. We also examine conditions linked to this collapse and propose a two-stage procedure that yields non-collapsed tokens while maintaining competitive performance in most cases. However, token variability alone does not prove that the model uses graph information: via interventions and decoding probes, we show that depending on the configuration, a token can reflect the question, an answer prior, or graph-derived content that the LLM may or may not use. Overall, our results offer a practical approach to evaluating graph-token alignment rather than inferring it from task accuracy alone. Code to reproduce our experiments is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Fine-Grained Analysis of Catastrophic Forgetting in Continual Learning via Algorithmic Stability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31551)**  `arXiv:2610.31551`  
  _Guodong Zheng, Yan Sun, Peng Wang, Enneng Yang, Jiafei Lyu, Xiaochun Cao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding catastrophic forgetting, in which learning new tasks degrades performance on previously learned tasks, remains a fundamental challenge in continual learning (CL). Existing theoretical analyses often rely on specific model or task structures and offer limited insight into how iterative optimization affects forgetting. To address this gap, we develop an algorithmic stability framework for expected population forgetting under SGD without prescribing a particular model architecture. Specifically, we decompose forgetting into intra-task and cross-task generalization gaps and empirical forgetting. To bound the generalization gaps, we track how a sample perturbation affects updates within a task and propagates through subsequent tasks. We consider constant, intra-task decaying, and continuously decaying learning-rate schedules. Comparing the two decaying schedules allows us to characterize how restarting learning-rate decay at task boundaries affects the propagation of historical sample perturbations. For empirical forgetting, we derive bounds for all three schedules by retaining cumulative gradient alignment along the optimization trajectory, without requiring convexity. Combining these results yields upper bounds on expected forgetting that capture the effects of training set size, the number of updates per task, the total number of tasks, learning-rate schedules, and cumulative gradient alignment. Finally, we conduct experiments on Split CIFAR-100 and Split MiniImageNet to examine the empirical implications of our theoretical findings.
  </details>

- **[Shift-aware score transport for privacy auditing without retraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31549)**  `arXiv:2610.31549`  
  _Yaohong Yang, Gauri Pradhan, Qi Chen, Samuel Kaski, Antti Honkela_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Privacy auditing empirically assesses privacy leakage by using membership inference attacks to establish lower bounds on privacy loss. We consider an observational auditing setting in which the auditor cannot insert canaries, control data inclusion, or retrain the target model, and must instead rely on fixed sets of known members and non-members. This setting is relevant for large deployed models or when retraining is restricted by computational or access constraints. In this case, non-members available for auditing may differ in distribution from the training data, causing membership-inference scores to be skewed due to the distribution shift. We address this problem by estimating the counterfactual in-distribution non-member score distribution using the available out-of-distribution (OOD) non-members. Our proposed method learns the relationship between data representations and membership-inference scores from OOD non-members and transports it to the target covariate distribution. Because this transport may become unreliable under extrapolation or imperfect conditional invariance, we further introduce a conservative correction calibrated directly to the nominal transported privacy level. We construct controlled calibration tasks from the available non-member data to estimate how representation-space displacement inflates the audit, and use this estimate to correct the transport-induced audit error. Across multiple datasets with distribution shifts, our method yields substantially more stable privacy estimates while retaining tighter lower bounds than baselines.
  </details>

- **[Same Luck, Different Choice: Twin-World Credit Assignment for Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31544)**  `arXiv:2610.31544`  
  _Zeropy Surio, Tobias Engel, Mei-Lin Chou, Anika Raman, Samuel Okafor_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic reinforcement learning rewards a complete episode, yet the policy needs credit for every decision inside it. Counterfactual branching estimates this credit by re-running the episode after an alternative decision, but the branch draws its sampling noise independently of the factual episode, so the outcome difference mixes the effect of the decision with the luck of everything that follows. We observe that in agentic search the retriever, the corpus, and the grader are deterministic, so an episode is a deterministic function of the policy's decisions and its sampling noise. Twin worlds are therefore realizable: the same noise, a different decision. We introduce TWICE (TWIn-world Credit Estimation), which keys the Gumbel noise of every token by turn and line, rolls out counterfactual twins that inherit the factual episode's downstream noise, and turns their paired outcome differences into turn-level advantages for policy-gradient updates on actor-sampled trajectories; only the actor is deployed. Twin credit is unbiased for any coupling quality, its variance is that of an independent counterfactual scaled by $1-\rho$, where $\rho$ is the twins' outcome correlation, and under coupling the number of rollouts needed to resolve an advantage of size $\Delta$ falls from $\Theta(\Delta^{-2})$ to $\Theta(\Delta^{-1})$. A square-root rule spends the twin budget where outcomes are predicted to disagree per unit cost. On seven question-answering benchmarks, TWICE improves the average exact match of GRPO by 5.3 and 4.5 points on Qwen2.5-3B and 7B, outperforms tree-search and counterfactual-branching baselines, and uses 68% fewer auxiliary tokens than VinePPO.
  </details>

- **[PYTHIA, a transferable machine learning framework for simultaneous defect and adsorption site optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31539)**  `arXiv:2610.31539`  
  _Ioannis Kouroudis, Zhaosu Gu, Poonam, Leon Muller, Alessio Gagliardi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine learning has permeated material optimization, providing high value. Nevertheless, its re- liance on a high number of samples limits its applicability to relatively explored material families. In this paper we extend the applicability of machine learning to novel and understudied materials through the PYTHIA (Probabilistic Yet Transferable Hierarchical Interaction Architecture) framework. Pythia utilizes two of the most powerful machine learning concepts, foundational models and transfer learning. We employ a complex foundational model trained on a large dataset of chemically similar molecules, on a target that is physically close to our prediction target. Then, we freeze the weights and perform subspace alignment with the use of a transformer layer and add uncertainty quantification with gaussian processes. These two layers can be trained on a limited dataset yet allow us to leverage the gained knowl- edge and the studied materials and complex architectures. The resulting model is used as a surrogate for a Bayesian Optimization scheme. To showcase the robustness of the framework, we applied it to the investigation of adsorption and defect optimization of two distinct systems. Not only were we able to correctly and efficiently identify the optimal adsorption sites of even complex a
  </details>

- **[Training Small Language Models for Transferable Tool Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31483)**  `arXiv:2610.31483`  
  _Zhaoyi Zhou, Akshayaa Magesh, Jeongyeol Kwon, Artem Kuriksha, Daniel Jiang, Andrea Zanette, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As LLM agents operate over corpora of more than ten thousand tools, selecting the right tool for a complex multi-step user instruction becomes a central bottleneck. Tool descriptions cannot all fit in context, and relevance-based retrievers only rank tools without knowing which are actually needed. Recent work instead leverages proprietary commercial LLMs like Claude or ChatGPT to drive a retriever, decomposing the instruction and selecting over retrieved candidates. We show that a small, open model can be trained via reinforcement learning to perform the same functionality. On ToolBench, a Qwen3-8B model post-trained with our recipe outperforms Llama-4-Maverick (402B). In addition, the trained model transfers directly to different retrievers and generalizes to new tool corpora in different domains.
  </details>

- **[RheOPT: Few-Shot Non-Newtonian Flow Prediction via Newtonian Pretraining of RheOFormer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31465)**  `arXiv:2610.31465`  
  _Maedeh Saberi, Amir Barati Farimani, Safa Jamali_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite advances in numerical methods, non-Newtonian flow simulations remain computationally expensive because velocity and stress must be advanced together through rate- and history-dependent constitutive dynamics. Neural-operator surrogates reduce inference cost after training, but their training data require the same expensive simulations they aim to replace, making data-efficient learning essential when only a few non-Newtonian simulations can be afforded. Here, we introduce the Rheological Operator Pretrained Transformer (RheOPT), which pretrains a transformer-based neural operator on inexpensive Newtonian simulations and fine-tunes it on a small number of non-Newtonian samples. Rather than transferring the Newtonian constitutive response itself, RheOPT reuses geometry-conditioned transport structure that remains useful after the stress model changes. We evaluate RheOPT in two geometry-matched settings: a $4{:}1$ contraction channel for Giesekus, Oldroyd-B, and Bingham fluids, and the oscillatory wake behind a triangular obstacle for Oldroyd-B flow. In the contraction, Newtonian pretraining substantially improves few-shot prediction, and a model fine-tuned on four target samples can match or exceed a from-scratch model trained on four times as many. This benefit is reliable under reversible instance normalization (RevIN) but dataset dependent under Global normalization, a difference we trace to the dynamic range of each fluid's velocity and stress fields. In the wake, where relative $L_2$ error alone cannot verify that vortex shedding is recovered, phase-insensitive diagnostics show that pretraining recovers the shedding frequency and wake fluctuation amplitude that are suppressed by the from-scratch model in the few-shot regime. Overall, RheOPT establishes Newtonian simulation as a physically grounded pretraining source for data-efficient non-Newtonian operator learning.
  </details>

- **[Drift Says When, Budget Says How Much: Rate-Controlled KV Re-selection for Long-Form Multimodal Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31433)**  `arXiv:2610.31433`  
  _Bin Hu, Yingbo Xiao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  KV cache compression for multimodal LLMs usually selects the visual tokens to keep once, at prefill, and reuses that selection for the whole generation. We show that this one-shot selection is severely limited on long-form generation: on detailed image description, even an oracle that selects with the attention of the uncompressed answer reaches only 0.44-0.52 ROUGE-L agreement with the uncompressed output, because the evidence the model reads moves as the description unfolds. Re-selecting whenever attention drifts out of the kept set is the natural remedy, but we find that a fixed drift threshold fails systematically: drift magnitudes are not comparable across samples, so low-drift samples are almost never refreshed although they benefit the most. We propose Rate-Controlled Refresh (RCR), which lets drift decide when and where to re-select while a per-sample controller regulates how often, combined with a low-rank drift detector, candidate-restricted re-selection, and a decision that stays on the GPU. With one setting for all models and tasks, RCR outperforms the per-step retrieval method InfiniGen by 1.6-3.8 ROUGE-L points on five of six model-task settings with no significant difference on the sixth, while staying within 1.7 points of exact per-step re-selection, with comparable estimated data movement per step and a 4x smaller GPU-resident detector. On DetailCaps, drift-chosen timing beats random timing at the same refresh rate and periodic timing even at a higher rate, by 2.0-5.0 points. On pre-registered video description, where drift is homogeneous across samples, RCR is on par with InfiniGen on Qwen2-VL and exceeds it on Qwen3-VL but no longer beats a fixed threshold at the nearest tested rates, consistent with the diagnosis; object-level evaluation shows that RCR achieves higher object F1 against the uncompressed output than InfiniGen on two of three models.
  </details>

- **[Predictable performance is suboptimal, and what it means for model routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31428)**  `arXiv:2610.31428`  
  _Guanhua Zhang, Moritz Hardt_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) differ in capabilities and costs, motivating routing methods that adaptively choose a model for each query. These methods rely on performance prediction: given a query, estimate whether a model will answer it correctly. Most routing methods train separate predictors of candidate models' performance to guide model selection. Our work shows that performance prediction only beats a simple baseline when the candidate model is suboptimal on the task or the performance predictor adds significant capacity relative to the candidate model. Specifically, we take a model's self-confidence, the probability it assigns to its chosen answer, as a baseline and study how much a performance predictor can improve on it. First, we show that any performance predictor that outperforms a model's self-confidence can be used to improve the model's loss: the predictor's advantage transfers exactly in log loss through recalibration. Second, accounting for the predictor's additional parameters, we bound its advantage by the model's suboptimality plus the gain from those parameters. Third, we extend our theoretical results to two-model cascades, which route queries to a more capable model when the first model is predicted to fail. Experiments with 16 language models on four benchmarks show that predictors gain a larger advantage over self-confidence on more suboptimal models. This advantage shrinks as models improve through finetuning. Cascading exhibits the same two patterns: predictor-based routing offers larger gains over confidence-based routing for more suboptimal models, and these gains diminish after finetuning.
  </details>

- **[Post-Hoc Feature Selection Layer for Neural Networks Interpretability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31420)**  `arXiv:2610.31420`  
  _João Kenji Suwa, João Pedro Silveira e Silva, Bruno Iochins Grisci_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The interpretability of complex neural networks remains a critical challenge, especially for models already deployed in high-stakes domains. To address this, we adapted the Feature Selection Layer (FSL) to optimize feature weights post-training, a variant we term post-hoc FSL. Our approach reframes the FSL as a lightweight, trainable module that integrates with already frozen pre-trained models on tabular datasets to highlight the features the original model considers most important. This post-hoc FSL learns feature relevance by fine-tuning its weights based on the original model's learned outputs. Crucially, this process is non-invasive, operating without altering the original model's architecture or its learned parameters. We conducted our experiments using both statistical and visual metrics, including accuracy, F1 score, recall, precision, weighted t-SNE and silhouette score, and also analyzed the stability of the post-hoc FSL on high-dimensional synthetic and real-world tabular datasets. We compared the post-hoc FSL feature weighting method using these metrics against the original embedded FSL and other post-hoc interpretability methods, such as Integrated Gradients, Noise Tunnel, DeepLIFT, Gradient SHAP, and Feature Ablation. Experimental results demonstrate that the post-hoc FSL feature weighting method successfully identified relevant features across the different datasets, maintaining the predictive power of the original neural network while enhancing its interpretability.
  </details>

- **[Harmful Responses Are Jailbreak Templates: Transferring Autoregressive Jailbreaks to Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31331)**  `arXiv:2610.31331`  
  _Jinwei Gan, Zifeng Cheng, Zhiwei Jiang, Ran Sun, Shiping Ge, Qing Gu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) enable flexible masked generation through bidirectional context modeling, but this capability also introduces a distinct jailbreak attack surface. We find that a harmful response to a given query naturally serves as an effective jailbreak template: simply masking a subset of its tokens can induce a dLLM to reconstruct harmful content. Based on this observation, we propose RMT-Jailbreak, a general framework that transfers existing autoregressive (AR) jailbreak attacks to dLLMs through their induced harmful responses. RMT-Jailbreak converts an AR-generated harmful response into a high-mask-ratio template using uniform token retention, and further applies prompt-based safety filtering to mask any remaining visible harmful content. The refined template is then provided to the target dLLM for bidirectional reconstruction. Extensive experiments across multiple AR jailbreak methods and target dLLMs demonstrate the effectiveness and generality of RMT-Jailbreak, revealing strong cross-paradigm transferability of jailbreak attacks.
  </details>

- **[Bridging the Data Gap: Source-Informed Task-Space Transport for Few-Shot Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31323)**  `arXiv:2610.31323`  
  _Jinjian Jiang, Jing Peng, Rehan Deen, Yu Cao, Ziquan Liu, Muhammad Salman Haleem, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many deployments require a model to move rapidly from a data-rich source to a related target observed only sparsely. This is especially consequential where target data are difficult to collect, as in healthcare settings with persistent population or acquisition imbalances. Retraining or fine-tuning a high-capacity generator for every target is costly and can be statistically unreliable when target data are scarce. We introduce Meta-Diff, which instead learns a recurring source--target relation once in a compact task space. A shared encoder maps each distribution to a low-dimensional coordinate; a learned relation transports a new source coordinate to its predicted target, and a shared diffusion backbone generates from that prediction without target-specific retraining. Controlled Gaussian-mixture experiments identify when this works: task identity must survive the representation, the relation must generalise across tasks, and the source--target shift must leave useful headroom for transport. On held-out CIFAR-100 validation tasks, correct source $+$ transport achieves 13.9% lower mean SW than transport from the wrong source. With validation-selected adaptation settings in the final-test one-target comparison, its mean SW is 14.7% lower than target-only modelling and 32.7% lower than full fine-tuning; the main route itself uses no target image and assumes the applicable relation is known. On matched CIFAR test episodes, generating from the clean-source coordinate and then applying the known corruption yields lower SW than task-space transport. Office-Home exposes a complementary boundary: transport can align target-domain statistics without reliable object identity. Together, these results recast extreme-few-shot adaptation as reusable transport in task space, while making the conditions for its success and failure directly measurable.
  </details>

- **[The Value of System 1 Models in Medicine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31315)**  `arXiv:2610.31315`  
  _Xiufeng Yang, Konstantinos Vrettos, Zeineb Ben Chaaben, Fabian Drexel, Jannik Lübberstedt, Johannes Moll, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many clinical decisions are a choice among a few fixed options, taken quickly and in large numbers. Such decisions also need an uncertainty estimate to know when to defer to a clinician. Generative language models answer slowly in free text and state their confidence poorly. In contrast, System 1 models answer zero-shot with a probability for each answer. However, their performance across clinical tasks is unknown. Four System 1 models, two proprietary and two with open weights, were evaluated zero-shot on 86 public tasks from 14 clinical domains in 20 languages, spanning examination questions and medical imaging. Tasks were assigned to six capability tiers before analysis. The primary outcome was the area under the receiver-operating-characteristic curve (AUROC). Secondary outcomes included calibration, language effects and comparisons with published results and generative models. Across 179,111 cases, the mean AUROC of the best model per task was 0.94 for medical knowledge, 0.93 for text understanding, 0.83 for clinical judgement from text, 0.89 for image perception, 0.84 for 2D image findings and 0.69 for volumetric findings. Imaging tasks scored 0.09 (95% CI, 0.06 to 0.11) lower than text tasks (P < 0.001). The best model matched or exceeded the best published result on 13 of 26 text tasks and on 8 of 35 imaging tasks. Triage nurses outperformed the best model (accuracy, 0.85 vs. 0.56). One decision took 0.34 to 0.44 seconds and 24 to 81 joules on open-weight models run locally. Accuracy on identical questions fell by up to 39 percentage points from English to Yoruba. Generative models of the same family were 11 to 76 times slower, with 3 to 5 percentage points higher balanced accuracy. System 1 models decide quickly, cheaply and with usable confidence when the answer is stated in a text or recalled from medical knowledge, but not when it must be judged or read from an image.
  </details>

- **[Identifiability from Stationary Covariance in Linear SDEs with Reciprocal Interactions and Latent Variables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31310)**  `arXiv:2610.31310`  
  _Ana Leticia Garcez Vicente, Saber Salehkaleybar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continuous-time stochastic differential equations (SDEs) provide a natural framework for modeling interacting dynamical systems. A central causal question is whether causal effects among observed variables can be identified from observational data drawn from the stationary distribution, given the underlying causal graph. For linear SDEs, existing identifiability results are largely restricted to fully observed systems whose causal graphs contain no directed 2-cycles. We first establish new graphical conditions for the generic identifiability of causal effects in systems that may contain directed 2-cycles, thereby allowing for reciprocal interactions. Next, we address the previously unexplored setting of latent variables by developing an instrumental-variable framework for identifying a target direct causal effect under partial observability.
  </details>

- **[Extreme Compression of Per-Layer Embeddings via Cross-Layer Latent Vector Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31309)**  `arXiv:2610.31309`  
  _Jiacheng Liu, Yaxin Luo, Xiaohan Zhao, Peng Sun, Xinyi Shang, Jiacheng Cui, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling embedding capacity alongside Transformer computation is an emerging trend in language models. This trend makes high compression rates increasingly useful. Hence, we study extreme compression of Per-Layer Embeddings (PLE), which follow this trend and account for 35.2% of the parameters of Gemma 4 E4B. At such rates, conventional quantizers give each token-layer vector its own code of only a few bytes. We instead develop OPTIC (Output-Path-fitted, Token-shared Indexed Codes), which stores one quantized code tuple per token and decodes it at every layer with a layer-specific affine decoder. Codes and decoders are fitted to the PLE output path rather than to the stored vectors. At about $100\times$ PLE compression of bfloat16 (BF16) E4B, OPTIC retains an aggregate 97.8% of the uncompressed model's performance across seven benchmarks, whereas product quantization, the strongest of six conventional baselines, retains 66.3%. At $594\times$ and $1,002\times$ PLE compression of BF16 E4B, OPTIC retains 90.5% and 84.1%. On the official E4B release with 4-bit linear weights and 16-bit activations (W4A16), OPTIC retains 96.9% at about $100\times$ PLE compression. During inference at about $100\times$ PLE compression, BF16 E4B uses 29.7% less GPU memory than with uncompressed PLE at the same key-value cache size, while running at most 0.40% slower.
  </details>

- **[The Bayesian Learning Rule Beyond KL Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31308)**  `arXiv:2610.31308`  
  _Sophia Sklaviadis, Wu Lin, Andre Martins, Mario Figueiredo, Thomas Moellenhoff, Emtiyaz Khan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The Bayesian learning rule (BLR) exploits the relation between Bayesian updating and the Kullback-Leibler divergence (KLD) to derive efficient additive updates for exponential family variational posteriors. The KL geometry is powerful but restrictive: combined with posterior families with parameter-dependent support it can lead to undefined objectives. We ask which non-KL geometries preserve the additive BLR computation. Previous works that use $f$-divergences suggest that this is only possible with the KLD. Here, we show that this is not true. We show that additive information-geometric optimization algorithms can be derived for the class of mixed-space Bregman or Fenchel-Young discrepancies. These discrepancies induce generalized maximum-entropy families, dual coordinates, and generalized-BLR canonical gradient updates, recovering the classical BLR. The generalized BLR leads to new algorithms that \textit{cannot} be derived from the KL-based BLR. For the Tsallis negentropy with quadratic statistics, the generalized BLR yields bounded-support and heavy-tailed $q$-Gaussian posteriors and an easy Newton-like implementation. Experiments up to CIFAR-100 illustrate controllable support and tail behavior and competitive performance.
  </details>

- **[DISC: Domain-Integrated Similarity Cost for Time-Series Dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31288)**  `arXiv:2610.31288`  
  _Yen-Ku Liu, Hongjie Chen, Franck Dernoncourt, Ryan Rossi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The growth of time-series datasets and foundation models has made dataset comparison a central part of time-series workflows. Assessing source-target transfer before model adaptation requires similarity estimates that can summarize transfer-related dataset relationships without exhaustive source-target evaluation over every candidate pair. Many metrics have been proposed for measuring similarity between time-series datasets, but they capture different dataset relationships and vary in how well they align with downstream utility across tasks. We evaluate this gap by measuring the correlation between source-target similarity scores and downstream forecasting errors in out-of-distribution and transfer-learning settings. We propose Domain-Integrated Similarity Cost (DISC), a learnable aggregation framework that uses calibration transfer observations to select, weight, and combine similarity metrics for different downstream tasks. By integrating complementary metric values, DISC better distinguishes source-target dataset differences that matter for downstream behavior. After calibration, DISC scores source-target pairs using only their similarity-metric values and yields higher alignment than single-metric references on the reported split. These results show that time-series dataset similarity is task-dependent, and that learned metric integration provides a stronger proxy for downstream transfer behavior than manually selecting one similarity metric.
  </details>

- **[QuaNAS: Uncertainty-Aware Reuse of Earlier Searches for Query-Efficient Neural Architecture Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31251)**  `arXiv:2610.31251`  
  _Brama Seta Putra Satria, Dwi Sunaryono, Misbakhul Munir Irfan Subakti, Irzal Ahmad Sabilla, Laras Suciningtyas_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural architecture search (NAS) on a new dataset often affords only tens of short training runs, so what the search knows before its first evaluation decides its efficiency. Yet most NAS methods start from scratch, guided at best by zero-cost proxies that are unreliable across tasks. QuaNAS treats a library of earlier searches in the same search space as explicit knowledge of uncertain reliability. Main effects of design decisions learned from this library and, secondarily, zero-cost proxies become priors over candidate architectures without any label of the new dataset. A Gaussian process starts at the prior optimum and re-estimates after every evaluation how far each prior is trusted. Under a protocol frozen before any confirmation data were seen, QuaNAS was evaluated once on 12 new image, time-series, text and tabular datasets. It lowered anytime regret on the short-training objective 2.7- to 3.9-fold relative to five pre-specified NAS baselines that start from scratch (Holm-adjusted p = 0.0012 each). Its final models matched (non-inferiority margin 0.5 pp) or exceeded matched-recipe reference models on nine datasets, as did the prior optimum, selected without a single evaluation. Post-hoc comparisons with established transfer methods given the same library trace the gain to the library: without it, the same Gaussian process had over twice the regret. They also show where each transfer mechanism works best: the pooled main effects of QuaNAS for weakly transferable tabular tasks, per-task surrogates for image, time-series and text tasks and for small or mismatched libraries. Finally, the main effects make the transferred knowledge inspectable.
  </details>

- **[Towards Field-Deployable Wearable IMU Systems for Occupational Activity Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31160)**  `arXiv:2610.31160`  
  _Ihsan Ul Haq, James Brusey, Farnad Nasirzadeh, Chandan Karmakar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Wearable inertial measurement unit (IMU) systems for construction worker human activity recognition (HAR) routinely report aggregate accuracies above 95 %, rarely under genuine subject-independent (leave-one-subject-out (LOSO)) evaluation, masking the per-individual variance that determines real-world deployment performance. Using the VTT-ConIoT benchmark, we conduct, to our knowledge, the first systematic configuration sensitivity analysis of this dataset: six classifiers across five sensor placements and three window sizes under a single LOSO protocol, a personalised-calibration pilot, and a validated comparison of tabular GAN-based and standard time-series augmentation. Per-subject LOSO accuracy for the main multi-sensor, 16-class configuration ranges from 61.9 % to 88.7 % (mean 77.5 %), a 27 pp spread, though all data are laboratory-collected. Multi-sensor fusion and longer windows both improve accuracy; standard augmentation gives small, consistent gains, while Conditional Tabular GAN (CTGAN) augmentation actively harms accuracy with a demonstrated distributional-fidelity mechanism; and personalised calibration recovers 16.8 pp for the weakest participants from just 30 % of their own data. These findings provide evidence-grounded configuration guidance for scaling occupational body sensor networks towards field deployment
  </details>

- **[Is High Local Accuracy Enough? Geometric Consensus with Support-Adaptive Statistical Personalization for Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31133)**  `arXiv:2610.31133`  
  _Junyu Lin, Bosong Qi, Pengcheng Shi, Jiayuan Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world deployment of personalized federated learning (PFL) requires adapting to heterogeneous clients while preserving recognition performance beyond local distributions. However, high local accuracy can conceal failures on locally rare or absent classes. Effective personalization should therefore strengthen global recognition while raising the personalization floor. We propose an evaluation protocol that complements local accuracy with global, rare-class, and unseen-class evaluations to reveal generalization obscured by local scores, and assesses personalization floors among the worst-performing clients to expose weaknesses hidden by aggregate performance. To meet these deployment challenges, we propose FedGRC, a unified PFL framework integrating generalization and personalization through (i) Consensus-Guided Representation Learning (CGRL), which learns discriminative representations through shared directional and magnitude guidance; (ii) Multilayer Metric Synthesis (MMS), which constructs a shared covariance metric from these multilayer representations for statistical classification; and (iii) Support-Adaptive Shrinkage Personalization (SASP), which adapts class means within this metric by shrinking local estimates toward shared means according to local sample support, enabling adaptation while retaining shared knowledge. Extensive experiments, including benchmarking against 21 methods across 5 heterogeneity settings on 3 datasets and comprehensive ablation studies, validate the effectiveness of FedGRC. On CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100, FedGRC surpasses state-of-the-art (SOTA) baselines in global recognition and personalization floors by at least 2.17% and 2.13%, respectively. On the real-world heterogeneous FedRS dataset, it outperforms the respective best baselines by 2.15%, 3.30%, and 4.61% in local adaptation, global recognition, and the personalization floor.
  </details>

- **[nnInteractive Agent: General-Purpose AI Agents as 3D Image Annotators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31080)**  `arXiv:2610.31080`  
  _Fabian Isensee, Constantin Ulrich Harsy, Maximilian Rokuss, Benjamin Hamm, Philipp Vollmuth, Klaus H. Maier-Hein_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interactive 3D segmentation models such as nnInteractive turn a few spatial prompts (points, scribbles, boxes) into accurate volumetric masks, but a human still does the annotation work: finding each structure, placing prompts, checking the result and correcting it. General-purpose AI agents can now interpret images, call tools and write code. We show that such agents can take over this work without additional training: the user makes a request in plain language, and the agent works out what has to be segmented, locates the targets, prompts nnInteractive, inspects the 3D result, corrects it and responds to feedback. Asked for 'the muscles used for chewing', an agent identifies and segments the masseters and pterygoids; asked to count liver lesions, it writes its own code to find candidates and splits a merged lesion on request. Our open-source nninteractive-agent provides nnInteractive as tools over the Model Context Protocol (MCP), together with a skill that encodes how experienced users prompt it. On a benchmark spanning CT, MRI, PET and micro-CT of humans, a mouse and an ant (14 images; 51 targets from organs and tumours to muscles, foot bones, vessel trees and an insect brain), Claude Code and Codex reach a mean Dice of 0.81 and 0.84 from the request alone; when told where each target is, both reach 0.85 and prompt nearly as effectively as an oracle that places prompts using the reference segmentation. A text-prompted 3D model (VoxTell) is more accurate on most standard radiological targets but less robust: it returns an empty mask for one of them and fails on the leg muscles, the foot bones, the mouse and the ant, where the agents remain accurate. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[When Forgotten Facts Return: Relearning-Aware Unlearning of Knowledge Graph Embeddings via Tangent Kernels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31075)**  `arXiv:2610.31075`  
  _Parnian Fazel, Mohammad M Maheri, Hamed Haddadi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Approximate unlearning answers a deletion request on a knowledge-graph embedding by editing entity embeddings instead of retraining from scratch. These embeddings keep being updated as new facts arrive. Under this ordinary continued training, forgotten facts recover more than in the exact-unlearning baseline, a model retrained without them and given the same continued training. We trace this relearning to entity embeddings shared by forgotten and new facts. To our knowledge, this is the first study of relearning in knowledge-graph embeddings. Relearning-robust methods developed for deep networks do not account for the graph structure underlying this recovery. We propose Kernel-Anticipated Unlearning (KAU), which uses the tangent kernel to model how continued training changes forgotten facts' scores through these shared parameters. KAU expresses this first-order response in closed form and differentiates through it inside the forgetting loss. The objective steers edits toward directions that continued training reverses less. We compare KAU with state-of-the-art knowledge-graph unlearners and relearning-robust methods under identical continued training, measuring leakage beyond the exact-unlearning baseline. Across three backbones, KAU reduces the strongest knowledge-graph unlearner's leakage by 46-63% on FB15k-237 and 50-82% on CoDEx-L, at higher retained utility.
  </details>

- **[XALIGN: A Human-centric Visual Analytics Approach to Understanding AI Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31015)**  `arXiv:2610.31015`  
  _Bahavathy Kathirgamanathan, Gennady Andrienko, Natalia Andrienko, Tatiana von Landesberger_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Explainable AI (XAI) methods are usually evaluated for technical properties such as faithfulness and robustness. In high-stakes application domains, experts also need to check model predictions against established knowledge. Different explanation methods often disagree, and this disagreement is hard to see when each method is inspected separately. We propose XALIGN, a visual analytics workflow and interface that shows rule-based explanations and several feature attribution methods (TreeSHAP, KernelSHAP, and LIME) side by side for the same Random Forest model. Agreements and contradictions between the methods become visible signals that users can explore and compare with domain knowledge. We apply XALIGN in two case studies: the German Credit dataset and a meteorological dataset, in which events derived from ICON model output and Cloudnet observations are classified. In both, the views reveal discrepancies between the rule-based explanation and the attribution methods that a single method would not show. In an expert evaluation with three specialists, participants found XALIGN useful for identifying influential features and for avoiding over-trust in predictions. Linking rules to attributions was harder for the participant without an XAI background.
  </details>

- **[EEG-Captioner: Grounding EEG-to-Language Generation in Fundamental Signal Properties](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30995)**  `arXiv:2610.30995`  
  _Leyuan Jin, Jiahao Mei, Yixuan Yuan, Shimeng Li, Mengyue Wu, Yue Ding_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Electroencephalography (EEG)-to-language models offer a promising approach for translating complex neural signals into human-readable descriptions. However, generating plausible language does not necessarily demonstrate that a model has learned to interpret the underlying EEG signal. A direct way to assess such interpretation is to probe objectively measurable properties of the signal itself. We therefore construct an EEG-language dataset of 160,781 segments annotated with signal-level question answering (QA) pairs, where each question targets one of five property families and requires a quantitative comparison involving spectral, spatial, temporal, or lateral signal properties. Each segment is also paired with a feature-grounded EEG caption. We further propose a Dual-Domain EEG Encoder that jointly models temporal waveforms and spectral-spatial representations, and introduce EEG-Captioner, a unified EEG-to-language framework trained with both signal-level QA and EEG caption supervision. Experiments show that the proposed encoder achieves the highest overall macro-averaged balanced accuracy (Macro-BA) among the compared EEG encoders, while EEG-Captioner achieves a Macro-BA of 88.81% on signal-level QA and improves caption factual consistency from 82.06% to 83.46% over caption-only language adaptation, demonstrating the complementary benefits of signal-level QA and caption supervision.
  </details>

- **[Energy and Latency First: How Deployment Constraints Reverse Time-Series Model Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30965)**  `arXiv:2610.30965`  
  _Shruti Bothe, Illyyne Saffar, Ayush K. Varshney_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI models, and time-series models in particular are typically ranked by accuracy and deployed under constraints absent from that ranking. In this paper, we study a real setting where these constraints are explicit and measurable: handover pre- diction in cellular networks, where inference runs on the User Equipment once per 40 ms measurement period and must complete before the network acts. The timing bound is hard: a late prediction is superseded by the measurement it was predicting, while energy is a cumulative budget spent for the life of the device. Neither appears in the objective these models are selected on. We evaluate eight architectures under a protocol that screens for deadline feasibility at the tail rather than the mean, then ranks the feasible models on measured inference energy, count- ing the feature computation that precedes the forward pass and is usually omitted. Gradient-boosted trees on handcrafted temporal features are the most accurate model (F1=0.974) and among the cheapest to run, while the neural architecture an accuracy-only benchmark would rank first (CNN-1D, F1=0.914) costs over 3 times more energy per inference under a realistic duty-cycled deployment pattern. We give three conditions under which this outcome should be expected, so the finding applies beyond telecommunications. Accuracy-only benchmarking does not merely under-report deployment cost. It can rank first a model that cannot be deployed.
  </details>

- **[Doubly Robust Distributionally Robust Offline Contextual Pricing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30957)**  `arXiv:2610.30957`  
  _Min Xu, Xinyi Yin, Yunfan Zhang, Yuxuan Han, Houcai Shen, Caihua Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Offline contextual pricing often relies on logged observational data, but faces challenges from distributional shifts between training and deployment environments. Distributionally robust optimization (DRO) provides a principled approach to off-policy evaluation and learning (OPE/L). However, existing methods are mostly limited to discrete actions. Recent work has explored DRO for continuous treatments using inverse propensity weighting (IPW), while such IPW-based estimators can be sensitive to the convergence rate of propensity score estimates, particularly when estimated nonparametrically, which may lead to larger estimation errors and regret. In this work, we develop a doubly robust (DR) framework for distributionally robust OPE/L in continuous pricing settings. For evaluation, we propose a localized DR estimator that addresses the computational challenges of worst-case expectations by fitting only a small number of regressions, comparable to standard non-robust DR, while achieving semiparametric efficiency under mild product rate conditions. For learning, we leverage the inherent smoothness of demand noise to handle pricing-specific discontinuities in revenue outcomes (e.g., threshold-based purchase decisions), establishing a finite-sample regret bound of $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_p(T^{-s/(2s+1)})$ for smoothness orders $s=1,2$. This bound improves upon existing regret rates in existing DRO-based off-policy learning (OPL) for continuous treatments. Extensive experiments under various levels of distribution shift validate our proposed framework.
  </details>

- **[Ground-Truth-First Benchmarking of Reinforcement Learning via Converse Optimality: From Smooth, Fully Observed Systems to Non-Smooth or Partially Observed Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30954)**  `arXiv:2610.30954`  
  _Grégoire Ouerdane, Sinan Ibrahim, Stefan Streif, Pavel Osinenko_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmarking in reinforcement learning is usually relative: algorithms are ranked by returns on environments with unknown optima. Distance to optimality remains unobservable, while conclusions may depend on reward design, stochasticity, hyperparameters, environment selection, and simulator details. We develop a ground-truth-first framework based on converse optimality, constructing each task with a certified optimal value function and policy withheld from learners and used only for evaluation. The framework unifies two modes: sensitivity benchmarking samples certified environments from continuous-time generators to assess robustness to task variation, while oracle-referenced comparison uses native discrete-time generators to evaluate policies via optimality gap, regret, and policy error. Continuous-time models support physically interpretable sensitivity studies but introduce dependence on the simulator step ($\Delta t$) and numerical scheme. Native discrete-time models align the environment, oracle, and learner on the same transition law, eliminating external integration choices as a confounder. We reproduce quantitative results covering optimizer sensitivity and oracle-referenced comparisons on serial planar arms, dynamically extended nonholonomic vehicles, and quadrotor-inspired tasks. The methodology extends beyond smooth affine Gaussian systems: virtual-input pullback handles non-affine raw-action interfaces and finite-covariance non-Gaussian disturbances. A non-smooth converse optimality formulation enables certified benchmarks with kinked value functions and switching surfaces. For partially observed evaluation, an information-state formulation reduces benchmarking to converse optimality on a sufficient statistic and provides an information-penalty reference. Code, fixtures, and diagnostics are available on the public project page.
  </details>

- **[OpenAttention: One Percent Is Enough with Tail Proxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30951)**  `arXiv:2610.30951`  
  _Hyunwoo Yu, Jongbeom Lee, Hyunbin Kim, Sungjun Park, Jaemin Choi, Jincheol Yang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse attention reads only a small subset of the key-value cache, and how to treat the unread tail remains a key challenge. Discarding the tail biases the output, and sampling it as previous approaches do fails at reads of 5% and below, where the tail likely holds heavy hitters the selector missed. Our key observation is that the simplest possible proxy, one token that folds the entire tail into its count-aware mean, already yields lower output error than a sample at every read behind a practical selector. We further show in closed form that at low reads sampling nearly doubles the weight error of discarding while a single proxy stays below it. Building on these findings, we explore approximating the dense attention distribution with a few proxies that target the tail. We propose OpenAttention, a pipeline of clustering, ErrorGate and top-k selection followed by a count-aware mean proxy. To implement clustering and selection both efficiently and effectively, we design training-free Query-Geometry Clustering (QGC) and a Query-Geometry Selector (QGS), which compress the keys while respecting the query directions. Additionally, unlike prior methods that rank clusters by centroid alone, ErrorGate allocates the exact read by the estimated weight error of each cluster. On RULER-32K HARD with Llama-3.1-8B, OpenAttention retains 99% of dense accuracy at a 1% read. It also achieves state-of-the-art accuracy across models, context lengths and datasets. Our customized attention kernel,this http URL, runs the decode attention stage 10$\times$ faster than dense attention at a 1% read.
  </details>

- **[Random Initialization as a Baseline for Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30950)**  `arXiv:2610.30950`  
  _Pinar Sungu Isiacik, Georgiana Ifrim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series foundation models (TSFMs) use self-supervised pretraining to learn reusable representations for downstream tasks such as multivariate time series classification (MTSC). We examine how much pretrained weights contribute beyond the architecture itself by comparing seven publicly released instances from four TSFM families with architecture-matched randomly initialized counterparts on the UEA MTSC archive. For each instance, five random initializations are evaluated using four classifiers on frozen representations: ridge classification, logistic regression, RBF SVM, and random forest. After model-specific exclusions due to computational constraints, the study comprises 183 paired dataset-level comparisons. Pretraining improves mean accuracy for all seven instances, but the gains are modest, ranging from 1.43 to 5.19 percentage points, with an overall average improvement of 2.58 points. Three of seven per-instance Wilcoxon signed-rank tests have raw $p$-values below 0.05, but only NuTime remains significant after Holm correction. A secondary pooled test across all 183 pairs gives $p < 0.001$, while the corrected per-instance tests remain the primary evidence. We also compare with ROCKET, Hydra, and Quant, which remain competitive without requiring large-scale pretraining. These findings suggest that MTSC evaluations should distinguish the effect of pretrained weights from that of the underlying architecture and should include both architecture-matched random initialization and strong non-pretrained baselines.
  </details>

- **[Same molecule, Different Knowledge: Cross-Identifier Binding Failures in LLMs For chemistry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30903)**  `arXiv:2610.30903`  
  _Khouloud Saadi, Di Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chemical AI workflows routinely represent the same molecule through multiple co-referring identifiers, including trivial names, IUPAC names, and SMILES, implicitly assuming that these representations provide interchangeable access to an LLM's knowledge. To test this assumption, we first introduce a controlled benchmark and show that it does not hold. Across foundation models spanning 7B to 72B parameters, even chemistry-specialized models, co-referring molecular identifiers consistently expose different subsets of the model's knowledge. We then conduct a series of controlled behavioral, representational, and causal analyses to characterize this failure. We show that even when a model can identify a molecule from its SMILES and retrieve a fact through the corresponding name, the same fact can remain inaccessible directly from SMILES. We term this phenomenon \emph{cross-identifier binding failure}. Further analysis of internal representations, conflicting identifiers, and parameter editing shows that neither improved chemical representation competence nor modification of individual facts is sufficient to establish reliable access across identifiers. Together, these results indicate that the core failure lies in the binding between an identifier and knowledge already present in the model. Motivated by this diagnosis, we introduce Binding Self-Distillation, a simple mitigation that uses lexical identity during training to transfer the model's existing knowledge access to the structural route. This enables factual retrieval directly from SMILES at inference time, without requiring a name lookup or training the underlying model from scratch.
  </details>

- **[Building Presentation Intelligence for High-Quality Slide Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30888)**  `arXiv:2610.30888`  
  _Yuhang Xie, Cheng Li, Chenliang Li, Mingyu Xu, Mingwei Sun, Suhang Zheng, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  PowerPoint presentations (PPTs) are a dominant medium for communication across diverse domains, yet high-quality PPT generation still depends heavily on labor-intensive human effort. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) for agentic tasks have created both new opportunities and challenges (Chen et al., 2026) for automatic PPT generation. In this work, we present a unified training framework for automatic PPT generation toward presentation intelligence, integrating cross-validated data synthesis, guided teacher enhancement, and verifiable reward optimization. We first construct a cross-validated pipeline for data synthesis, with explicit control over both difficulty and validity. We then develop a teacher-enhancement framework grounded in rules and prior knowledge to substantially strengthen the instructional capacity of teacher models. Building on this, we further improve supervised fine-tuning (SFT) through a mixed-teacher strategy and a lightweight outcome-driven trajectory filtering method. Finally, based on the resulting SFT models, we introduce a suite of verifiable aesthetic metrics together with a GRPO-based reinforcement learning framework to optimize slide-level visual attributes beyond text-only supervision. Experiments show that our enhanced SFT pipeline improves Qwen-3.6-27B and Qwen-3.6-35B-A3B by 7.45 and 9.35 points on PresentBench, respectively, surpassing GLM-5.2 while maintaining strong generalization. Reinforcement learning further improves performance across five aesthetic dimensions, compensating for the limitations of SFT in visual refinement. Overall, the results demonstrate the effectiveness of our framework for high-quality PPT generation.
  </details>

- **[Tracing Subliminal Learning Through Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30884)**  `arXiv:2610.30884`  
  _Angela Tang, Vishesh Anand, Gonçalo Paulo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models trained on data generated by other models can sometimes inherit traits unrelated to that data, a phenomenon called subliminal learning.
