We study both hard label distillation, where students train on numbers generated by a biased teacher, and on-policy soft label distillation, where students generate the data and the teacher's logits provide the learning signal. Using Pythia, PolyPythia, and DataDecide OLMo models, we find that transfer persists in both settings across differences in weight initialization, pretraining data order, pretraining corpus, model size, and architecture. Across pretraining checkpoints, the ability to transmit a preference and the ability to acquire one develop separately: some checkpoints are more effective teachers but weaker students, and vice versa. Consistent with this distinction, cross-model transfer can be strongly asymmetric in magnitude when teacher and student roles are reversed. To investigate what carries the preference signal, we construct synthetic number sequences that match the teacher's generated number frequencies but have no dependence on the prompt or preceding numbers and find that these still induce substantial transfer in the hard-label setting. In top-$K$ soft-label distillation, removing animal tokens from the loss reduces transfer, although not completely in the same-model setting. In our experiments, soft label distillation produces more consistent transfer with less extensive training than hard label distillation. The growing use of on-policy distillation in model post-training makes this a particularly relevant setting for assessing unintended preference transfer.
  </details>

- **[MOER: Mix-Policy Optimization under Expert Prefix Guidance with Selective Rollback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30873)**  `arXiv:2610.30873`  
  _Xuyang Zhong, Jiuding Yang, Yiwen Guo, Chen Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mix-policy optimization incorporates off-policy expert data into the reinforcement learning (RL) loop to transcend the capability boundary of on-policy RL. However, existing methods either provide static full-trace guidance that suppresses self-exploration, or adopt expert prefix guidance without correctness guarantees, leading to suboptimal performance or even training collapse. In this paper, we propose MOER, a mix-policy optimization framework that harmonizes expert guidance with self-exploration. MOER injects a decaying expert chain-of-thought prefix into the prompt and lets the model complete the remaining reasoning, with the prefix length progressively annealed to encourage self-exploration. A selective rollback mechanism replaces failed completions with the original full expert trace, guaranteeing the correctness of every off-policy trajectory. Furthermore, a harmonized mix-policy objective optimizes on-policy tokens with a GRPO loss while supervising both expert prefix and completion tokens with a trust-region SFT loss, substantially stabilizing training. Extensive experiments show that MOER achieves the highest average scores in mathematical and general domain reasoning tasks, outperforming all basic and mix-policy baselines, and generalizes consistently across diverse tasks, model scales, and base model categories. Codes are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Training Mixed-Monotone Neural Networks for Verification Without Set Propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30853)**  `arXiv:2610.30853`  
  _Jonas Fingerhut, Lukas Koller, Matthias Althoff_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most formal verification approaches for neural networks conservatively enclose the output set of the neural network. However, computing a tight output enclosure is in general computationally hard. We sidestep this challenge with a novel neural network architecture that realizes mixed-monotone functions as the difference of monotone subnetworks. By utilizing the theory on mixed-monotone systems, this reduces the computation of a tight output enclosure from complicated set propagation to two simple forward passes of opposite corners of an input interval. Moreover, because the size of the resulting enclosure is a closed-form function of the weights of the neural network, we can directly minimize it during training. We prove a lower bound on the size of any such enclosure, given by the total variation of the represented function generalized to several inputs, which makes it possible to compare the tightness of the enclosure against the optimum. Our evaluation on regression and dynamical-system benchmarks demonstrates that our training reduces the enclosure size to within 0.5% of the lower bound without sacrificing prediction accuracy. Computing our enclosure is orders of magnitude faster than CROWN and $\alpha$-CROWN and on deep networks it is also tighter than zonotope propagation and CROWN, while its size exceeds that of $\alpha$-CROWN by only 5.1%.
  </details>

- **[Reverse-KL Policy Distillation: Statistical Guarantees and Limits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30844)**  `arXiv:2610.30844`  
  _Haolin Liu, Chen-Yu Wei, Julian Zimmert_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reverse Kullback-Leibler (KL) distillation has shown empirical promise for training language models, but its statistical guarantees and limits remain poorly understood. In particular, the effects of two design choices remain unclear: Does access to the teacher's full action distribution provide an advantage over observing only the probability it assigns to the sampled action? Must training trajectories come from the student (online distillation), or can teacher-generated trajectories (offline distillation) suffice? Our work takes a first step toward understanding the theory underlying these questions.We study the sample complexity of online reverse-KL distillation under two feedback models: the teacher reveals either its full action distribution or only the probability of the student-generated action. We establish minimax bounds for the former and show that substantially more data may be required for the latter. In contrast, offline reverse-KL distillation can attain the same minimax rate whether the teacher reveals its full distribution or only its probability of the teacher-generated action. The key distinction is that teacher-generated actions themselves reveal the teacher's preferences, while queries at student-selected actions can leave those preferences unobserved. This highlights an additional statistical challenge for online distillation compared to offline distillation.These statistical guarantees do not follow simply from optimizing standard distillation objectives: our lower bounds show that common online objectives can fail when candidates are evaluated only on past student-generated trajectories. We develop trajectory-loss and importance-ratio truncation techniques together with policy-space learning to achieve optimal online distillation guarantees.
  </details>

- **[Neural Spectral Capacity with Residual Context for Heterogeneous Resource Allocation within Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30823)**  `arXiv:2610.30823`  
  _Chenyu Zhu, Ruoyu Zhao, Zhichao Lu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predicting relative trained performance from architectural specifications can guide design when training costs limit experimentation. Neural Spectral Capacity (NSC) provides such a heuristic, but scores projections independently and assigns identical scores to compatible reorderings of the same layer configurations. We introduce Residual-Aware Neural Spectral Capacity (ResNSC) for Transformers, using a specification-derived statistical reference for accumulated residual updates to determine the input and residual-state statistics in each projection's capacity calculation, while retaining additive evaluation without data or instantiated weights. When residual widths vary, earlier choices affect later scores, so we augment NSC's allocator (NSC-DP) with residual history beyond its layer-and-budget state. ResNSC-DP jointly allocates residual and feed-forward widths under a resource budget, with certified bounds on the best numerically evaluated ResNSC score within a specified finite candidate set. At the 121M and 374M pretraining scales, selected architectures achieve lower mean perplexity than uniform, variable-width, and tapered allocations on FineWeb-Edu, WikiText-2, and LAMBADA at comparable parameter budgets. In inherited-weight LLaMA-7B pruning using the LoNAS supernet, ResNSC-DP improves eight-task average accuracy over NSC by 3.28--5.46 percentage points across eight matched parameter budgets. Paired reversals preserve the projection shapes and NSC scores yet worsen performance in both settings, exposing performance differences hidden by position-independent scoring. The code for this work is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Is spectral predictability enough? Testing Ω-matching for synthetic pretraining under distribution shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30802)**  `arXiv:2610.30802`  
  _Edric Castel C. Hao, Juan Raphael S. Seña, Neon S. De Jesus_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Synthetic pretraining under distribution shift requires deciding what synthetic data to use. A natural criterion is to match the spectral predictability $\Omega$ of the source to the target, but is $\Omega$ sufficient? We test this hypothesis, which we call predictability-aware pretraining, across three architectures (LSTM, PatchTST, TCN) and three seeds. Under ideal synthetic-to-synthetic conditions, $\Omega$-matched pretraining can outperform even an oracle baseline (+46.8% MSE reduction, LSTM), though the benefit is already architecture-dependent. On the real-world ETTm1 benchmark, however, this relationship does not hold: sine-plus-noise pretraining at matched $\Omega$ yields only a marginal mean improvement +2.3% averaged over all three architectures, up to +65.5% on individual channels), whereas $\Omega$ matched chaotic ODE trajectories systematically degrade performance -13.2%). The two sources agree on the direction of transfer for only 53% of configurations across all three architectures (Pearson r=-0.02), indicating that $\Omega$ carries little information about which source will transfer beneficially. We further observe an architecture-dependent asymmetry in the response to the direction of the $\Omega$ gap. We conclude that $\Omega$ matching can help in controlled settings but is not sufficient to predict transfer across sources, and we discuss additional signal properties that a source-selection criterion may need to consider.
  </details>

- **[A Computationally Efficient and Rate-Optimal Algorithm for Adversarial Linear MDPs with (Aggregate) Bandit Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30801)**  `arXiv:2610.30801`  
  _Haolin Liu, Chen-Yu Wei, Hanbin Zhou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study episodic linear Markov decision processes with unknown transitions, adversarial losses, and bandit feedback. The state-of-the-art result for this setting (Liu et al., 2024) either achieves $\sqrt{K}$ regret with a computationally inefficient algorithm or achieves suboptimal $K^{3/4}$ regret, where $K$ is the number of episodes. Our work gives the first $\sqrt{K}$ regret with a polynomial-time algorithm. Our method reduces adversarial linear MDPs to adversarial linear bandits whose action set consists of policies' expected feature vectors. The central challenge is that this action set is unknown and can be estimated only through exploration. We address this challenge by constructing an approximate separation oracle for the action set that either generates executable policies from queried features, or refines an outer approximation of the action set. This approach draws inspiration from the reduction of van Erven et al. (2025) for linear contextual bandits and the oracle-efficient linear bandit algorithm of Ito et al. (2019), but additionally deals with the imprecision of the oracle. The resulting algorithm requires only aggregate loss feedback, and to our knowledge. This is the first polynomial-time algorithm for adversarial MDPs with aggregate feedback beyond the tabular case.
  </details>

- **[GIFT: Gradient Informed Filtering for Distillation Augmented Reasoning RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30796)**  `arXiv:2610.30796`  
  _Shijie Li, Yilin Gao, Tieyuan Chen, Yun Luo, Weiyao Lin, Hang Yu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training large language models for complex reasoning tasks relies on two complementary signals: sparse, outcome-grounded reinforcement learning (RL) for correctness, and dense, token-level distillation for guidance. However, complementarity in supervision does not guarantee compatibility in optimization; imitation-focused distillation can conflict with the outcome-driven RL objective, and existing hybrids lack a principled resolution. We propose \textbf{GIFT} (\textbf{G}radient-\textbf{I}nformed \textbf{F}il\textbf{T}ering for Distillation-Augmented Reasoning RL), a novel method that directly addresses the allocation problem of which teacher updates to apply. GIFT performs a token-level geometric analysis, decomposing the distillation gradient into a component \textbf{parallel} to the RL gradient, which measures first-order conflict and provides a \textbf{safety} filter, and a component \textbf{orthogonal} to it, which quantifies novel directional guidance and provides a measure of \textbf{value}. This decomposition yields a principled scoring rule to rank token-level guidance. We combine this with a progressive \textbf{coverage annealing} schedule that shrinks the budget of tokens receiving distillation, ensuring that the most conflicting updates are discarded first while the most complementary ones are retained at full strength. This facilitates a smooth transition from dense, geometrically-filtered guidance to a pure, outcome-grounded objective at convergence. Extensive experiments on mathematical and logical reasoning benchmarks show that GIFT consistently outperforms strong baselines across multiple model scales, achieving relative gains of up to 9.5\% on difficult mathematical reasoning tasks. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Gandalf: Flash Shall Not Die! Increasing Flash Storage Lifetime for KV Cache Offloading in LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30791)**  `arXiv:2610.30791`  
  _Maharani A. P. Irawan, Kuntai Du, Jiayi Yao, Dongjoo Seo, Yuyang Huang, Haryadi S. Gunawi, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  KV caching reduces the serving cost of large language models (LLMs), but the growing KV cache footprint is increasingly exceeds GPU and host-memory capacity. Therefore, people use SSD to expand the KV cache storage capacity with high I/O bandwidth. However, in production LLM workload, such as multi-round conversation and LLM agents, the total amount of KV cache writes significantly exceeds SSD capacity, shortening SSD lifetime from years to months. To improve SSD lifetime, a natural solution is to reduce the KV cache writes by gating the KV cache writes via historical access patterns. However, that significantly lowers the KV cache hit rate (and increase the LLM serving cost) since KV caches from different attention types might have identical historical access pattern but different future access patterns. Our insight is that, to reduce KV cache writes without hurting KV cache hit rate, one need to not only consider the access pattern, but also application-level information such as the attention type of KV cache. Leveraging this observation, we propose Gandalf , the first SSD KV cache offloading system with SSD life time awareness. Gandalf prioritize the admission of full-attention KV cache into SSD over the cache generated by other types of attention, which significantly increases SSD lifetime without sacrificing cache hit rate. Our evaluation shows that Gandalf extends the SSD lifetime by 7.3X on average, while having negligible impact on cache hit rate across multiple workload traces and wide range of hardware capacity. Gandalf design can be extended to other flash-based storage, such as High Bandwidth Flash (HBF).
  </details>

- **[One Pretrained Decision Model Is Enough for Training-Free Generalist Graph Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30777)**  `arXiv:2610.30777`  
  _Xinyu Zhao, Qingyun Sun, Ziwei Zhang, Tianchen Zhu, Daqing Li, Jianxin Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph anomaly detection (GAD) aims to identify irregular entities in graph-structured data, yet heterogeneous anomaly patterns and distributional differences across graphs challenge generalizable detection. Existing training-free approaches avoid task-specific optimization but primarily rely on predefined numerical scoring rules, leaving the semantic interpretation of heterogeneous anomaly evidence largely unexplored. In this paper, we propose Jev-GAD, a training-free generalist graph anomaly detection framework that bridges heterogeneous numerical evidence and pretrained decision mechanisms. To address heterogeneous anomaly patterns, we construct complementary structural, attribute, and neighborhood evidence and transform heterogeneous statistics into graph-relative anomaly percentiles. To move beyond predefined numerical scoring rules, we leverage a single frozen pretrained language model for evidence-conditioned anomaly assessment. To bridge the semantic gap between numerical evidence and pretrained models, we design a semantic Graph-to-State (G2S) transformation that organizes graph context and semantically labeled node evidence into structured decision states for non-generative option-logit readout. The resulting decision scores are integrated with four complementary statistical scores through equal-weight rank-based fusion, without GAD-specific gradient-based optimization. Extensive experiments on seven graph anomaly detection benchmarks spanning citation, social, and co-review networks demonstrate the effectiveness of Jev-GAD, achieving average AUROC and AUPRC scores of 89.18\% and 52.40\%, respectively. Further analyses validate the contributions of pretrained decision readout and semantic evidence grounding.
  </details>

- **[Primal-Dual Representation Learning for Low-Rank Constrained MDPs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30756)**  `arXiv:2610.30756`  
  _Chenhao Zhou, Chao Zhang, Wensong Bai, Hanbin Zhao, Hui Qian_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Representation learning has substantially improved the efficiency of unconstrained continuous-state Markov Decision Processes (MDPs), but its extension to Constrained MDPs (CMDPs) is difficult because the transition representation must be learned while constraint violation is controlled. In this paper, we study representation learning for low-rank CMDPs with continuous state spaces, and propose REP-PD-TEG for the soft cumulative-violation setting. In the feature learning period, the algorithm interacts with the environment using a decaying-$\epsilon$-greedy mixture execution policy, and uses the collected samples to learn transition features by maximum likelihood. Based on the learned features, it constructs uncertainty bonuses and plans with an optimistic Lagrangian objective to obtain the return policy. We prove regret and soft-violation guarantees for the return policies, and quantify the influence of the decaying-$\epsilon$-greedy exploration. In particular, REP-PD-TEG achieves the optimal $\widetilde O(\sqrt K)$ return policy bound, and gives a $\widetilde O(K^{3/4})$ overall sample bound when exploratory actions are charged. For the stricter hard cumulative violation, where feasibility must be verified episode by episode, we propose REP-PD-H-TEG. It plans under a bonus-corrected lower-confidence utility certificate with the learned representation, and performs a stabilized dual search to obtain the return policy, yielding regret and hard-violation bounds. Together, these results give the first theoretically guaranteed representation-learning method for low-rank continuous-state CMDPs with both soft cumulative guarantees and certified hard-violation control.
  </details>

- **[Visualizing and Interpreting Temporal Care Team Dynamics for Cancer Survival Risk Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30748)**  `arXiv:2610.30748`  
  _Yuhua Huang, Hsiao-Ying Lu, Kwan-Liu Ma_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cancer care is a longitudinal, time-sensitive process requiring coordination among diverse healthcare professionals (HCPs), whose collaboration evolves throughout treatment. Prior studies link care team structure to patient outcomes, but the temporal dynamics of collaboration and their relationship to evolving patient risk remain poorly understood. We introduce a visual analytics framework for modeling and interpreting time-varying care team interactions in oncology. We construct dynamic collaboration networks from electronic health record (EHR)-mediated communication data and use a temporal graph neural network (TGNN) to estimate patient outcome risk over time. We then investigate how changes in care team composition and specialist participation relate to shifts in estimated risk across treatment stages. Our multi-view interactive system combines TGNN-based risk modeling with coordinated visualizations of care team networks, feature-level explanations from a surrogate logistic regression model, and exploratory what-if analysis. Without making causal claims, the system helps analysts examine how risk estimates co-evolve with collaboration patterns and identify care team characteristics associated with these changes. We demonstrate its utility through expert-guided analysis scenarios involving 505 patients with breast, colorectal, and lung cancers, evaluating its ability to reveal clinically meaningful collaboration patterns. This work improves the interpretability of temporal graph-based models in longitudinal, team-based healthcare, with potential applicability to other evolving collaborative settings.
  </details>

- **[Random Remasking Scales Masked Diffusion Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30744)**  `arXiv:2610.30744`  
  _Zhaoyi Zhou, Frank Wu, Andrea Zanette_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Masked diffusion models (MDMs) generate sequences by progressively unmasking tokens, but errors introduced during decoding are typically irreversible. Recent work has shown that iterative correction can improve sample quality, yet existing theory fails to explain this given a learned, imperfect denoiser model. We re-formulate MDM inference as a Markov chain on the clean sequence space, defined by a remask-denoise kernel that randomly re-introduces masks and then denoises. This helps us analyze the actual denoising trajectory towards the data distribution. The analysis motivates Random Remask-Denoise (R$^2$D) inference, a purely test-time, finetuning-free, reward-free procedure. To further improve performance, we propose Trajectory Correction Training (TCT), a denoiser-training procedure that exposes the model to its own errors along the generation trajectory. On logical tasks, our approach exhibits a striking near-log-linear test-time scaling, achieving over 97% accuracy on hard Sudoku puzzles and over 99% accuracy on 3-SAT. Our approach also applies to pretrained models on actual language tasks, beating iterative correction baselines and the official Q-mode accuracy of LLaDA2.1-mini on HumanEval+.
  </details>

- **[Federated Graph Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30728)**  `arXiv:2610.30728`  
  _Xiaobao Wang, Renda Han, Zhiliang Xia, Meng Liu, Renxiang Guan, Di Jin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph Retrieval-Augmented Generation (GraphRAG) leverages centralized knowledge graphs to enhance large language models with structured knowledge retrieval. However, real-world knowledge is inherently distributed across multiple organizations rather than being centrally accessible. Privacy constraints further prohibit sharing entire local knowledge graphs, preventing GraphRAG from effectively retrieving and utilizing distributed knowledge. To this end, we propose Federated GraphRAG (FedGRAG), a unified framework for distributed graph retrieval and knowledge-enhanced generation over multiple clients with knowledge graphs. Specifically, we address two fundamental challenges in federated GraphRAG. First, the absence of a global knowledge graph makes unified retrieval impossible. To address this, we develop a Federated Knowledge Routing mechanism that efficiently identifies relevant clients through lightweight knowledge indices without exposing local graph structures. Second, local graph memory cannot be shared directly because it contains sensitive entities and knowledge information. We therefore propose a controlled-disclosure evidence construction method. It extracts retrieved evidence subgraphs and converts them into an evidence abstract, enabling evidence conversion and reconstruction on the server. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Federated GraphRAG consistently outperforms existing baselines in both retrieval and downstream generation tasks, establishing a new paradigm for deploying GraphRAG in federated environments.
  </details>

- **[Solvable models of learning to pursue a moving target](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30721)**  `arXiv:2610.30721`  
  _John J. Vastola, Kanaka Rajan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agents tasked with intercepting a moving target must learn how to reach it, ideally as efficiently as possible. As observers of animal pursuit behavior have noticed, different strategies are possible: on one extreme, the agent reactively moves toward the target's current location; on the other, the agent predicts the target's future location and moves directly there. Motivated by the desire to understand how such strategies might be learned, we introduce a continuous-time linear-quadratic model of open-loop target pursuit whose optimal control strategies interpolate between these possibilities. Usefully, a small number of interpretable parameters control which type of strategy is optimal, and it is possible to derive closed-form solutions for optimal strategies, policy learning dynamics, and value learning dynamics. Exploiting our model's linear structure, we find that the time scales of learning precisely correspond to the eigenvalues of certain matrices, and that relevant eigenvalue spectra generically have a gap. We show that this gap indicates that agents tend to learn how to reach their target before optimizing their movement. Our results provide a detailed mathematical characterization of pursuit behavior and its learning dynamics, which can both serve as a benchmark for empirical work, and as a foundation for more elaborate theoretical treatments of pursuit.
  </details>

- **[An Agent Architecture with Tools and Memory for Selective Correction of Wrist PPG Heart Rate Estimates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30688)**  `arXiv:2610.30688`  
  _Xu Wang, Shuhan Yi, Zhen Cheng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Wrist photoplethysmography (PPG) heart rate estimates are vulnerable to motion artifacts, and corrections that reduce mean error can worsen individual windows. We propose an agent architecture with tools and memory around a fixed heart rate estimator. Pulse, motion, and limited historical evidence generate candidate estimates, whose predicted benefits and harms guide selection. An independent risk gate determines whether to retain, correct, or reject each output. We trained the architecture on PPG-DaLiA and tested it on Wrist Exercise, GalaxyPPG, and WildPPG with frozen weights. Across these four public datasets, mean absolute error (MAE), macro-averaged over ten estimators, decreased by up to 44.5%, with the largest reduction on GalaxyPPG. Beneficial and harmful corrections decreased and increased absolute error by more than 1 bpm, respectively. Their ten-estimator macro-averaged proportions ranged from 16.06% to 36.64% and from 1.68% to 6.77%, respectively, across the four datasets. At 60% and 80% target coverage, numerical correction on the same accepted windows contributed more to total error reduction than window selection. We implemented real-time wearable acquisition, Bluetooth reception, and smartphone deployment of a selected estimator and the framework. In backend analysis of four prototype recordings from one participant, ten-estimator macro-averaged MAE decreased from 9.09 to 4.45 bpm without additional rejection. Under the evaluated conditions, the architecture improved average accuracy while quantifying correction benefits and harms, supporting wearable heart rate analysis that considers error, correction risk, and coverage.
  </details>

- **[Learning Domain Trajectories with Flow Matching for Gradual Domain Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30685)**  `arXiv:2610.30685`  
  _Yubo Huang, Yushe Cao, Zixi Wang, Jingzehua Xu, Xin Lai, Xiongyu Hu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large distribution shifts remain a primary obstacle for domain generalization and unsupervised adaptation. Although gradual domain adaptation alleviates this difficulty by introducing ordered intermediate domains, most existing approaches still operate in a discrete, step-wise manner, which can accumulate errors and often relies on unstable Min--Max optimization. We propose \textbf{FlowGDA}, a flow-matching framework that models gradual adaptation as \emph{continuous} transport on a statistical manifold in a representation space. FlowGDA learns time-dependent velocity fields that induce smooth domain trajectories, using a simulation-free regression objective rather than adversarial training. To prevent semantic drift, we couple left-to-right transport between consecutive domains with a source-anchored transport and a semantic consistency loss, and we further stabilize learning via a two-stage sequential optimization scheme. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method outperforms state-of-the-art techniques, validating flow matching as an effective alternative for domain adaptation.
  </details>

- **[Interpreting Model Reasoning through Behavioral Counterfactuals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30630)**  `arXiv:2610.30630`  
  _Shawn Im, James Oldfield, Adel Bibi, Sharon Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Faithfully understanding the internal process that occurs as models form their chain of thought is an important problem with the rise in model capabilities. However, this remains challenging due to the variance in sampled responses, making it difficult to isolate the effects of interventions and compare model reasoning systematically. We propose a method for localizing and analyzing how a given intervention affects the model's reasoning through behavioral counterfactuals: given a fixed response, how does introducing an intervention to the prompt change the next-token distributions? We train a module to edit the residual stream such that it minimizes the KL divergence between the output distributions for a fixed response under a source prompt and its counterfactual target prompt. We find that matching the output distributions recovers the counterfactual behavior more effectively than standard activation reconstruction objectives and validate that attribution of the trained module outputs is effective for identifying causally important locations for counterfactual recovery. The attribution maps and visualizations of the downstream effects of model edits provide an interface for generating hypotheses about the original model's computation, which can then be tested with activation patching. In a case study on Gemma-4-E4B-it and DeepSeek-R1-0528-Qwen3-8B, we investigate how a suggestion influences model reasoning even when it is not verbally acknowledged. Our analysis identifies representations at the question statement and filler tokens that mediate a substantial part of the suggestion's influence, with downstream effects such as changing the framing of the justification or even the final answer.
  </details>

- **[Future of Time Series Anomaly Detection: From Historical Review to Unified Outlook](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30628)**  `arXiv:2610.30628`  
  _Zhijie Zhong, Zhiwen Yu, Pengyu Li, Liujian Zhang, Qiaoli Ni, Jiang Jun, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series anomaly detection (TSAD) is a foundational problem in data mining, aiming to identify observations that deviate significantly from nominal temporal patterns. Driven by escalating data volumes and application complexity, recent research has yielded diverse architectures, including diffusion models, state space models, and large language models (LLMs). However, existing surveys provide limited coverage of these emerging designs and lack a unified treatment of multivariate anomalies, cross-domain unified modeling, metric reliability, and contemporary evaluation challenges. To bridge these gaps, this survey delivers three core contributions: (1) We establish a refined anomaly categorization and a structured taxonomy of paradigms reflecting modern advances, comparing detection mechanisms and charting developmental trajectories across paradigms. (2) We provide an extensive analysis of evaluation methodologies and conduct comprehensive experiments to assess metric fidelity, complemented by an open-source benchmark repository encompassing algorithms, datasets, and evaluation tools. (3) We outline key open challenges, offering forward-looking perspectives on dynamic thresholding, model interpretability, zero-shot transfer, multi-modal alignment, and foundational models for time series. By addressing existing theoretical and empirical limitations, this survey establishes a coherent framework to guide future TSAD research and practical deployments.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Type-1 Diabetes Digital Twins by Decision Quality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30577)**  `arXiv:2610.30577`  
  _Victor Li, Owen Tucker, Michael S. Hughes, Temiloluwa Prioleau, Shengpu Tang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A digital twin (DT) for type 1 diabetes (T1D) can predict how an individual's blood glucose will respond to different insulin treatments without actually trying them. For each treatment, downstream statistics of predicted glucose trajectories, such as glycemic risk, can be computed to guide clinical decisions. However, the quality of DTs is usually measured by the accuracy of their predicted glucose trajectories, which may not reflect the quality of the downstream treatment decisions they inform. In this work, we directly evaluate DTs by this decision quality on downstream treatment ranking, i.e., whether a DT predicts glucose trajectories that can be used to rank candidate treatments as the real patient would. We present an evaluation protocol over 30 virtual patients from the FDA-accepted UVA/Padova model, each simulated under 21 different basal-bolus treatments. For each patient, a DT is fitted under one treatment and then produces glucose trajectories for the remaining 20 treatments. Our results show that prediction accuracy and decision quality disagree. A baseline that models the average patient, never seeing the individual's glucose data, has the lowest prediction accuracy of all six methods (RMSE: 63.6 mg/dL) but ranks treatments with the second-highest mean Spearman (0.766), while a black-box neural network that predicts glucose nearly twice as accurately (RMSE: 32.9 mg/dL) ranks them no better than a random ordering of the 20 treatments (0.049 Spearman). Therefore, selecting a DT based on prediction accuracy alone can prefer one that recommends treatments at chance, and decision quality must be evaluated directly rather than assumed to follow from prediction accuracy.
  </details>

- **[NEST: Nested Embedding Representation for Multiscale Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30520)**  `arXiv:2610.30520`  
  _Zhanqi Zhang, Mikio Aoi, Gal Mishne_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world data often exhibits cluster structure at multiple resolutions, yet existing methods typically learn a single partition or construct an explicit hierarchy. Inspired by Matryoshka Representation Learning, we introduce NEST, an unsupervised framework that encodes coarse-to-fine structure within a single embedding. NEST jointly optimizes clustering and separation across nested prefix subspaces, enabling progressively finer distinctions as embedding dimensionality increases, with rank and within-cluster regularization that keep the embedding from collapsing. Empirically, distances in the learned embedding follow the hierarchy distance more closely than those of the input, although the hierarchy is never observed during training. On synthetic hierarchies, NEST recovers multiscale structure given only the number of clusters per level. Experiments on vision, text, and single-cell data further demonstrate multiscale organization across real-world representations. By making clustering granularity an intrinsic property of the representation, NEST provides a unified approach to multiscale structure discovery.
  </details>

- **[DiffLTM: Continuous Diffusions Large Tabular Models for Conditional Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30472)**  `arXiv:2610.30472`  
  _Alankrita Bhatt, Mukur Gupta, Andrea Montanari, Pulkit Tandon_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conditional generation is a key capability to be supported by Large Tabular Models, enabling a number of applications. Accurate conditional generation has to contend with two key challenges: $(i)$ Data comprise a mixture of numerical and categorical entries, the latter often with high cardinality and heavily unbalanced proportions; $(ii)$ Conditional generation becomes increasingly difficult for rare sub-populations. The best models in this class use a hybrid of continuous and discrete diffusions. In this paper, we design a continuous diffusion model with strong generation quality. An important element is the use of cross-entropy loss for categoricals, for which we provide a first theoretical justification. We introduce metrics that focus on rare subpopulations. Further, we generalize conditional generation techniques to enable new types of conditioning. Finally, we show that our model (trained on labor data) can capture classical insights from econometrics.Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Short-to-Long: Detached Features for Long-Horizon Value Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30461)**  `arXiv:2610.30461`  
  _Tong Che, Boris Ivanovic, Marco Pavone_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning useful representations from continually changing value targets remains a challenge in deep reinforcement learning. We study the temporal horizon of this supervision: smaller discounts make these targets less sensitive to distant bootstrap changes, but using them for behavior changes the control objective. We introduce Short-to-Long (S2L), which supplies a long-horizon behavior learner with features trained by smaller-discount source networks. Detached connections prevent the behavior value loss from updating the sources, allowing each source to follow its own value objective while the behavior discount remains unchanged. Across Atari, online continuous control, and offline continuous control, the approach yields aggregate performance gains, improving the paired terminal median on 40 of 57 Atari games. Matched Atari ablations support the roles of feature transfer, smaller source discounts, and gradient isolation. Fixed-trajectory measurements further show reduced drift in source value targets with mixed discounts.
  </details>

- **[aggmetrics: Aggregating Classification Performance Measures for Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30456)**  `arXiv:2610.30456`  
  _Ronaldo Cristiano Prati_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  aggmetrics is a Python package that extends any binary performance measure expressed in the four confusion-matrix counts to multiclass, multilabel, ordinal, cost-sensitive, soft-label and cross-validation settings, by substituting one of four aggregation operators for what libraries usually expose as an average switch. The package also stores, for every measure, the structural properties that decide which identities apply to it, can infer those properties from a user-supplied formula, and uses them to warn when a measure is used where it does not behave as commonly assumed. Its test suite encodes the results of the companion theory as executable tests. The package is BSD-3 licensed, depends only on NumPy, and is available atthis https URLdocumentation atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Stability Analysis of Signed Graphs under Structural Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30444)**  `arXiv:2610.30444`  
  _Ning Zhang, Li Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Signed graph learning has emerged as a powerful framework for modeling networks with both positive and negative interactions. While graph-filter-based methods have achieved promising empirical performance on signed networks, their stability under edge perturbations remains less well understood. Existing stability analyses primarily focus on unsigned graphs and do not account for sign-flip perturbations. In this paper, we study the stability of signed graph filters under edge additions, deletions, and sign flips. Our theoretical analysis reveals how signed edge perturbations and node feature correlations jointly determine the learned representation stability. Based on this analysis, we propose a theory-guided perturbation strategy that selects structurally influential signed-edge modifications. Experiments on synthetic and real signed graphs show that the proposed method consistently induces larger embedding drift, polarity change, and downstream link prediction degradation than baselines. These results highlight the importance of \textit{sign-aware} and \textit{distribution-aware} stability analysis for signed graph learning.
  </details>

- **[Towards Faithful Data Selection: Statistical Limits and Scalable Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30442)**  `arXiv:2610.30442`  
  _Ahmed Mehdi Inane, Mahdi Haghifam, Gintare Karolina Dziugaite, Ioannis Mitliagkas_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  What does it mean to have an optimal data selection algorithm? Optimality is conventionally framed as finding a subset that minimizes the population error of the classifier trained only on that subset. Our starting point in this work is first to show that this goal is information-theoretically impossible unless the data selection algorithm has access to a validation set order-wise larger than the training set. This impossibility result motivates shifting from unverifiable population risk minimization to a one-sided guarantee that we refer to as faithfulness: for any candidate subset, we provide an estimable proxy that upper-bounds the difference between training on the full training data and on only the subset. We model training with the Unadjusted Langevin Algorithm and bound the Kullback--Leibler divergence between subset-trained and full-data-trained parameter distributions by the trajectory-accumulated gradient discrepancy. Minimizing this discrepancy gives a principled way to select a subset. Even though the resulting problem is NP-hard, we propose a swap-based local search that decreases the bound at every accepted step. It can therefore refine the output of any selector without worsening its bound. Finally, we provide experimental results on CIFAR10 and CIFAR100 showing that it matches existing data selection baselines. Implementation of our algorithm and the code for reproducing the results are available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[AcoustiPrint: Physics-Guided Neural Design of Manufacturable Acoustic Structures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30411)**  `arXiv:2610.30411`  
  _Shanmuka Sadhu, Harshvardhan C. Takawale, Yehia Zakaria, Ahmed Allam, Nirupam Roy_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work in additive manufacturing and neural inverse design enables the creation of complex three-dimensional structures to manipulate physical fields, yet these designs must also adhere to discrete, non-differentiable fabrication constraints. Current methods limit the design space by imposing differentiable soft manufacturability loss penalties, or by fixing structures through post-processing, causing a discrepancy between the geometry optimized by the physics model and the final fabricated result. Thus, we present AcoustiPrint, a physics-informed neural architecture for constrained inverse design that represents three-dimensional geometries with an implicit neural representation (INR) and passes each candidate through a printability operator before evaluating its final response. The printability operator executes layer-wise self-support, discrete material selection, and whole-body connectivity, assuring fabrication feasibility. To allow for backpropagation through the discrete non-differentiable operations, we use surrogate gradients for the backward pass and a self-supervised printability objective that progressively drives the INR toward a valid design domain. We demonstrate AcoustiPrint for personalized transcranial focused ultrasound, where inter-patient differences in acoustic properties and head geometry deform wave propagation and requires patient-specific acoustic focusing. AcoustiPrint jointly optimizes three-dimensional geometry and material composition through differentiable wave physics while ensuring manufacturability. Experiments show manufacturable acoustic structures with enhanced focal accuracy and over density-based and phase-based baselines, demonstrating a generalized methodology for neural design through non-differentiable manufacturing constraints and differentiable physics.
  </details>

- **[Bayesian Fixed-Confidence Best-Arm Identification with Abstention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30384)**  `arXiv:2610.30384`  
  _Yuqi Huang, Yunlong Hou, Vincent Y. F. Tan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study the Bayesian fixed-confidence best-arm identification problem in which a learner can abstain from making a terminal recommendation. Subject to a target error probability $\delta$ and an abstention budget $\alpha$, we propose Screen-and-Confirm (S&C) and analyze its expected stopping time. We further derive the minimum expected stopping time for any algorithm that satisfies these constraints. For Gaussian priors and rewards, as $(\delta,\alpha)\downarrow(0,0)$, we establish matching upper and lower bounds up to multiplicative constants, with rate $\kappa_\nu^2[1+\log((\alpha+\delta)/\delta)]/(\alpha+\delta)$, where $\kappa_\nu$ is the prior density of the top-two gap at zero, highlighting that nearly tied instances govern the sampling cost. When abstention is disallowed ($\alpha=0$), S&C yields the optimal $\Theta(1/\delta)$ dependence for fixed model parameters, removing the additional $\log(1/\delta)$ factor in the upper bound of Jang et al. (2024) under our Gaussian assumptions. We further characterize the exact multiplicative constant of the minimum expected stopping time for practical regimes including (i) fixed $\alpha \in (0,1)$ and (ii) $\alpha/\delta\to\infty$, which are also achieved by S&C.
  </details>

- **[CTRL-FM: Inference-Time Structural Control for Flow-Based Virtual Sensing under Long-Horizon Node Outages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30362)**  `arXiv:2610.30362`  
  _Boshra Khalili, Andrew Smyth_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon node outages are harder than conventional missing-value imputation because complete sensor trajectories remain unobserved for extended periods, while the usefulness of spatial and temporal dependencies varies across outage locations and operating regimes. Existing graph, diffusion, and flow-based methods typically impose a fixed structural bias, which can oversmooth abrupt changes or propagate errors from unreliable neighbors. We introduce CTRL-FM, an observation-preserving flow-matching framework that treats structural guidance as an outage-conditioned inference-time decision. A missing-only flow-matching backbone learns a conditional velocity field while keeping observed measurements exactly fixed. For each realized outage, CTRL-FM constructs self-supervised proxy outages from surviving measurements and uses their known values to select among uncontrolled, graph-dominant, temporal-dominant, and balanced control policies. The selected policy is locally modulated by graph and temporal reliability estimated exclusively from surviving observations, while boundary-anchored trends provide structural guidance. Control is applied only to missing entries and is rejected when proxy evidence does not sufficiently improve over uncontrolled sampling. Across PeMS08, METR-LA, Air-36, and AQI, CTRL-FM achieves the strongest overall performance under 30% and 60% node outages. Stress tests with up to 75% sensor loss further show graceful degradation and consistent gains over training-time and fixed structural control. These results demonstrate that validating, selecting, and locally adapting structural guidance for each realized outage is more effective than imposing a single global structural prior.
  </details>

- **[Spike-Aware Gradient-Based Mixed-Precision Quantization of Spiking Neural Networks for Vision Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30339)**  `arXiv:2610.30339`  
  _Rajshekhar Rakshit, Devgokul B.V, Rakshitha Kalkura, Gopalakrishnan Srinivasan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In recent years, Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs) have gained massive attention as an energy-efficient neural computing paradigm due to their event-driven and sparse computing capability. However, maintaining accuracy under low-precision constraints, necessary for practical deployment, has been largely unexplored for SNN models. In addition, existing Hessian-based approaches for quantization, such as HAWQ-V2, incur significant memory and computational overhead due to temporal unrolling across multiple SNN timesteps. We propose a spike-aware gradient-based mixed-precision quantization framework for SNNs that adaptively assigns bit-widths using first-order gradient information, avoiding costly second-order Hessian computations. We evaluate our method across various SNN-based architectures, including VGG, ResNet, and Vision Transformer models obtained from both ANN-to-SNN conversion and direct training frameworks, on CIFAR10/100, ImageNet, and DVS-CIFAR-10 datasets. Notably, on ResNet-34 evaluated on ImageNet, our method achieves 73.30% accuracy at 4.78$\times$ compression, which is comparable to that provided by HAWQ-V2, while reducing memory consumption by 2.31$\times$ and bit-allocation runtime by 100$\times$. We also present an energy analysis for mixed-precision SNNs showing that the proposed quantization strategy enables energy-efficient deployment without compromising performance.
  </details>

- **[GAV: A scientific experimentation environment for PDE agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30292)**  `arXiv:2610.30292`  
  _Muheng Li, Wenlong Mou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable feedback is essential for coding agents to develop programs for partial differential equations (PDEs). Yet evaluating these programs on new PDE problems often requires expensive reference computations, while fixed datasets restrict the range of experiments. We introduce Generator-Agent-Verifier (GAV), a scientific experimentation environment that makes new PDE problems verifiable by construction. The Generator learns a distribution of continuous reference solutions using data from numerical simulations or experimental measurements to represent a target physical regime. Using the method of manufactured solutions, it works backward from the generated reference solutions to construct matching PDE problems without a separate numerical solve. The Agent designs experiments, investigates failures, and iteratively improves its program. The Verifier evaluates candidate programs against the reference solutions and provides numerical feedback on accuracy and computational cost. This feedback can also guide the generation of new problems that probe the current program's weaknesses. We provide a theoretical analysis of evaluation reliability and guided development. Experiments on Navier-Stokes and Vlasov-Poisson-type equations across different data sources and regimes demonstrate the effectiveness of GAV. In particular, on the numerical Navier-Stokes task, GAV reduces the mean test error of three existing PDE agents by more than an order of magnitude relative to their original setups. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SWE-VERIFIER: Selective Verification for Cost-Efficient Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30284)**  `arXiv:2610.30284`  
  _Kaijian Zou, Vaibhav Chennareddy, Yunxiang Zhang, Naihao Deng, Longju Bai, Jiaxin Pei, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents spend substantial computation generating actions and processing long interaction histories. Poor action choices can steer agents into low-value states, wasting computation and increasing API costs. Sampling and verifying alternative actions can improve the agent's decisions, but applying this procedure at every step adds substantial overhead. We introduce SWE-Verifier, a lightweight generative model that selectively triggers candidate sampling and verification. It scores the coding agent's proposed action to determine when additional candidate sampling is needed, then ranks the resulting actions before execution. We train SWE-Verifier from Qwen3.5-4B using preference data that groups contrasting actions with their resulting environment observations and rubric-guided teacher scores. Using the mini-swe-agent harness, SWE-Verifier improves resolution from 75.47% to 77.20% on MiniMax-M2.7 at 1.09 times cost and generalizes to DeepSeek-V4-Flash without training on its actions, improving resolution from 72.60% to 74.27% at 1.13 times cost. Across both models, SWE-Verifier verifies fewer than 4% of steps and achieves comparable or higher resolution than the trajectory and all-step baselines at substantially lower cost. These results demonstrate that selective candidate sampling and lightweight verification provide complementary gains in coding-agent performance and inference efficiency. Our model, code, and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[A Quadratic Lens on Muon's Polar Update: Benefits, Pitfalls, and the Role of Newton-Schulz](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30280)**  `arXiv:2610.30280`  
  _Egor Shulgin, Sam Laing, Antonio Orvieto, Peter Richtárik_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Muon updates matrix-valued parameters along approximately orthogonalized directions and often trains large language models faster than AdamW, yet it remains unclear when and why orthogonalization helps. We study PolarGD, the orthogonalized update at the core of Muon, on matrix quadratics, where optimizers can be analyzed in closed form, and compare update directions under line search to remove the effect of learning-rate tuning. Orthogonalization pays off when the gradient is far from low rank and its largest singular directions see high curvature. PolarGD reaches the minimizer in one step whenever the rows of the error are orthogonal with equal norms, for any Hessian, whereas gradient descent needs all Hessian eigenvalues to be equal; and unlike sign descent, PolarGD is unaffected by rotations of the problem. Over many steps, PolarGD acts as gradient descent preconditioned by the current iterate, so its speed is governed by the conditioning of the iterate rather than of the Hessian. This explains fast regimes as well as a slow one, caused by tiny singular values of the iterate, in which PolarGD is much slower than gradient descent. Finally, the few Newton-Schulz iterations used in Muon treat these tiny singular values as gradient descent does, so fewer iterations can converge faster than exact orthogonalization; in a transformer trained with Muon, about a quarter of the singular values of the attention matrices fall in this regime.
  </details>

- **[Edit, Don't Imitate: Minimal-Edit On-Policy Distillation for Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30231)**  `arXiv:2610.30231`  
  _Wen Ye, Ailin Deng, Zirui Wang, Shixing Yu, Han Zhang, Tian Yun, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual mathematical reasoning can fail before reasoning begins: when a vision-language model (VLM) misreads a diagram, subsequent reasoning proceeds from false premises. This perceptual bottleneck is especially pronounced in smaller models, while accurate textual descriptions can substantially narrow the gap to larger models. Because obtaining such descriptions at inference requires additional annotation or model calls, we propose minimal-edit corrective on-policy distillation. Using reference figure descriptions and verified reference solutions as training-only information, a teacher scores incorrect student rollouts under a minimal-edit objective, and only teacher-disfavored tokens contribute to the corrective loss. Correct rollouts receive task-reward reinforcement learning. This preserves on-policy supervision while avoiding full-response imitation. Across model scales, the method requires no descriptions at test time and meets or exceeds the corresponding base model supplied with a reference figure description at inference. It also improves perception-oriented performance, particularly low-level geometric perception, and transfers to external mathematics and perception benchmarks. Because the minimal-edit teacher evaluates corrections rather than generating its own solution, the method remains effective with a frozen same-size teacher, although its gains are smaller than with a larger teacher on most metrics.
  </details>

- **[GANet: A Lightweight Group Attention Network for Plant Disease Identification on Embedded Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30225)**  `arXiv:2610.30225`  
  _Anubhab Tripathi, Bibek Das, Asfak Ali, Sheli Shinha Chaudhuri_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Global warming and climate change are major catalysts for plant diseases, significantly affecting food production worldwide. Addressing this issue requires continuous monitoring and early detection of plant diseases. While recent advancements in deep learning offer potential solutions, most models demand high processing power and struggle to perform efficiently on low-end electronic hardware. Furthermore, many existing works focus on specific crop diseases, limiting their broader applicability. This paper presents a lightweight Group Equivariance Attention Network (GANet) designed for the prediction and monitoring of 38 types of plant diseases. GANet introduces a novel Group Attention block and Group-Wise Feature Fusion block, making it both sparse and efficient. With only 0.001M parameters, the model achieves an impressive 99.99% accuracy on the PlantVillage dataset. Additionally, GANet is optimized for deployment on edge devices such as the Raspberry Pi 4b and Jetson Nano, where it reaches 5 FPS and 11 FPS, respectively, demonstrating its potential for real-time plant disease monitoring on low-power devices.
  </details>

- **[Prediction Is Not Calibration: Item Parameter Recovery and Representation Coupling in Knowledge Tracing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30211)**  `arXiv:2610.30211`  
  _Wenrui Yuan, Stéphanie M. van den Berg, Johannes Steinrücke_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge tracing (KT) models can estimate item parameters through an item response decoder trained solely for next-response prediction, yet predictive accuracy does not guarantee parameter recovery. We study 2PL, GPCM, and NRM decoders paired with LSTM, Transformer, and DKVMN encoders. Response-model derivatives show why discrimination and option slope updates depend directly on the scale and spread of estimated ability. Affine logit identities show how item-dependent shifts and rescaling in estimated ability are absorbed into item parameters without changing response probabilities. In simulations, difficulty, mean threshold, and correct-option intercept recovery remain high under shuffled order across representation widths, whereas discrimination and keyed option slope contrast recovery require wider item representations and degrade further under fixed item order. Separating the sequence item representation (width $d_S$) from the item parameter key (width $d_M$), and pairing a wide key with a thin sequence representation ($\mathrm{SK}(64,8)$), reduces fixed-order ability distortions and improves recovery and cross-order consistency without sacrificing test accuracy or increasing model size relative to a shared width-64 baseline ($\mathrm{SH}(64)$). On TIMSS and EdNet, widening $d_M$ consistently improves agreement with an offline IRT reference, while the trade-off from narrowing $d_S$ depends on the encoder's reliance on sequence width for prediction. These findings show why item parameters, especially discrimination and option slopes, must be evaluated separately from response prediction.
  </details>

- **[MoEZip: Routing-Aware KV Cache Compression for Sparse Mixture-of-Experts LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30206)**  `arXiv:2610.30206`  
  _Minsang Kim, Seungjun Baek_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) language models scale model capacity efficiently by activating a small subset of parameters per token. However, in long-context inference, memory remains a major bottleneck because the KV cache grows linearly with sequence length. Existing KV cache compression methods reduce this cost for dense transformers, but overlook a problem specific to MoE: KV eviction can change which experts are selected during answer generation, degrading the generation quality. We propose MoEZip, a routing-aware KV cache compression for sparse MoE language models. To estimate the sensitivity of expert routing to perturbation, we propose a novel metric based on the Fisher information matrix of routing distributions. MoEZip combines this metric with attention weights for routing-aware scoring of contexts. Finally, we compose a complementary scoring system that combines eviction methods for dense and sparse-MoE architectures. Experiments show that MoEZip achieves robust performance under aggressive cache compression on various benchmarks. At a KV retention ratio of 10\%, MoEZip improves over the SoTA baseline by +24 and +20 points on average on LongBench and RULER, corresponding to 2.3$\times$ and 4.9$\times$ higher scores, respectively.
  </details>

- **[Parallelizable Probabilistic Latent Reasoning for Diverse Reasoning Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29385)**  `arXiv:2610.29385`  
  _Seokhyun Lee, Seongjun Lee, Changhee Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language models relying on verbalized Chain-of-Thought (CoT) are constrained to token-by-token generation in the language space, where many tokens serve textual coherence rather than core reasoning. This entangles genuine differences in logical trajectories with superficial variations in verbalization. Moving reasoning into a compact, continuous latent space offers a promising alternative for efficiently representing and exploring diverse reasoning trajectories. However, current latent models remain largely deterministic, capturing only a single reasoning trajectory rather than modeling a distribution over multiple valid alternatives. While stochastic approaches parameterize distributions over latent reasoning steps, they frequently suffer from unstable optimization and limited empirical performance. Furthermore, their reliance on autoregressive latent prediction during training precludes effective parallelization, leading to prohibitively long training times. To overcome these bottlenecks, we propose Probabilistic Latent Reasoning Models (PLRM), a parallelizable variational framework for probabilistic latent reasoning. PLRM adopts a JEPA-style objective in which the posterior infers latent distributions in parallel from observed reasoning trajectories, while the prior learns to predict the subsequent latent distribution via teacher forcing. To model heterogeneous reasoning processes, we introduce a discrete strategy variable together with a mixture-based prior, allowing the model to preserve the Gaussian-mixture structure induced by multiple distinct reasoning trajectories rather than collapsing them into a single Gaussian. Across diverse benchmarks, PLRM achieves performance competitive with deterministic latent reasoning models on single-trajectory datasets while enabling efficient training and consistently outperforming competing stochastic baselines on multi-trajectory datasets.
  </details>

- **[NADS: Navigator-Guided Data Selection for Mitigating Catastrophic Forgetting in Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25751)**  `arXiv:2610.25751`  
  _Jianhao Zhang, Ou Wu, Yi Du_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Catastrophic forgetting is a central obstacle in single-stage fine-tuning of large language models (LLMs) on a new task, especially when the original pretraining data are unavailable. Synthetic data offers a practical mitigation path, but candidate pools are often noisy and redundant, making effective selection essential. Existing methods often rely on indirect proxy signals whose alignment with actual training benefit can be limited, while utility--diversity selection over large candidate pools remains computationally demanding. To address these challenges, we propose NAvigator-guided Data Selection (NADS), a fine-tuning framework for LLMs. NADS first follows the target fine-tuning recipe without preservation regularization to obtain a navigator model, whose deviation from the pretrained model exposes recipe-induced capability drift. It then uses the predictive divergence between the navigator and the pretrained model to score forgetting-aware utility for each synthetic candidate, and constructs a constraint set through an efficient utility--diversity selection strategy. Finally, NADS fine-tunes on the new-task data while distilling pretrained behavior on the selected constraints to preserve general capabilities. Experiments show that NADS delivers a stronger balance between new-task performance and general capability preservation, while reducing utility--diversity selection overhead. Code at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Generative Retrieval for Reverse Target Fishing with a Proteome-Scale Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24458)**  `arXiv:2610.24458`  
  _Zhichao Deng, Haichuan Tan, Xin Hong, Huaizhi Wang, Tao Huang, Bowen Gao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reverse target fishing identifies protein targets for a query molecule and is central to drug discovery and repurposing. Discriminative approaches adapted from virtual screening face sparse, long-tailed associations and missing-not-at-random measurements: treating unrecorded pairs as negatives can suppress genuine targets. We reformulate the task as generative retrieval and introduce GenFish. Residual vector quantization converts frozen protein language model features into shared semantic identifiers. Given a molecular fingerprint, GenFish autoregressively models codes conditioned on the query and preceding code, ranking proteins by identifier likelihood without explicitly labeling unrecorded pairs as negatives. We introduce ProTargetBench, the first proteome-scale benchmark for reverse target fishing to jointly support measured-panel discrimination and full-catalogue recovery of experimentally recorded positives. Covering 20,431 UniProt-reviewed human proteins, it integrates curated bioactivity evidence and distinguishes experimental inactivity from unmeasured associations. On 198 held-out molecules, GenFish achieves higher mean performance across all three full-catalogue retrieval metrics than four discriminative neural baselines and three non-neural baselines, including ligand-based methods. A controlled positive-hiding experiment shows that targeted removal of false-negative competition improves contrastive retrieval beyond matched random masking; GenFish remains stronger under the same incomplete supervision. Pfam alignment indicates that the semantic identifiers capture biologically meaningful relationships among proteins. These results establish generative retrieval as a principled paradigm for reverse target fishing under incomplete bioactivity records.
  </details>

- **[Predictable Scaling: Efficient Model Exploration via Scaling-Law Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23453)**  `arXiv:2610.23453`  
  _Aishwarya Wesanekar, Sheng-Tao Yang, Jiawei Zang, Flavio Sales Truzzi, Chen Chen, Nader Beigiparast, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating candidate changes (hypotheses) to recommendation models requires full-scale training runs consuming thousands of GPU-hours, severely limiting the hypothesis space engineers can feasibly explore. We introduce Predictable Scaling (PS), a systematic framework that exploits empirically verified regularity conditions to predict full-scale model performance from small-scale proxies. PS constructs scaled-down variants via composable scaling functions along distinct resource axes, and projects metrics to the target scale via curve fitting with calibrated uncertainty estimates. We compare three prediction strategies of increasing expressiveness: even the simplest (raw proxy) achieves prediction error within the empirical noise floor of repeated-run variance; the most expressive (Gaussian Process) provides an indicative 15% relative MAE improvement and calibrated uncertainty. Evaluation on two ranking models of increasing complexity---a task-specific model and a foundation model---demonstrates that PS correctly ranks actionable hypotheses across diverse hypothesis types at 79-88% compute reductions.
  </details>

- **[DashVMC: Real-Time Discrete World Model Control in Geometry Dash](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40003)**  `arXiv:2609.40003`  
  _Florent Tariolle, Florian Yger_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World-model agents are usually evaluated in simulators that can wait for the policy; live games impose the opposite constraint, requiring capture, prediction, and action before the next frame. We present DashVMC, which learns a compact, action-conditioned world model from approximately two hours of recorded Geometry Dash gameplay. To test whether the learned dynamics are actionable, a controller is initialized by behavioural cloning (BC) and refined with Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) entirely in frozen-model rollouts, without further interaction with the live game. Across three controller seeds, the refined policies survive longer than their BC initializations on all three official levels and a held-out community layout. At deployment, the baseline skips visual generation and sustains a 60-Hz capture-to-action loop on a consumer GPU. Action-conditioned continuations and rollout diagnostics show that the model remains useful for control despite imperfect long-horizon fidelity.
  </details>

- **[UnlearningSoup: Is Repeated Tuning Necessary for Large Language Model Unlearning?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37076)**  `arXiv:2609.37076`  
  _Puning Yang, Qizhou Wang, Junchi Yu, Bo Han, Xiuying Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models trained on vast corpora inherently risk memorizing harmful content that may later re-emerge in their outputs. To mitigate this issue, existing unlearning methods typically rely on training-based parameter updates, such as gradient ascent and its variants, to delete targeted content while preserving other knowledge. However, balancing the competing goals of forgetting and retention makes hyperparameter choices for these methods particularly difficult, often requiring repeated tuning to obtain a strong model that still leaves substantial room for improvement and transfers poorly across models and datasets. To address this challenge, we investigate whether unlearning runs exhibit exploitable structure in weight space, and observe that models from different runs still lie in a shared evaluation-performance basin. This suggests that stronger models may be recovered through an unlearning-tailored soup strategy, reducing the need for repeated tuning for further improvement or new settings. Motivated by this, we propose UnlearningSoup, a unified framework that provides two strategies: EfficientSoup uses binary-search-based interpolation to quickly discover a well-performing model in the early stage, where repeated tuning would otherwise make strong model selection costly. PerformanceSoup uses reweighted souping to efficiently unlock the remaining performance potential in the later stage, where repeated tuning becomes increasingly inefficient. Extensive experiments across diverse datasets and models show that UnlearningSoup delivers 2.4x to 3.3x efficiency gains in hyperparameter selection, while consistently improving performance across settings.
  </details>

- **[PROACT-Agent: Progressive Runtime Oversight and Active Circuit-breaking for Real-Time Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34415)**  `arXiv:2609.34415`  
  _Ding Jia, Wei Liu, Xianglong Du, Yingjie Li, Yingqing Yang, Huili Yu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The transition from Large Language Models (LLMs) to agents shifts safety stakes from toxic text to irreversible environmental harm. While current defenses remain largely retrospective, proactive runtime intervention is bottlenecked by the lack of large-scale, causally-consistent data. We propose PROACT-Agent, a framework for synthesizing high-fidelity trajectories to enable real-time guardrails. We identify a critical "safety drift" in prior benchmarks, where lenient annotation paradigms fail to enforce temporal consistency. PROACT-Agent addresses this through: (1) Progressive Trajectory Unrolling to reveal risks hidden in long-context interactions; (2) Reasoning-Augmented Causal Rectification to enforce monotonic causal consistency; and (3) Culturally-Aware Data Localization for cross-border robustness. We introduce PROACT-Bench, a bilingual safety benchmark with 155,780 states labeled through multi-model adjudication. Evaluating updated context before the next LLM inference, the trained guard achieves 91.46% unsafe-class F1 and 90.63% exact-boundary detection under complete source holdout. In AgentDojo, it reduces non-DoS targeted attack success from 20.82% to 0.40%.
  </details>

- **[Residual Correlation as a Diagnostic for Joint-Uncertainty Gains from GP Coregionalisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30085)**  `arXiv:2609.30085`  
  _Fangqin Zhou, Joaquin Vanschoren_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In multi-target regression, correlated targets are often coupled through multi-output Gaussian processes with an intrinsic model of coregionalisation (GP-ICM), assuming that sharing statistical strength improves overall performance. In practice, the benefits are inconsistent. Across the settings studied, we find that the main benefit of coregionalisation is joint uncertainty quantification rather than point prediction. Raw target correlation does not predict when coupling helps; in the separable GP-ICM settings studied here, residual correlation, the cross-target dependence left unexplained by independent per-target predictors, is the strongest predictor of joint-uncertainty gains.We introduce a lightweight diagnostic, $D_{\rm logdet}=-\frac{1}{2}\log\det R_{\rm res}$, which represents the idealised joint negative log-likelihood (NLL) gain from modelling a full rather than diagonal residual covariance and is computable from independent GPs alone. Across a controlled synthetic study, 16 multi-target benchmarks, and frozen transformer and convolutional neural network representations for keypoint regression, point prediction remains largely unchanged ($\Delta R^2\approx 0$). In contrast, $D_{\rm logdet}$ strongly predicts observed ICM NLL improvements ($\rho_s=-0.83$, $p<0.001$), outperforming heuristics such as the feature-to-sample ratio. We also propose Residual-ICM, which preserves independent marginal variances while adding residual-correlation structure to the joint covariance. Residual-ICM achieves the best average joint NLL among the compared methods, while the diagnostic indicates when covariance coupling is likely to be useful. The diagnostic is specific to global Gaussian residual dependence, the structure captured by separable coregionalisation.
  </details>

- **[FlashBoB: I/O-Efficient Exact Backward-over-Backward for Softmax Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24089)**  `arXiv:2609.24089`  
  _Anthony Givans, Michael Crawshaw, Mingrui Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transformer models built on the attention mechanism have become a central building block in modern deep learning, yet softmax attention remains a major bottleneck for long-context workloads. While FlashAttention makes the forward and first backward passes I/O-efficient, it does not support backward-over-backward (BoB), which enables exact differentiation through the backward pass for applications such as second-order optimization, test-time training, gradient-based memory, and meta-learning. Existing BoB implementations either materialize large intermediate tensors or exhaust GPU memory at long sequence lengths. We present FlashBoB, an exact, I/O-efficient algorithm for BoB in softmax attention that keeps computation within on-chip tiles and avoids all $N \times N$ intermediate tensors, where $N$ is the sequence length. The key insight is a hierarchical affine structure in the softmax double backward: two row-wise scalars determine all outputs through affine transformations. This yields a two-pass schedule with bounded on-chip static random-access memory (SRAM) usage and minimal off-chip high-bandwidth memory (HBM) traffic. FlashBoB achieves $\Theta(N^2 d^2/M)$ HBM traffic ($d$ is the head dimension and $M$ is the memory size) and, within the standard FlashAttention-style score-recomputation model, matches the inherited large-cache lower bound for exact forward attention. Empirically, it scales exact attention BoB to $N=262\text{K}$ on a single A100 80GB GPU, where prior PyTorch exact baselines fail by $N=16\text{K}$, and is up to $6.3\times$ faster than FlashBack. These results make exact second-order attention practical at long-context sequence lengths where prior implementations cannot run efficiently.
  </details>

- **[Trajectory Learnability for Offline On-Policy Distillation with Imperfect Teachers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18321)**  `arXiv:2609.18321`  
  _Yihao Ai, Beibei Lin, Weilong Yan, Huaijian Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Offline on-policy distillation gains efficiency by collecting student trajectories and teacher supervision once and reusing them throughout optimization. The same reuse makes imperfect supervision persistent. Since even strong teachers can fail, we ask \emph{what remains learnable from imperfect teacher supervision?} Teacher failure is only a coarse problem-level signal and does not imply that all supervision along the associated student trajectory is unhelpful. A natural alternative is to estimate teacher recoverability along the trajectory, but repeated continuations largely erase the efficiency advantage of offline distillation. We instead use teacher-successful problems to define a cheap reference for what the student can learn. We train on teacher-successful problems and measure how the likelihood of each observed token in trajectories from teacher-failed problems changes.
